P·L IN VISIONEM EZECHIELIS 196

Patrologia Latina · Vol. 196 ·

IN VISIONEM EZECHIELIS

PL 196, coll. 527–600  ·  Latin from the Migne printing  ·  First English translation  ·  column numbers follow the original plates, not the Garnier reprint

Richard of Saint Victor

Richardus S. Victoris

d. 1173

Canon regular of the Abbey of Saint Victor in Paris — of British, probably Scottish, origin — and its prior from 1162. After Hugh, whose pupil he was, he was the leading theologian of the Victorine school: his De Trinitate reasoned toward the persons of God from the nature of perfect love, and his Benjamin minor and Benjamin major mapped the ascent to contemplation for generations of later readers.

PROLOGUS. Animalium et rotarum litteralis explicatio.

527A

Multis divinae Scripturae multo amplius dulcescunt, quando congruum in eis aliquem secundum litteram intellectum percipere possunt. Et tunc, ut eis videtur, spiritualis intelligentiae structura firmius 527Bstatuitur quando in historici sensus solido apte fundatur. Supervacuum enim et inane quis possit solidum aliquid fundare, vel firmiter statuere? Cum enim mystici sensus ex earum congrua rerum similitudine eruantur atque formentur, quae in littera proponuntur, quomodo nos ad spiritualem intelligentiam instruat in his duntaxat locis, ubi ipsa sibi expugnat, vel nihil nisi frivolum resonat? Scandalizantur itaque saepe potius quam aedificentur, qui ejusmodi sunt, cum hujusmodi Scripturarum loca incurrunt. Econtra autem antiqui Patres libenter accipiebant cum in hujusmodi Scripturarum loca inciderent, quae juxta litteram stare non poterant. Ex his namque quosdam, quae Scripturam sanctam recipiebant, sensus tamen allegoricos subsannabant, 527Cex his, inquam, absurdis litterae locis compellebant ad spiritalem intelligentiam confugere, cum negare non auderent Spiritum sanctum in quantalibet fatuitate litterae nihil inaniter scripsisse. Hinc contigisse arbitror, ut litterae expositionem in obscurioribus quibusdam locis antiqui Patres tacite praeterirent, vel paulo negligentius tractarent, qui si plenius insisterent multo perfectius procul dubio, quam aliquis ex modernis id potuissent. Sed nec illud tacite praetereo, quod quidam quasi ob reverentiam Patrum nolunt ab illis omissa attentare, ne videantur aliquid ultra majores praesumere. Sed inertiae suae ejusmodi velamen habentes, otio torpent et aliorum industriam in veritatis investigatione et inventione derident, subsannant, exsufflant. Sed qui habitat in coelis 527Dirridebit eos, et Dominus subsannabit eos. Nos autem a Patribus pertractata cum omni aviditate suscipiamus, et ab ipsis omissa cum omni alacritate perquiramus, et sagaciter inventa cum omni liberalitate in commune proferamus, ut impleatur quod scriptum est: Pertransibunt plurimi, et multiplex erit scientia (Dan. XII). Ecce mirabilem illam visionem de coelestibus animalibus, quam propheta Ezechiel vidit, B. Gregorius, secundum mysticam intelligentiam, exponit. Sed quomodo ad litteram stare valeat, non dicit. De secunda visione dicit, quod juxta litteram stare non possit. Et hoc quidem verum, sed secundum eam acceptionem, quam ipse ibi assignavit; nam si eamdem de qua hoc dicit litteram velimus 528Asecundum aliam acceptionem discutere, fortassis, etiam juxta historicum sensum, ex ipsa poterimus congruum intellectum eruere. Certe, idem in eadem Ezechielis expositione dicit, plerumque in sacro eloquio ideo aliquid obscure dicitur, ut, dispensante mirabiliter Deo, multipliciter exponatur. Multiplex 528Bitaque sacrae Scripturae expositio nunquam respuatur, in quantum rationi consentire, vel utilitati deservire videtur. In quantum ergo quisque in hoc gratiam accepit, nemo dubitet cum omni diligentia quaerere ea quae praecedentium Patrum sagacitas, vel ex industria praeteriit, vel magis necessariorum occupatione implicata explicare non potuit. Dico itaque libenter quod mihi videtur de illa prophetica visione: nam fortassis haec nostra qualiscunque expositio (et si non possit alienae solertiae satisfacere) poterit forte aliorum in hoc ipsum diligentiam, et quaerendi studium nonnihil adjuvare.

Ecce de animalibus dicitur (Ezech. I): Similitudo hominis erat in eis. Haec profecto hominis similitudo, quantum ad litteram pertinet, ex parte erat, 528Cet ex parte non erat, sicut subjuncta declarant. Ex parte erat, quia faciem et manus hominis habebant; et item ex parte non erat, quia pennas et pedes vitulinos, et plures facies habebant, unde et de eis additur: Quatuor facies uni, et quatuor pennae uni. Si coelestia animalia quatuor facies, et per quatuor partes habent, ergo contra terrestrium naturam sub eodem momento in omnem partem videre valent. Et pedes eorum pedes recti. Rectos pedes oportebat habere, ut possent in omnem partem, sicut ex sequentibus colligitur, sine ulla corporis conversione incedere: Et planta pedis eorum quasi planta pedis vituli. Vitulinam plantam dicta animalia habere describuntur, ut non ex industria, velut hominum, sed naturaliter munita intelligantur. Ut 528Dautem possent pari modo in omnem partem incedere, oportebat formam pedum vitulinam similitudinem nonnulla ex parte excedere. In hoc autem quod dicitur, quia scintillae erant in eis, vel potius ex eis quasi aeris candentis, ostenditur quod facies igneas habebant quae ardentes scintillas spargebant. Et facies et pennas per quatuor partes habebant. Certe si, ut superius dictum est, quatuor facies habebant; si eas, ut hic dicitur, per quatuor partes habebant, oportet ut in singulis partibus (quantum ad litteram spectat) nonnisi solam haberent. Quatuor autem pennas habebant, ut aeque quocunque versi quaquaversum volare potuissent.

Notandum quod quatuor facies, quatuor pennas, 529Aquatuor manus, et ejusdem numeri pedes habuere; numerum tamen pedum non videtur exprimere, eo quod nec rarum sit, nec insolitum quatuor pedes habere. Junctaeque erant pennae eorum alterius ad alterum. Incedentium itaque animalium tanta distantia erat, quanta extensarum alarum copula pati poterat. Non revertebantur cum incederent, sed unumquodque ante faciem suam gradiebatur. Cum in posterioribus dicturus sit: Ibant et revertebantur, quomodo hic dicitur quod incedentes non revertebantur? Sed vide ne forte revertebantur sub alio sensu ponatur hic, et sub alio ibi, si non facimus prophetam mentiri. Idem enim videtur hoc sonare revertebantur quod convertebantur. In sequentibus autem idem videtur sonare quod redire. 529BRevertentes ergo non revertebantur, quia reverti, hoc est redire, volentes non revertebantur, hoc est non retro vertebantur, quia non convertebantur. Hinc est quod longe post de rotis loquens, dicit: Cum ambularent in quatuor partes gradiebantur, et non revertebantur ambulantes. Certe, si per quatuor partes ibant, fuit eis necesse ire et redire. Quomodo ergo ambulantes non revertebantur, nisi quo modo per subjuncta verba determinatur? Statim namque adjungitur: Sed ad locum ad quem ire declinabat, quae prima erat, sequebantur et caeterae, nec convertebantur. Ecce pro eo quod prius posuit revertebantur, eamdem sententiam repetens ponit convertebantur, et determinavit quod prius obscure posuit. 529CSed quid inter haec dicimus, nisi quod ad sententiam beati Gregorii recurrimus, quod, dispensante Deo, idcirco obscure ponitur, ut multipliciter exponatur? Et absque dubio ex hac obscuritate Scripturae agitur, ut quamvis fortassis una sit expositio secundum litteram, multiplex esse possit secundum mysticam intelligentiam: dicatur itaque de animalibus, non revertebantur cum incederent. Cur autem sine ulla corporis conversione ire et redire potuissent, ostenditur ex eo quod subjungitur: Sed unumquodque ante faciem suam gradiebatur; ac si aperte diceret: Idcirco non erat eis necesse corpus convertere cum redire vellent, quia in eam etiam partem faciem habebant Nos cum ad posteriora redire volumus corporis conversione necesse habemus, qui 529Dsolam unam faciem habemus. Sed illa revertebantur, nec retro vertebantur, sed ante faciem suam gradiebantur, illam videlicet quam ad eam partem habebant ad quam ire volebant. Sequitur:

Similitudo vultus eorum facies hominis, et facies leonis a dextris ipsorum quatuor. Facies autem bovis a sinistris ipsorum quatuor. Et facies aquilae desuper ipsorum quatuor. Ecce in sua descriptione quomodo quibusdam gradibus incedit. Primo dixit quod haberent quatuor facies, postea dixit quod eas haberent pro quatuor partes. Dicit modo novissime qualem, inquam, partem habuere. Si dicta animalia pro more nostro dextram et sinistram et posteriora haberent, facile esset prophetae singulas partes exprimere, et secundum eas sigillatim facies 530Adistinguere. Sed hoc idcirco non potuit, quia omnis in eis pars anterior fuit. Fecit ergo quomodo potuit, unam faciem primo ponit, et secundum illam caeteras distinguit. Dicit ergo: Similitudo vultus eorum facies hominis. Quis nesciat quod vultus in nobis praecipua et principalior pars esse soleat? Dicitur itaque quia vultus, hoc est prima et principalior facies, in eis erat facies, hominis. A dextris autem illius humanae faciei ipsorum quatuor erat facies leonis. A sinistris autem ejusdem erat facies bovis.

Notandum quod de aquilina facie non dicit in qua parte et ipsa fuerit, quia ex praecedentibus hoc ipsum manifestum fuit. Illud solum dixit, de quo, si dictum non esset, dubitari potuit, quod ea utique 530Bfacies caeteris eminentior fuit. Et facies, inquit, aquilae ipsorum quatuor erat desuper. Hoc est caeteris superior. Et aves quidem solent habere, ut omnes novimus, longiora colla caeteris animalibus. Et facies eorum, et pennae eorum extentae desuper. Quod dicit, et facies eorum, defectiva oratio est, quasi dicat: et facies eorum in hunc modum erant, et pennae eorum extentae desuper illas facies. Nam ad hoc praemittit et facies eorum, ut competentius inferat quod de pennis dicere volebat: Duae pennae singulorum jungebant. Hoc idem aliis verbis superius dixerat, sed repetit, ut addat: Et duae tegebant corpora eorum. Non sic intelligendum est, quasi eisdem semper duabus alis corpora tegerent, 530Cet duabus solummodo aliis volarent, cum utrumque utrisque sed vicissim facerent. Cum coram humana facie volare decrevissent, duabus illis alis quae a dextris et a sinistris ejus erant id faciebant, et eisdem ipsis in contrariam partem, hoc est coram aquilina facie, volabant, duabus vero alis corpora tegebant. Cum vero coram leonina, vel bovina facie volare voluissent, econtrario faciebant: Et unumquodque coram facie sua ambulabat. Videtur superius positam sententiam repetere, sed credo aliud quiddam hic ostendere. Superius docuit quia corpus idcirco non convertebant, quia in ea parte ad quam ire volebant, faciem quam sequerentur habebant, et coram ea ambulabant. Hic mihi videtur hoc dicere velle, quoniam per tot partes irent in quot 530Dpartes facies haberent. Idem est enim ac si diceret: Unumquodque eorum coram qualibet facie sua ambulabat. Huic sententiae astipulatur, quod sequitur:

Ubi erat impetus spiritus, illuc gradiebantur, nec revertebantur cum ambularent. Vere coram unaquaque facie sua gradiebantur, quia ubicunque erat impetus spiritus, sive ad eundum, sive ad redeundum, sive in quamlibet partem divertendum, ita illuc gradiebantur, ut non converterentur, et inter eundum et redeundum nulla corporis conversione uterentur. Et similitudo animalium aspectus eorum quasi carbonum ignis ardentium, et quasi aspectus lampadarum. Ecce quod superius jam diximus, quoniam igneas facies habebant, qui calorem in 531Amodum carbonum ardentium, et lumen in modum lampadarum fundebant. Sequitur:

Haec erat visio discurrens in medio animalium. Splendor ignis, et de igne fulgur egrediens. Ista satis patent. Et animalia ibant, et revertebantur in similitudinem fulguris coruscantis. Ecce quod superius jam diximus, quia animalia ibant et redibant. Sed quomodo putatis? In similitudinem fulguris coruscantis. Celeritas stupenda et solis coelestibus animalibus attribuenda. Cumque aspicerem animalia, apparuit rota una super terram juxta animalia. Non una sola tantum juxta omnia, sed singulae juxta singula, sicut declarant sequentia. Apparuit itaque rota una juxta singula animalia. Sed qualis? Habens, inquit, quatuor facies. Quomodo autem quatuor 531Bfacies habuerit melius ex sequentibus patebit: Et aspectus rotarum, et opus earum, quasi visio maris. Maris similitudinem dicuntur habere pro incredibili magnitudine. Et una similitudo ipsarum quatuor, et aspectus earum, et opera quasi si sit rota in medio rotae. Cogitemus quasi duae rotae essent sibi invicem insertae, et in unam operis facturam per transversum conjunctae, et tunc intelligere poterimus quomodo dicatur unaquaeque quatuor facies habere. Per quatuor partes earum euntes ibant, et non revertebantur cum ambularent. Hoc eodem modo de rotis dicimus quomodo superius de animalibus determinavimus. Statura quoque erat in rotis, et altitudo, et horribilis aspectus. Quantum mihi videtur, quoties stabant uniformiter id agebant, nec 531Cpassim in omnem se partem, ut in rotis nostris videmus, ad standum erigebant; sed ubi superius designata illa, quasi duarum rotarum factura, hinc inde per transversum inserebatur, et invicem copulabatur. Cum stare vellent unam illarum partium semper in terra figebant, alteram in altum erigebant. Facile est ergo videre quam promptum eis esset post stationem in omnem se partem pari modo sine ulla corporis conversione volvere, quae, ut dictum est, quatuor facies habuere. Possumus autem quod dicitur horribilis, et ad staturam, et ad altitudinem referre, ut intelligatur tantae magnitudinis esse, ut esset horror aspicere, unde et superius dictum est de rotis, quod aspectus earum esset quasi 531Dvisio maris. Et totum corpus plenum oculis in circuitu ipsarum quatuor. Cumque ambularent animalia, ambulabant pariter et rotae juxta ea; et cum elevarentur animalia de terra, elevabantur simul et rotae. Patent haec omnia, nec egent expositione nostra. Quocunque ibat spiritus, illuc eunte spiritu, et rotae pariter elevabantur sequentes eum. Spiritus enim vitae erat in rotis. Videtur alius esse spiritus quem rotae sequebantur, et alius qui eis inesse perhibetur. Et similitudo super caput animalium firmamenti quasi aspectus crystalli horribilis et extenti super capita eorum desuper. Quod dicitur quasi crystalli horribilis, dictum puto pro enormitate magnitudinis: Sub firmamento autem pennae eorum recte alterius ad alterum. Unumquodque duabus alis 532Avelabat corpus suum, et alterum similiter velabatur. Quando unum aliquod illorum duabus illis alis quae a dextris et a sinistris humanae faciei erant corpus suum velabat, similiter quodlibet caeterorum faciebat. Quando autem duabus aliis alis aliquod velabatur, eadem corporis velandi forma sub eodem tempore ab aliis servabatur. Quomodo ergo quodlibet unum corpus suum velabat, similiter quodlibet alterum velabatur. Sonum quasi sonum sublimis Dei dicit, eo quod tantae magnitudinis esset, ut jure divinus dici debuisset, sicut carmen regium, vel regis digne dici debeat, quod regiam audientiam deceat.

Rursus de his rotis, vel animalibus alio loco legimus: Rota una juxta cherub unum, et rota alia juxta cherub unum. Species autem erat rotarum quasi 532Bvisio lapidis chrysolithi, et aspectus earum similitudo una quatuor, quasi si sit rota in medio rotae. Cumque ambularent in quatuor partes gradiebantur. Non revertebantur ambulantes, sed ad locum ad quem ire declinabat quae prima erat sequebantur et caeterae nec convertebantur (Ezech. X). Haec omnia jam patent ex his quae superius jam dicta sunt. Sequitur:

Et omne corpus earum et colla, et manus et pennae, et circuli plena erant oculis in circuitu quatuor rotarum, et omne corpus earum. Quod dicit earum, intelligendum est factam relationem non ad rotas tantum, sed etiam ad animalia: nam de utrisque fuerat praemissum. Cum enim dicat earum, adnectit tamen quaedam quae conveniunt tam animalibus quam rotis, quaedam vero quae conveniunt rotis solis, quaedam 532Cautem quae congruunt solis animalibus, nisi forte qui putet rotas habere colla, pedes et manus. Usitatius autem loqueretur, si eorum, non earum posuisset. Sed quia novissimo loco de rotis locutus fuit, secundum genus quod proximo loco posuit relationem reddidit. Tale est illud in psalmo: Effuderunt sanguinem innocentem, sanguinem filiorum suorum, et filiarum suarum quas sacrificaverunt sculptilibus Chanaan (Psal. CV). Quas, non quos dixit, quamvis non ad solas filias relatio facta sit, nisi forte quis velit dicere solas filias sculptilibus sacrificatas fuisse. Sed quis hoc dicat? Quod hic dicitur, omne corpus earum, tam de rotis quam de animalibus intelligimus. Quod additur, et colla et 532Dmanus et pennae, de solis animalibus. Quod autem postea dicitur, et circuli, de solis rotis oportet intelligi, et quod novissime apponitur, in circuitu quatuor rotarum, et ad circulos tantummodo debet referri. Dicit ergo quia plena erant oculis, omne corpus earum, et colla et manus et pennae, et circuli in circuitu quatuor rotarum. Addit adhuc quaedam de rotis, quaedam autem quae conveniant animalibus solis: Et rotas istas vocavit volubiles, audiente me. Ecce de rotis. Et addit quod conveniat animalibus solis: Quatuor autem facies habebat unum. Si de solis rotis locutus fuit, quid est, vel unde quod unum dicit? Puto vel ex hoc manifestum fore eum non de solis rotis locutum fuisse. Sequitur:

533AFacies una, facies cherub et facies secunda, facies hominis, et in tertio facies leonis, et in quarto facies aquilae. Sed ubi facies cherub legitur, in quibusdam codicibus facies vituli invenitur. Sed quamvis sit littera diversa, utrobique tamen est sententia una. Ex superioribus agnovimus quod dictorum animalium corpus in vitulum desinebat; et idcirco, si recte perpendimus, vituli facies corpori magis congruebat, in tantum ut quodam proprietatis jure haec facies descripti animalis specialiter dici valeat, vel debeat. Sed forte quis ad hoc respondeat et dicat: Sicut ad naturam vituli magis accedebat in eo quod plantas vitulinas habebat, sic ex eo quod manus, vel pennas gerebat, in uno hominis, ex alio avis proprietatem praeferebat. Sed scimus quia proprium 533Best hominis manus habere. Non ergo potuit vitulinas manus, quemadmodum vitulinas plantas habere. Hoc idem est de pennis intelligere. Pedes autem habere commune est tam homini quam bovi, tam aquilae quam leoni. Quid itaque est quod animal, quod quatuor facies habuit, vitulinos pedes accepit, nisi quod vitulina proprietas prae caeteris amplius aliquid sui juris in ejus corpore vindicavit, quae in illis utique membris, quae sunt aeque et aliis communia, propriae qualitatis similitudinem impressit? Propter hanc ergo proprietatis praerogativam, recte faciem vituli intelligimus, in eo quod faciem 534Acherub legimus. Non est autem diversum quod faciem bovis superius, hic faciem vituli invenimus. Est enim omnis vitulus bos, sed non convertitur cum bovis nomine, quo animalis species designatur.

Notandum autem quod idem positionis ordo hoc loco invenitur, qui superius exprimitur. Sed ibi facies hominis primo numeratur, eo quod sit principalior, secundum dignitatem. Hic autem facies vituli, eo quod sit principalior secundum proprietatem. Quoniam autem caeteris dignior non est facies vitulina, noluit dicere facies cherub, seu facies vituli, facies prima, sed facies una: Facies, inquit, una facies cherub, et facies secunda facies hominis. Erat ergo facies hominis facies secunda, hoc est 534Bsecundo loco ab ea quae primo est posita, et in tertio loco facies leonis, et in quarto facies aquilae.

PROLOGUE. A literal explanation of the living creatures and of the wheels.

527A

To many, the Holy Scriptures grow far sweeter when they are able to perceive in them some fitting understanding according to the letter. And then, as it seems to them, the structure of spiritual understanding is more firmly 527Bestablished when it is aptly founded upon the solid ground of the historical sense. For upon the superfluous and the empty, who could found anything solid, or firmly establish it? For since the mystical senses are drawn out and formed from the fitting likeness of those things which are set forth in the letter, how shall it instruct us unto spiritual understanding in just those places where it assails itself, or sounds nothing but what is frivolous? And so men of this sort are often scandalized rather than edified, when they run upon such places of the Scriptures. But the ancient Fathers, on the contrary, gladly received it when they fell upon such places of the Scriptures as could not stand according to the letter. For from these they compelled certain men — men who received Holy Scripture, yet mocked the allegorical senses — 527Cfrom these absurd places of the letter, I say, they compelled them to take refuge in spiritual understanding, since they did not dare to deny that the Holy Spirit had written nothing emptily in however great a foolishness of the letter. Hence I judge it to have come about that the ancient Fathers silently passed over the exposition of the letter in certain more obscure places, or handled it somewhat more negligently — men who, had they pressed on more fully, could without doubt have done it far more perfectly than any of the moderns. But neither do I silently pass this by, that certain men, as though out of reverence for the Fathers, are unwilling to attempt what those men left undone, lest they seem to presume something beyond their elders. But having such a veil for their own sloth, they grow numb in idleness, and deride, mock, and blow away the industry of others in the investigation and discovery of truth. But he who dwells in the heavens 527Dshall laugh at them, and the Lord shall mock them. But let us receive with all eagerness what has been treated by the Fathers, and let us search out with all alacrity what was left undone by them, and let us bring forth for the common good, with all liberality, what has been shrewdly discovered, so that what is written may be fulfilled: Very many shall pass over, and knowledge shall be manifold (Dan. XII). Behold, that wonderful vision concerning the heavenly living creatures, which the prophet Ezekiel saw, blessed Gregory expounds according to the mystical understanding. But how it may be able to stand according to the letter, he does not say. Of the second vision he says that it cannot stand according to the letter. And this indeed is true, but according to that acceptation which he himself there assigned to it; for if we should wish to examine that same letter of which he says this 528Aaccording to another acceptation, perhaps, even according to the historical sense, we shall be able to draw out of it a fitting understanding. Assuredly, the same man says, in the same exposition of Ezekiel, that for the most part in sacred speech something is said obscurely for this reason: that, God wondrously dispensing it, it may be expounded in manifold ways. Let the manifold 528Bexposition of Holy Scripture, therefore, never be rejected, in so far as it seems to agree with reason, or to serve usefulness. In so far, then, as each has received grace in this matter, let no one hesitate to seek with all diligence those things which the sagacity of the preceding Fathers either passed over of set purpose, or, entangled in the occupation of more necessary matters, could not unfold. And so I gladly say what seems to me concerning that prophetic vision; for perhaps this exposition of ours, such as it is (even if it cannot satisfy another man's skill), may perhaps in some measure help the diligence of others in this very thing, and their zeal for inquiry.

Behold, it is said of the living creatures (Ezech. I): The likeness of a man was in them. This likeness of a man, so far as pertains to the letter, in part was, 528Cand in part was not, as the words subjoined declare. In part it was, because they had the face and the hands of a man; and again in part it was not, because they had wings and calves' feet, and several faces, whence also it is added concerning them: Four faces to one, and four wings to one. If the heavenly living creatures have four faces, and have them on four sides, then, contrary to the nature of earthly creatures, they are able at one and the same moment to see toward every side. And their feet were straight feet. It behooved them to have straight feet, so that they might be able, as is gathered from what follows, to walk toward every side without any turning of the body: And the sole of their foot was as the sole of a calf's foot. The said living creatures are described as having the sole of a calf, so that they may be understood to be furnished not by contrivance, as men's are, but by nature. But that 528Dthey might be able in like manner to walk toward every side, it behooved the form of their feet to exceed in some part the likeness of a calf's. But in this, that it is said that sparks were in them, or rather from them, as of glowing bronze, it is shown that they had fiery faces which scattered burning sparks. And they had faces and wings on four sides. Assuredly, if, as was said above, they had four faces; if they had them, as is here said, on four sides, then it must be that on each single side (so far as concerns the letter) they had none but one alone. And they had four wings, so that, turned in whatever direction alike, they might be able to fly in every direction.

It is to be noted that they had four faces, four wings, 529Afour hands, and feet of the same number; yet he does not seem to express the number of the feet, for the reason that it is neither rare nor unusual to have four feet. And their wings were joined one to another. And so the distance between the living creatures as they walked was as great as the joining of their outstretched wings could allow. They did not turn back when they walked, but each one went before his own face. Since in what follows he is going to say, They went and returned, how is it here said that as they walked they did not turn back? But see whether perhaps "they turned back" is put here in one sense and there in another, if we are not to make the prophet lie. For "they turned back" seems to sound the same as "they turned about." But in what follows it seems to sound the same as "to go back." 529BTurning back, therefore, they did not turn back, because, wishing to turn back — that is, to go back — they did not turn back, that is, they were not turned to the rear, because they were not turned about. Hence it is that, speaking long afterward of the wheels, he says: When they went, they walked toward the four sides, and they did not turn back as they walked. Assuredly, if they went toward four sides, it was needful for them to go and to go back. How then, as they walked, did they not turn back, save in the manner determined by the words subjoined? For at once it is added: But to the place whither the first, which was going, inclined to go, the rest also followed, and they were not turned about. Behold: in place of what he first put, "they turned back," repeating the same sentiment he puts "they were turned about," and he determined what he had first put obscurely. 529CBut what shall we say amid these things, save that we have recourse to the sentiment of blessed Gregory, that, God dispensing it, a thing is therefore put obscurely so that it may be expounded in manifold ways? And without doubt it is brought about by this obscurity of Scripture that, although perhaps there be one exposition according to the letter, it may be manifold according to the mystical understanding. Let it be said, therefore, of the living creatures, that they did not turn back when they walked. But why they could go and go back without any turning of the body is shown from what is subjoined: But each one went before his own face; as if he said openly: It was therefore not needful for them to turn the body when they wished to go back, because they had a face toward that side also We, when we wish to go back to what is behind us, must needs use a turning of the body, we who 529Dhave only one single face. But those creatures turned back, and yet were not turned to the rear, but went before their own face — that one, namely, which they had toward the side to which they wished to go. There follows:

The likeness of their countenance was the face of a man, and the face of a lion on the right of the four of them. And the face of an ox on the left of the four of them. And the face of an eagle above the four of them. Behold how in his description he proceeds by certain degrees. First he said that they had four faces; afterward he said that they had them for four sides. He now says lastly what sort of side, I say, they had. If the said living creatures had, after our fashion, a right side and a left and a hinder part, it would be easy for the prophet to express the several sides, and according to them to distinguish the faces 530Aone by one. But this he could not do, because in them every side was a front side. He did, therefore, as he could: he first puts one face, and according to it distinguishes the rest. He says, then: The likeness of their countenance was the face of a man. Who does not know that in us the countenance is wont to be the chief and more principal part? And so it is said that the countenance — that is, the first and more principal face — in them was the face of a man. And on the right of that human face of the four of them was the face of a lion. And on the left of the same was the face of an ox.

It is to be noted that of the eagle's face he does not say on which side it too was, because from what precedes this very thing was manifest. He said only that of which, had it not been said, there could have been doubt: namely, that that 530Bface was assuredly higher than the rest. And the face, he says, of an eagle of the four of them was above. That is, higher than the rest. And birds indeed are wont, as we all know, to have longer necks than other animals. And their faces and their wings were stretched out above. His saying, "and their faces," is a defective sentence, as though he said: and their faces were in this manner, and their wings stretched out above those faces. For he sets "and their faces" before it, so that he may the more fittingly bring in what he wished to say of the wings: The two wings of each joined. This same thing he had said above in other words, but he repeats it in order to add: And two covered their bodies. It is not so to be understood as if they always covered their bodies with the same two wings, 530Cand flew with two others only, since they did both with both, but by turns. When they had determined to fly before the human face, they did it with those two wings which were on its right and on its left; and with those same ones they flew in the contrary direction, that is, before the eagle's face, but with two wings they covered their bodies. But when they wished to fly before the lion's or the ox's face, they did the contrary: And each one walked before its own face. He seems to repeat the sentiment set down above, but I believe he shows something else here. Above he taught that they did not turn the body for this reason, that on the side to which they wished to go they had a face which they might follow, and they walked before it. Here he seems to me to wish to say this, that they went toward as many sides as the sides 530Don which they had faces. For it is the same as if he said: Each one of them walked before any face of its own whatever. What follows agrees with this sentiment:

Where the impulse of the spirit was, thither they went, nor did they turn back as they walked. Truly they went before each one of their faces, because wherever the impulse of the spirit was — whether for going, or for going back, or for turning aside toward any side whatever — they so went thither that they were not turned about, and between going and going back they used no turning of the body. And the likeness of the living creatures, their appearance was as of burning coals of fire, and as the appearance of lamps. Behold what we have already said above, that they had fiery faces, which poured forth heat after the 531Amanner of burning coals, and light after the manner of lamps. There follows:

This was the vision running about in the midst of the living creatures. A brightness of fire, and out of the fire lightning going forth. These things are plain enough. And the living creatures went and returned after the likeness of flashing lightning. Behold what we have already said above, that the living creatures went and went back. But in what manner, do you suppose? After the likeness of flashing lightning. A swiftness astonishing, and to be attributed to heavenly living creatures alone. And as I beheld the living creatures, there appeared one wheel upon the earth beside the living creatures. Not one only beside them all, but single wheels beside single creatures, as what follows declares. And so one wheel appeared beside each single living creature. But of what sort? Having, he says, four faces. But how it had four 531Bfaces will better appear from what follows: And the appearance of the wheels, and their work, was as the vision of the sea. They are said to have the likeness of the sea because of their incredible magnitude. And the four of them had one likeness, and their appearance and their works were as if a wheel were in the midst of a wheel. Let us imagine that there were, as it were, two wheels inserted one into the other, and joined crosswise into a single fabric of one work; and then we shall be able to understand how each one is said to have four faces. Going toward their four sides, they went, and they did not turn back as they walked. We say this of the wheels in the same manner in which we determined it above of the living creatures. There was also stature in the wheels, and height, and a terrible appearance. So far as it seems to me, as often as they stood they did it uniformly, nor 531Cdid they raise themselves up to stand indiscriminately toward every side, as we see in our own wheels; but where that fabric designated above, as of two wheels, was inserted crosswise on this side and on that, and coupled one to the other — when they wished to stand, they always fixed one of those parts in the earth and raised the other on high. It is easy, therefore, to see how ready it was for them, after standing still, to roll themselves in like manner toward every side, without any turning of the body — they that, as has been said, had four faces. And we may refer the word "terrible" both to the stature and to the height, so that it may be understood to be of such magnitude that it was a horror to look upon; whence also it was said above of the wheels that their appearance was as 531Dthe vision of the sea. And the whole body was full of eyes round about the four of them. And when the living creatures walked, the wheels also walked alongside them; and when the living creatures were lifted up from the earth, the wheels also were lifted up together with them. All these things are plain, nor do they need any exposition of ours. Whithersoever the spirit went, the spirit going thither, the wheels also were lifted up together, following it. For the spirit of life was in the wheels. The spirit which the wheels followed seems to be one, and the spirit which is said to be in them another. And the likeness of a firmament above the head of the living creatures, as the appearance of terrible crystal, and stretched out above their heads from above. What is said, "as of terrible crystal," I think was said for the enormity of its magnitude: And under the firmament their wings were straight, the one toward the other. Each one covered 532Ahis body with two wings, and the other was likewise covered. When any one of them covered its own body with those two wings which were on the right and on the left of the human face, any one of the rest did likewise. And when any one of them was covered by the other two wings, the same manner of covering the body was observed at the same time by the others. In whatever manner, therefore, any one covered its own body, in like manner any other one was covered. He calls the sound as it were the sound of the most high God, for the reason that it was of such magnitude that it ought by right to have been called divine — just as a song may worthily be called royal, or the king's, which befits a royal audience.

Again, concerning these wheels, or the living creatures, we read in another place: One wheel beside one cherub, and another wheel beside one cherub. And the appearance of the wheels was as 532Bthe vision of the chrysolite stone, and the aspect of them was one likeness of the four, as if a wheel were in the midst of a wheel. And when they walked, they went toward the four sides. They did not turn back as they walked, but to the place whither the first, which was going, inclined to go, the rest also followed, and they were not turned about (Ezech. X). All these things are already plain from what has been said above. There follows:

And their whole body and their necks, and their hands and their wings, and the circles, were full of eyes round about the four wheels, and their whole body. As for his saying earum, it is to be understood that the reference is made not to the wheels only, but also to the living creatures; for the statement had been set forth concerning both. For although he says earum, he nevertheless adds certain things which belong to the living creatures as much as to the wheels, certain things indeed which belong to the wheels alone, and certain things 532Cwhich are fitting to the living creatures alone — unless perhaps someone should think that the wheels have necks, feet, and hands. But he would speak more customarily if he had put eorum, not earum. But because he had spoken of the wheels in the last place, he made the reference according to the gender which he had put in the nearest place. Such is that in the psalm: They shed innocent blood, the blood of their sons and of their daughters, whom they sacrificed to the graven things of Chanaan (Psal. CV). He said quas, not quos, although the reference was not made to the daughters alone — unless perhaps someone should wish to say that the daughters alone were sacrificed to the graven things. But who would say this? What is here said, "their whole body," we understand of the wheels as much as of the living creatures. What is added, "and the necks and 532Dthe hands and the wings," of the living creatures alone. But what is said afterward, "and the circles," must be understood of the wheels alone; and what is set down last, "round about the four wheels," ought to be referred to the circles only. He says, therefore, that they were full of eyes: their whole body, and the necks and the hands and the wings, and the circles round about the four wheels. He adds still certain things concerning the wheels, and certain things which belong to the living creatures alone: And these wheels he called whirling, in my hearing. Behold, concerning the wheels. And he adds what belongs to the living creatures alone: And one had four faces. If he was speaking of the wheels alone, what is it, or whence is it, that he says unum? I think that from this very thing it will be manifest that he was not speaking of the wheels alone. There follows:

533AOne face was the face of a cherub, and the second face the face of a man, and in the third the face of a lion, and in the fourth the face of an eagle. But where "the face of a cherub" is read, in certain codices "the face of a calf" is found. But although the letter is different, yet in both places the sense is one. From the foregoing we have recognized that the body of the said living creatures ended in a calf; and therefore, if we weigh it rightly, the face of a calf agreed better with the body — so much so that by a certain right of propriety this face may, or ought to, be called specially that of the creature described. But perhaps someone may answer to this and say: Just as it approached more nearly to the nature of a calf in that it had calves' soles, so from the fact that it bore hands, or wings, it displayed in the one the property of a man, from the other that of a bird. But we know that it is proper 533Bto man to have hands. It could not, therefore, have calves' hands in the way it had calves' soles. The same is to be understood of the wings. But to have feet is common to man as much as to the ox, to the eagle as much as to the lion. What then does it mean that the living creature which had four faces received calves' feet, save that the calf-property claimed for itself in its body something more than the rest of its own right, seeing that it impressed the likeness of its own quality upon those very members which are equally common to others also? Because of this prerogative of propriety, therefore, we rightly understand the face of a calf where we read the face 534Aof a cherub. And it is not a different thing that above we find the face of an ox, and here the face of a calf. For every calf is an ox, but it is not convertible with the name of ox, by which the species of the animal is designated.

But it is to be noted that the same order of position is found in this place which is expressed above. But there the face of a man is numbered first, for the reason that it is more principal according to dignity. Here, however, the face of a calf, for the reason that it is more principal according to propriety. But because the calf's face is not more worthy than the rest, he would not say "the face of a cherub," or "the face of a calf," is "the first face," but "one face": One face, he says, was the face of a cherub, and the second face the face of a man. The face of a man, therefore, was the second face, that is, 534Bin the second place from that which was set first; and in the third place the face of a lion, and in the fourth the face of an eagle.

CAPUT PRIMUM. Compendiosa comprehensio eorum aedificiorum quae ad unam medietatem pertinent.

Locuturus de Ezechielis aedificio, volo prius universa summatim perstringere, ut postmodum liceat in singularum partium descriptione quanto liberius, tanto latius vacare. Habebat itaque hoc aedificium triplicem murum. Primum sexcentorum cubitorum, secundum quingentorum cubitorum, tertium vero in medio horum, et omnium minimum, in hunc modum. 533

533CIntra Intimum autem murum erat atrium interius; inter intimum et medium, erat atrium exterius. Inter medium vero et extimum erat vulgi locus. De atrio interiori legitur: Et mensus est atrium, longitudinem centum cubitorum, et latitudinem centum cubitorum, per quadrum (Ezech. XL). De secundo muro scribitur: Per quatuor ventos mensus est illud murum ejus undique per circuitum, longitudinem quingentorum cubitorum, et latitudinem quingentorum cubitorum, dividentem inter sanctuarium, et vulgi locum (Ezech. XLII). De hoc sanctuario alio rursum loco scribitur: Et erit ex omni parte sanctificatum quingentos per quingentos, quadrifariam per circuitum, 534Cet quinquaginta cubiti in suburbana ejus per gyrum (Ezech. XLV). Eumdem sane locum per suburbana, et vulgi locum designat congrue satis, utpote metaphoram servans, qui in descriptionis suae initio visum aedificium civitati comparaverat. Constat itaque inter medium murum et extimum quinquaginta cubitos fuisse, si per suburbana et vulgi locum oportet unum eumdemque locum intelligere. De hoc extimo muro in ipso descriptionis exordio dicitur: Et ecce murus forinsecus in circuitu domus undique (Ezech. XL).

Notandum sane quod hoc sanctificatum quod quingentos cubitos ex omni parte habere describitur, nunc aedificium, nunc sanctuarium, nunc domus 535Adicitur, nunc civitati assimilatur. Sane si consulas tam rationem quam Scripturam, inventes unumquemque trium horum murorum tres portas habuisse. Unam ab oriente, aliam ab aquilone, tertiam a meridie. De tribus siquidem portis atrii interioris, sive de tribus aliis atrii exterioris Scriptura multo sermone explicat. De una autem reliquarum trium breviter mentionem facit. Caeteras vero subintelligendas relinquit. Frustra namque muri interiores ab aquilone et meridie portas haberent, nisi e regione per murum extrinsecum ad illas aditus paterent. Habebant autem sex portae interiores unaquaeque porticum suam, quae omnes fabricatae erant juxta eamdem similitudinem, et ad mensuram unam, nisi quod portae atrii interioris 535Bhabebant porticus suas extrinsecus, et porticus atrii exterioris jacebant intrinsecus, et quod in gradibus septem ascendebatur in atrium exterius, et in gradibus octo in atrium interius. Ingredientibus itaque in atrium exterius prius occurrebant portae quam porticus suae. E converso autem ad ingressum atrii interioris, prius se offerebant porticus quam portae, ita ut omnes sex porticus pariter jacerent in atrio exteriore. Has autem porticus Scriptura nunc porticus, nunc vestibula nominat; et unam eamdemque porticum, nunc vestibulum, nunc vestibula vocat. In via sane orientali inter geminas porticus atrii exterioris et atrii interioris jacebat pavimentum stratum lapide, habens centum cubitos 535Clongitudinis, et centum cubitos latitudiuis; et triginta gazophylacia in gemino latere, quindecim a meridie, et quindecim ab aquilone. Erant autem et inter alias porticus duo alia pavimenta, unum ab aquilone, et aliud in via meridiana. Haec utique duo ad prioris illius similitudinem constat habere centum cubitos in sua utique longitudine, et quindecim gazophylacia ab orientali latere. Cujus autem latitudinis fuerint, vel quae gazophylacia in alio latere habuerint, melius patebit in subsequentium partium descriptione. Horum igitur quae jam descripsimus formam taliter repraesentamus. 535D

CHAPTER THE FIRST. A summary comprehension of those buildings which belong to the one half.

Being about to speak of Ezekiel's building, I wish first to run over the whole of it in summary, so that afterward I may be free to dwell in the description of the several parts the more amply, the more freely. This building, then, had a threefold wall. The first of six hundred cubits, the second of five hundred cubits, but the third in the middle of these, and the least of them all, in this manner. 533

533CWithin the innermost wall was the inner court; between the innermost and the middle was the outer court. But between the middle and the outermost was the place of the common people. Of the inner court we read: And he measured the court, the length a hundred cubits, and the breadth a hundred cubits, foursquare (Ezech. XL). Of the second wall it is written: By the four winds he measured it, its wall on every side round about, the length five hundred cubits, and the breadth five hundred cubits, dividing between the sanctuary and the place of the common people (Ezech. XLII). Of this sanctuary it is written again in another place: And it shall be sanctified on every side five hundred by five hundred, foursquare round about, 534Cand fifty cubits in its suburbs round about (Ezech. XLV). He designates one and the same place by "the suburbs" and by "the place of the common people," and fittingly enough, inasmuch as he keeps the metaphor, having at the beginning of his description likened the building he saw to a city. It is agreed, therefore, that between the middle wall and the outermost there were fifty cubits, if by "the suburbs" and "the place of the common people" one and the same place is to be understood. Of this outermost wall it is said in the very opening of the description: And behold a wall on the outside round about the house on every side (Ezech. XL).

It is indeed to be noted that this sanctified place, which is described as having five hundred cubits on every side, is now called a building, now the sanctuary, now the house, 535Aand now is likened to a city. Assuredly, if you consult reason as much as Scripture, you will find that each one of these three walls had three gates. One from the east, another from the north, a third from the south. Of the three gates of the inner court, and of the three others of the outer court, Scripture explains at much length. But of one of the remaining three it makes brief mention. The rest it leaves to be understood. For in vain would the inner walls have gates on the north and on the south, unless directly opposite, through the outer wall, approaches to them lay open. Now the six inner gates each had its own portico, all of which were built after the same likeness and to one measure — except that the gates of the inner court 535Bhad their porticoes on the outside, while the porticoes of the outer court lay on the inside; and that by seven steps one went up into the outer court, and by eight steps into the inner court. And so, for those entering into the outer court, the gates met them before their porticoes. But conversely, at the entrance of the inner court, the porticoes presented themselves before the gates, so that all six porticoes lay alike in the outer court. These porticoes Scripture names now porticoes, now porches; and one and the same portico it calls now a porch, now porches. On the eastern way, indeed, between the twin porticoes of the outer court and of the inner court, lay a pavement laid with stone, having a hundred cubits 535Cof length and a hundred cubits of latitudiuis; and thirty chambers on the twin side, fifteen on the south and fifteen on the north. And there were also two other pavements between the other porticoes, one on the north and another on the southern way. These two assuredly are agreed, after the likeness of that first one, to have a hundred cubits in their length, and fifteen chambers on the eastern side. But of what breadth they were, or what chambers they had on the other side, will better appear in the description of the parts that follow. Of these things, then, which we have now described, we represent the form in this fashion. 535D

CAPUT II. Compendiosa praelibatio eorum aedificiorum quae ad aliam medietatem pertinent.

536A

Nunc autem si totum hoc aedificium ducta linea de meridiana porta ad aquilonarem in gemina dividatur, ea quae ad unam ejus medietatem, orientalem videlicet partem, pertinent, transcurrisse videmur. Nunc igitur de occidua ejus parte aliquid breviter perstringamus; nam de his omnibus in subsequenti opere latius disseremus. Eminebat itaque templum cum vestibulo suo a parte occident eratali, situmque in atrio exteriori, cujus vestibulum ab orientali parte jungebatur atrio interiori. Per tres autem ejus partes, tres domus erant sitae: una ab occidente caeterae autem duae, una ab aquilone, et alia a meridie. 536BErant autem omnes ejusdem longitudinis: nam unaquaeque earum sicut et ipsum templum extendebatur centum cubitis. Quod autem ab occidente erat aedificium separatum Scriptura appellat, ex eo, ut arbitror, quod nulli alio aedificio cohaerebat. Hujus aedificii longitudo prope medium murum extendebatur versus aquilonem et meridiem. Nam aliarum porrigebatur ab oriente in occidentem. Habebat sane domus aquilonaris porticum unam in quinquaginta cubitos porrectam, porticuique atrii interioris conjunctam. De quo loco Scriptura commemorat, cum dicit: Ubi erat porticus juncta porticui triplici (Ezech. XLII). A porticu itaque aquilonaris portae atrii interioris, domus cum sua porticu occidentem versus extendebatur centum quadraginta 536Cquinque cubitis. Nam unus murus quinque cubitorum erat, tam domui quam ejus porticui communis. Domum autem australem ad eamdem similitudinem per omnia factam intelligimus, propter illud quod scriptum invenimus: Omnis introitus eorum et similitudines, et ostia eorum secundum ostia gazophylaciorum quae erant in via respiciente ad notum: ostium in capite viae; quae via erat ante vestibulum separatum per viam orientalem ingredientibus. Aedificium autem separatum probatur duo vestibula habere, unum ab aquilone, et aliud a meridie, quod nos possumus ex hac eadem Scriptura convincere. Si enim in aquilonari parte vestibulum erat ante aedificium separatum, et via ante vestibulum, 536Det in capite viae ostium, ut sit similis introitus, et una similitudo utrobique, oportet totum hoc et australem plagam habere vestibulum et viam ante et ostium in capite viae. Habebat itaque templum ab oriente interius atrium, ab occidente aedificium separatum, ab aquilone domum unam, et a meridie domum aliam. Inter tria autem illa aedificia et templum jacebat atrium undique per circuitum. In his vero tribus aedificiis erat mansio triplex. Nam in inferioribus gazophylacia, in superioribus autem erant coenacula, et praeter haec adhuc ante ipsa solaria quaedam deambulatoria de ipsis coenaculorum parietibus eminentia, quae Graeci quidam exedras, Scriptura vero appellat ethecas. Et mensus est, inquit Scriptura, ethecas in circuitu per tres partes 537Acontra uniuscujusque limen (Ezech. XLI). De quibusdam autem gazophylaciis ex industria hic praeterimus, de quibus tamen sicut et de coquinis suo loco dicemus. Haec itaque pauca ad dicendorum evidentiam 538Asummatim praelibavimus, nunc ad singulorum discussionem latius vacabimus. Horum itaque quae jam descripsimus formam hujusmodi repraesentamus. 537

CHAPTER II. A summary foretaste of those buildings which belong to the other half.

536A

Now if this whole building be divided into two by a line drawn from the southern gate to the northern, we seem to have run through those things which belong to the one half of it, namely the eastern part. Now, therefore, let us briefly touch on something of its western part; for concerning all these things we shall discourse more amply in the work that follows. The temple, then, with its porch, stood out on the occident eratali part, and was set in the outer court, and its porch was joined on the eastern part to the inner court. And along three of its sides three houses were situated: one on the west, and the other two, one on the north and another on the south. 536BAnd all were of the same length: for each one of them, like the temple itself, extended a hundred cubits. But that which was on the west, Scripture calls the separate building, for this reason, as I judge, that it adjoined no other building. The length of this building extended near the middle wall toward the north and the south. For that of the others reached from the east to the west. The northern house, indeed, had one portico stretched out to fifty cubits, and joined to the portico of the inner court. Of which place Scripture makes mention when it says: Where there was a portico joined to a threefold portico (Ezech. XLII). From the portico, then, of the northern gate of the inner court, the house with its portico extended westward a hundred and forty 536Cfive cubits. For one wall of five cubits was common both to the house and to its portico. But we understand that the southern house was made in all respects after the same likeness, because of what we find written: All their entrance and their likenesses, and their doors according to the doors of the chambers which were on the way looking toward the south: a door at the head of the way; which way was before the separate porch, by the eastern way, for those entering. But the separate building is proved to have two porches, one on the north and another on the south, which we can establish out of this same Scripture. For if on the northern side there was a porch before the separate building, and a way before the porch, 536Dand at the head of the way a door, so that the entrance may be alike, and the likeness one on both sides, it must be that this whole thing, and the southern region also, has a porch, and a way before it, and a door at the head of the way. And so the temple had on the east the inner court, on the west the separate building, on the north one house, and on the south another house. And between those three buildings and the temple lay the court, on every side round about. But in these three buildings there was a threefold dwelling. For in the lower parts were chambers, but in the upper were upper rooms; and besides these, there were still, before those very upper chambers, certain walking-terraces standing out from the walls of the upper rooms themselves, which certain Greeks call exedrae, but Scripture calls ethecae. And he measured, says Scripture, the ethecae round about on three sides, 537Aover against the threshold of each one (Ezech. XLI). But of certain chambers we here pass over of set purpose, of which nevertheless, as also of the kitchens, we shall speak in their own place. These few things, then, we have summarily set forth beforehand for the clearness of what is to be said; 538Anow we shall be free to dwell more amply upon the discussion of the several parts. Of these things, then, which we have now described, we represent the form in this fashion. 537

CAPUT III Quomodo, vel quam multis circumstantiis mystica haec descriptio commendetur.

537B

Notandum sane in primis quis, a quo, vel quomodo, unde, vel ubi ductus sit qui haec vidit et scripsit, nobisque legenda reliquit: Facta est, inquit, super me manus Domini, et adduxit me illuc. In visionibus Dei adduxit me in terram Israel (Ezech. XL). Propheta sane Domini erat qui ista videbat. A quo tamen ductus est, nisi per manum Domini, vel quomodo, quaeso, nisi quomodo scriptum est in visionibus Dei? Adductus est autem in terram promissionis, haud dubium ante quod de terra Babylonis, de terra ad terram tot terrarum spatiis interjectam. Quem, quaeso, non moveat, quem jure movere 537Cnon debeat quod talis a tali, taliter, tamque longe adductus est ut videret et audiret quae postea scriberet, posterisque legenda relinqueret? Quis nisi insensatus negligenter audiat, vel negligere audeat quod Scriptura sacra tot circumstantiis commendat? Sed nec ipsum tempus quidem in quo haec vidit propheta tacite praeterire voluit. Nam et hoc ipsum fortassis ad visionis ipsius commendationem pertinere praevidit: In vicesimo et quinto anno transmigrationis nostrae, in exordio anni, decima 538Bmensis, quartodecimo anno postquam percussa est civitas, in ipsa hac die facta est super me manus Domini, et adduxit me illuc in visionibus Dei. Judaicae jam captivitatis multa tempora fluxerant, jamdiu sub magna afflictione filii Israel laboraverant, et tam pium quam necessarium fuit eos inter tot dura et diuturna mala magnam aliquam consolationem recipere, et divinae erga se pietatis argumenta sentire, ne inter tot calamitates penitus deficerent, et de divino adjutorio funditus desperarent. Sed quis magnum, imo supra hominem esse dubitet quod propheta Domini per manum Domini deductus in visionibus Dei videt? Adduxit me, inquit, in terram Israel, et dimisit me super montem excelsum nimis, super quem erat quasi 538Caedificium civitatis vergentis ad austrum, et introduxit me illuc (Ezech. XLIII). Non negligenter praetereundum quod ostensum aedificium in monte nimis excelso dicitur constitutum. Multorum siquidem nobis intelligentiam claudimus, si haec negligenter consideramus; quod ex subsequentibus facile advertimus. In hoc sane aedificio interius atrium tenebat intimum locum; sed templum procul dubio tenebat summum. Hinc est quod in novissimis hujus descriptionis legitur, ubi dicitur: Ista est lex domus, in summitate 539Amontis omnes fines ejus, in circuitu sanctum sanctorum est (Ezech. XLIII). Domum hic pro more suo dicit templum. Mons itaque usque in id loci in quo templum constructum erat paulatim ad altiora surgebat, et ad eum locum ab omni parte inclinabat, et ibi se in planam quamdam aequalemque planitiem expandebat, quae templi structuram aequali eminentia circumquaque excipiebat. Si enim ab una qualibet parte templum altius emineret, omnis ejus finis procul dubio montis summitatem non teneret. Consideremus, si placet, prius hoc aedificium quasi in plano constitutum sit, ut forma montis aedificiorumque situs postmodum rectius intelligi possit. Ut ex superioribus colligitur medius murus quo atrium exterius cingitur ab omni latere in oppositum latus 539Bquingentos cubitos includere comprobatur.

Ut igitur incipiamus ab oriente ex his quingentis cubitis ducentos possidebat atrium exterius, in tertio vero centenario erat atrium interius, in quarto centenario templum situm fuit, et quintum centenarium, et eo amplius post tergum reliquit. Hoc tamen secundum plane superficiei considerationem dictum sit, nam ad hanc mensuram tumor montis nonnihil adjecit, de quibus in loco suo diligentius considerare oportebit. Ex hac tamen consideratione facile advertere licet contra quem locum mons altius intumesceret, vel cujusmodi formam haberet, vel ex qua parte devexior esset. Dicitur autem recte hoc aedificium habere similitudinem civitatis, eo quod esset undique cinctum muris, firmatum portis, 539Cmultis stipatum aedificiis. Et ecce vir, cujus erat species quasi species aeris, et funiculus lineus in manu ejus, et calamus mensurae in manu ejus. Stabat autem in porta. Vir iste qui speciem aeris habere describitur, imaginem non terreni sed coelestis portare videtur. Coelestia ergo de coelesti, sublimia de sublimi sperare debemus, et ea quae dicit vel facit studiosa mente pertractemus. Sed quod vir iste coelestis funiculum, et calamum mensurae ad aedificium dimetiendum in manu portat, utinam ad hoc saltem apud fastidiosos auditores valeat, ut nemo nos reprehendere audeat, cum nos viderit singulorum aedificiorum situm, qualitatem et mensuram subtiliter quaerere et investigare, et cum tanta diligentia assignare et singula quaeque prout possumus, 539Dvel Scripturae auctoritate, vel rationis attestatione probare. Sed ne forte quod hucusque dictum est ad horum commendationem minus sufficiat, audiamus adhuc quid idem vir dicat, quid de his Domini propheta praecipiat. Et locutus est, inquit, ad me idem vir: Fili hominis, vide oculis tuis, et auribus tuis audi, et pone cor tuum in omnia quae ego ostendam tibi; quia, ut ostendantur tibi, adductus es huc; annuntia omnia quae tu vides domui Israel. Situs et mensuram aedificiorum visu, ritus et modum caeremoniarum auditu percipere jubetur, et ad singula cor apponere, subtiliter intelligere, memoriter retinere, nec sibi soli tantae scientiae talenta avare retinere, sed nec illi vel illi, imo domui Israel omnia annuntiare. 540AQuem, obsecro, nequeat coelestis oraculi tanta contestatio attentum vel attonitum reddere, et ad horum intelligentiam desiderio inflammare? Sed ad id nunc redeamus, quod eumdem virum in porta stare audivimus, ad quem trium murorum praedictorum haec porta videatur pertinere, ex eo quod in subsequentibus adjungitur, possumus advertere.

Et venit ad portam, inquit, quae respiciebat viam orientalem, et ascendit per gradus ejus. Procul dubio alia est porta ad quam venit, et alia porta de qua venit, in qua et prius stetit. Sed luce clarius constat portam ad quam veniebat esse atrii exterioris, non atrii interioris. Hoc enim sequentia tam aperte indicant, ut nostra probatione non egeant. Sed si de aliqua atrii interioris porta ad hanc 540Borientalem portam, quam primo loco metitur veniret, per ejus utique gradus descendere, non autem ascendere haberet. Sed dicitur: Ei ascendit per gradus ejus. Sed, ne hoc de alia aliqua atrii exterioris porta possimus accipere, manifesta ratio videtur contraire. Primo enim in atrio exteriori orientalis portae mensuram accipit, et postea ad alias dimetiendas per ordinem transit. Cur, quaeso, primo loco ejus mensuras non caperet in qua primo stetisset, si hoc de alia aliqua atrii exterioris porta intelligi voluisset. Patet ergo quod extimi muri haec porta fuerit in qua primo loco stetit, de quo muro dicitur: Ecce murus forinsecus in circuitu domus undique (Ezech. XL). Sed quia de hoc muro ejusque mensura adjuncto Scripturae testimonio sufficienter 540Csuperius dixisse me credo, hic eadem replicare devito: Et in manu viri calamus mensurae sex cubitorum, et palmo (ibid.). Mensuram calami hoc loco definit, quod superius cum de eo loqueretur minime determinavit. Cur, quaeso, in hoc calamo ad sex cubitos palmus adjicitur, nisi quod inter metiendum ex ea parte apprehenditur atque tenetur? Quotiescunque igitur calami mensuram metientem prophetam colligere audimus, nihil aliud, quamvis quidam aliter autument, quam sex cubitos intelligere debemus. Hinc est quod thalamus calami mensuram prius habere describitur, et postea per recapitulationem de eodem scribitur. Thalami autem sex cubitorum erant hinc et inde. Multipliciter sane idipsum probari 540Dvalet, quod per seipsum satis diligens lector inveniet: Et mensus est, inquit, latitudinem aedificii calamo uno, altitudinem quoque calamo uno. Si ad totum refertur quod hic de quodam, nescio quo aedificio dicitur, totius descriptionis veritas et auctoritas evacuatur. Nonne, obsecro, multo rectius multoque convenientius est ad partem referre, quam ad totum referendo totum evacuare? Nonne magis congruum, rationique consentaneum videtur esse, credere Scripturam hoc loco cujusdam aedificii mensuras describere, quod juxta seu supra portam erat in qua virum stare, et necdum quoque recessisse commemorat? Sed nunquid quia semel Scriptura totum aedificium nominavit, hoc vocabulo deinceps partem designare non potuit, vel non debuit? 541ANonne totum similiter domum nominavit, et tamen hic nomen in subsequentibus ad partem referre non timuit, cum dicit: Et erat interior domus in lateribus domus? Quid si hoc ipsum aedificii nomen invenimus ubi ita positum sit, ut omnino ad totum referri non possit? Et erant ante aedificium gazophylacia, et via ante faciem eorum juxta similitudinem gazophylaciorum quae erant in via aquilonis.

Sed vel hoc solum debet nobis in hac re fidem facere; quia, si hoc hic aedificii nomen volumus ad totum referre, omnium quae hic dicuntur, ut dictum est, convincimur fidem cassare. Puto tamen neminem esse tam improbum, neminem tam posse inveniri protervum, quem in hac parte in tam aperta 541Bratione nobis contraire non pudeat, qui in hac nos sententia reprehendere non erubescat, cum caetera omnia juxta nostram expositionem ad litteram posse stare perspexerit, si in tam perspicua veritate sanum intellectum non subsannaverit.

Sed haec hactenus: nunc ad litterae expositionem redeamus: Et mensus est latitudinem aedificii calamo uno, altitudinem quoque calamo uno. Si dixisset: Mensus est latitudinem cujusdam aedificii in eo loco quo prius stetit; si hoc, inquam, dixisset, omnem ambiguitatem tolleret. Nec tamen quia hoc non dixit veritatem abstulit, sed pro more suo obnubilavit. Sed unum ex subsequentibus 541Cintelligi cogit, alterum ex praecedentibus innuit: nam quoniam virum qui haec metiebatur de porta qua prius steterat necdum recessisse memoratur, et loco hoc aedificium conjunctum fuisse manifeste innuitur. Quid autem illud dicimus esse quod cum dicat de latitudine et altitudine, omnino tacet de longitudine, cum naturaliter semper prior fit longitudo quam latitudo, vel altitudo?

Sed attende quam artificiose in eo quod latitudinem dixit, longitudinem non tacuit. Ubi enim longitudo et latitudo aequalis est in quamlibet partem, latitudo recte dici potest.

Tacendo ergo quod prius dicere debuit, eamdem esse longitudinem et latitudinem satis argute 541Dostendit. Quod autem de hac una porta dicimus, hoc de duabus reliquis extimi muri portis recte intelligimus, quamvis Scriptura manifeste id non dicat, sed magis subintelligendum relinquat. Neque enim oportuit in his eum diutius morari quae per se poterant satis subintelligi. Utrum autem haec aedificia in janitorum vel excubiarum ministeria deputanda sint, incertum habemus; nos tamen ea quasi ad excubiarum officia supra portas locamus, secundum quod scriptum invenimus: Super muros tuos, Hierusalem, constitui custodes, tota die et nocte non tacebunt laudare nomen Domini (Isa. LXII).

Horum itaque quae diximus tale exemplar formamus: 542A

542BNotandum sane quod alia translatio non aedificium, sed murum habet, ad quem sensum, qui litteram nostram applicare voluerit, ab omni se quaestione absolvit, cum latitudinem ad muri spissitudinem referre possit.

Et venit ad portam quae respicit viam orientalem, et ascendit per gradus ejus. Quia orientalis est porta ad quam venit, recte et orientalis esse creditur illa de qua venit. Nam si de alia aliqua veniret, tanto rectius quanto directius ad 542Caliam, hoc est ad ante positam properaret.

Post hanc portam orientalem de quo modo loquitur aquilonaris, et meridiana atrii exterioris, et tunc demum tres aliae portae atrii interioris nominatim exprimuntur: unde hanc atrii exterioris esse, quamvis id Scriptura tacuerit, indubitanter colligitur.

Hanc portam gradus habere Scriptura commemorat, quot tamen habuerit aperte nusquam determinat. Sed quia octo habere leguntur simul omnes tres atrii interioris, et septem duae aliae atrii exterioris, admonemur de similibus similia aestimare, et idcirco credimus eam ad exteriorum similitudinem septem gradus habere.

Et ascendit, inquit, per gradus ejus, et mensus est 542Dlimen portae calamo uno, latitudinem, id est limen unum calamo uno in latitudine, et thalamum uno calamo in longum, et uno calamo in latum, et inter thalamos quinque cubitos.

CHAPTER III In what manner, and by how many circumstances, this mystical description is commended.

537B

It is indeed to be noted first of all who he was, by whom, and in what manner, whence and whither he was led who saw these things and wrote them and left them to us to be read: The hand of the Lord, he says, came upon me, and brought me thither. In the visions of God he brought me into the land of Israel (Ezech. XL). He who saw these things was assuredly a prophet of the Lord. Yet by whom was he led, save by the hand of the Lord; or in what manner, I ask, save as it is written, in the visions of God? And he was brought into the land of promise — there is no doubt before, that it was from the land of Babylon, from a land to a land with the spaces of so many lands lying between. Whom, I ask, would it not move; whom by right ought it 537Cnot to move, that such a man was led by such a one, in such a manner, and so far, that he might see and hear what he should afterward write and leave to those coming after to be read? Who but a senseless man would hear negligently, or dare to neglect, what Holy Scripture commends with so many circumstances? But not even the very time in which the prophet saw these things did he wish to pass over in silence. For perhaps he foresaw that this very thing pertained to the commendation of the vision itself: In the twenty-fifth year of our transmigration, at the beginning of the year, on the tenth 538Bof the month, in the fourteenth year after the city was struck down, on this very day the hand of the Lord came upon me, and brought me thither in the visions of God. Many seasons of the Jewish captivity had already flowed by; long since had the children of Israel labored under great affliction; and it was as merciful as it was necessary that they should receive, amid so many hard and long-lasting evils, some great consolation, and should feel the proofs of the divine mercy toward them, lest amid so many calamities they should utterly fail, and should despair from the very foundation of the divine help. But who would doubt that it is a great thing, nay a thing above man, that a prophet of the Lord, led by the hand of the Lord, sees in the visions of God? He brought me, he says, into the land of Israel, and set me down upon an exceeding high mountain, upon which was as it were 538Cthe building of a city inclining toward the south, and he brought me in thither (Ezech. XLIII). It is not to be passed over negligently that the building shown is said to have been set upon an exceeding high mountain. For we shut off from ourselves the understanding of many things if we consider these matters negligently, as we easily notice from what follows. In this building, assuredly, the inner court held the innermost place; but the temple, without doubt, held the highest. Hence it is that in the last part of this description it is read, where it is said: This is the law of the house: on the summit 539Aof the mountain are all its borders; round about it is the Holy of Holies (Ezech. XLIII). Here, after his custom, he calls the temple "the house." The mountain, then, rose little by little to higher ground as far as that place in which the temple was built, and it sloped toward that place from every side, and there spread itself out into a certain flat and even plain, which received the structure of the temple with an equal eminence on every side. For if on any one side the temple stood out higher, without doubt not all its border would hold the summit of the mountain. Let us consider first, if it please, this building as though it were set upon a level, so that the form of the mountain and the situation of the buildings may afterward be more rightly understood. As is gathered from the foregoing, the middle wall, by which the outer court is girded, is proved to enclose from every side to the opposite side 539Bfive hundred cubits.

That we may begin, then, from the east: of these five hundred cubits the outer court possessed two hundred; in the third hundred was the inner court; in the fourth hundred the temple was situated; and the fifth hundred, and beyond that, it left behind its back. Yet let this be said according to the consideration of the plain surface only, for to this measure the swelling of the mountain added not a little, concerning which it will be needful to consider more diligently in its own place. From this consideration, nevertheless, it is easy to notice at what place the mountain swelled higher, or what sort of form it had, or on which side it was more sloping. And this building is rightly said to have the likeness of a city, in that it was girded on every side with walls, made fast with gates, 539Cand crowded with many buildings. And behold a man whose appearance was as the appearance of brass, and a line of flax in his hand, and a measuring reed in his hand. And he stood in the gate. This man, who is described as having the appearance of brass, seems to bear the image not of an earthly but of a heavenly thing. Heavenly things, therefore, we ought to hope for from the heavenly one, and sublime things from the sublime; and let us handle with a studious mind the things which he says or does. But as for this heavenly man's carrying in his hand a line and a measuring reed to measure out the building — would that it might avail at least for this among disdainful hearers, that no one dare to reprove us when he has seen us subtly seeking out and investigating the situation, the quality, and the measure of the several buildings, and with such great diligence assigning them, and proving each single thing as we can, 539Deither by the authority of Scripture or by the attestation of reason. But lest perhaps what has been said thus far be less than enough for the commendation of these things, let us hear still what the same man says, and what the prophet of the Lord enjoins concerning them. And the same man spoke, he says, to me: Son of man, see with thine eyes, and hear with thine ears, and set thy heart upon all things that I shall show thee; for thou hast been brought hither that they may be shown thee; announce all things that thou seest to the house of Israel. He is bidden to perceive by sight the situation and the measure of the buildings, and by hearing the rite and the manner of the ceremonies; and to set his heart to each single thing, to understand subtly, to retain by memory, and not to keep greedily to himself alone the talents of so great knowledge — but neither to this man nor to that: nay rather, to announce all things to the house of Israel. 540AWhom, I beseech you, could so great an attestation of the heavenly oracle not render attentive or astounded, and inflame with desire toward the understanding of these things? But let us now return to this, that we have heard the same man standing in a gate. To which of the three aforesaid walls this gate may seem to belong, we can notice from what is added in what follows.

And he came to the gate, he says, which looked toward the eastern way, and went up by its steps. Without doubt the gate to which he came is one, and the gate from which he came, in which he had also first stood, is another. But it is clearer than light that the gate to which he was coming is that of the outer court, not of the inner court. For the things that follow indicate this so openly that they need no proof of ours. But if he came from some gate of the inner court to this 540Beastern gate, which he measures in the first place, he would assuredly have to descend by its steps, not to ascend. But it is said: To him he went up by its steps. But lest we should be able to take this of some other gate of the outer court, manifest reason seems to go against it. For first, in the outer court, he takes the measure of the eastern gate, and afterward passes on in order to the measuring of the others. Why, I ask, would he not take first the measures of that gate in which he had first stood, if he had wished this to be understood of some other gate of the outer court. It is plain, therefore, that this gate in which he first stood was of the outermost wall, of which wall it is said: Behold a wall on the outside round about the house on every side (Ezech. XL). But since concerning this wall and its measure, with the testimony of Scripture adjoined, I believe I have sufficiently 540Cspoken above, I here avoid repeating the same things: And in the man's hand a measuring reed of six cubits and a palm (ibid.). In this place he defines the measure of the reed, which above, when he was speaking of it, he by no means determined. Why, I ask, is a palm added to six cubits in this reed, save because in the act of measuring it is grasped and held by that part? As often, then, as we hear the prophet in measuring reckon up the measure of the reed, we ought to understand nothing else — although some judge otherwise — than six cubits. Hence it is that the little chamber is described as first having the measure of a reed, and afterward it is written of the same by way of recapitulation. And the little chambers were of six cubits on this side and on that. This very thing can assuredly be proved 540Din many ways, which a sufficiently diligent reader will find for himself: And he measured, he says, the breadth of the building with one reed, the height also with one reed. If what is here said of a certain building, I know not which, is referred to the whole, the truth and authority of the whole description is emptied out. Is it not, I beseech you, far more right and far more fitting to refer it to a part, than by referring it to the whole to empty out the whole? Does it not seem more agreeable and more consonant with reason to believe that Scripture in this place describes the measures of a certain building which was beside or above the gate in which it records that the man was standing, and had not yet withdrawn? But could Scripture, because it once named the whole a building, not thereafter designate a part by this word, or ought it not to have done so? 541ADid it not likewise name the whole "the house," and yet here it did not fear in what follows to refer this name to a part, when it says: And the interior of the house was in the sides of the house? What if we find this very name of "building" set down where it can in no wise be referred to the whole? And there were chambers before the building, and a way before their face, after the likeness of the chambers which were on the northern way.

But even this alone ought to make us credited in this matter: that if we wish here to refer this name of "building" to the whole, we are convicted, as has been said, of annulling the credit of all the things that are here said.
