Yet I think there is no one so shameless, none so impudent can be found, that he would not be ashamed in this matter to contradict us in a reason so open, 541Band would not blush to reprove us in this opinion, when he has clearly seen that all the rest can stand according to the letter after our exposition — if in a truth so perspicuous he has not mocked at sound understanding.

But so much for these things: now let us return to the exposition of the letter: And he measured the breadth of the building with one reed, the height also with one reed. If he had said: He measured the breadth of a certain building in that place in which he first stood — if, I say, he had said this, he would have taken away all ambiguity. Yet not because he did not say this did he take away the truth, but after his custom he clouded it over. But one thing he compels to be understood from what follows, 541Cthe other he hints from what precedes: for since it is recorded that the man who was measuring these things had not yet withdrawn from the gate in which he had first stood, it is also manifestly hinted that this building was joined to that place. But what shall we say that this is, that, though he speaks of breadth and of height, he is altogether silent about length — since naturally the length always comes before the breadth or the height?

But mark how artfully, in that he said the breadth, he did not keep silent about the length. For where the length and the breadth are equal toward every side, it may rightly be called breadth.

By keeping silent, therefore, about what he ought to have said first, he showed shrewdly enough 541Dthat the length and the breadth were the same. And what we say of this one gate we rightly understand of the two remaining gates of the outermost wall, although Scripture does not manifestly say it, but rather leaves it to be understood. For it was not needful for him to linger longer over things which could of themselves be sufficiently understood. But whether these buildings are to be assigned to the services of the doorkeepers or of the watches, we hold uncertain; we nevertheless place them above the gates, as though for the offices of the watches, according to what we find written: Upon thy walls, O Jerusalem, I have set watchmen; all the day and the night they shall not be silent to praise the name of the Lord (Isa. LXII).

Of these things, then, which we have said, we form such a pattern: 542A

542BIt is indeed to be noted that another translation has not "building" but "wall"; and whoever shall wish to apply our letter to that sense frees himself from every question, since he can refer the breadth to the thickness of the wall.

And he came to the gate which looks toward the eastern way, and went up by its steps. Since the gate to which he came is the eastern one, that from which he came is rightly believed to be eastern also. For if he were coming from some other, the more directly he hastened to another — that is, to the one set opposite — the more rightly 542Cwould he do so.

After this eastern gate, of which he is now speaking, the northern and the southern of the outer court, and then at last the three other gates of the inner court, are expressed by name; whence it is gathered beyond doubt that this one is of the outer court, although Scripture has kept silent about it.

That this gate has steps Scripture records, yet how many it had it nowhere openly determines. But because all three of the inner court are read to have eight together, and the two others of the outer court seven, we are admonished to reckon like things from like; and therefore we believe that it has seven steps, after the likeness of the outer ones.

And he went up, he says, by its steps, and he measured 542Dthe threshold of the gate with one reed, the breadth — that is, one threshold, one reed in breadth — and the little chamber one reed in length and one reed in breadth, and between the little chambers five cubits.

CAPUT IV. Collectis hinc inde Scripturae testimoniis, hoc hic de una porticu astruitur quod de caeteris omnibus jure intelligatur.

Quoniam ad portarum, seu etiam porticuum descriptionem ex ordine jam venimus, et multa de eis tam obscure prolata, tam ambigue dicta invenimus, libet tam ex hoc quam ex aliis Scripturae locis hoc hic de una portica astruere, quod oporteat de caeteris aliis omnibus indubitanter intelligere. Erant 543Aenim omnes sex per omnia similis facturae, ejusdemque mensurae, nihilque aliud, si bene recolo, habebant dissimile, nisi quod interiorum portarum porticus jacebant extrinsecus, exteriorum vero jacebant intrinsecus, et quod illorum ascensus in octo, istorum autem erat in septem gradibus. Quod autem omnes has sex porticus ejusdem mensurae esse dicimus, non nostra auctoritate, sed Scripturae attestatione probamus. Post hujus portae dimensiones de qua modo loquitur de aquilonari porta statim subinfertur:

Portam quoque quae respiciebat viam aquilonis atrii exterioris mensus est, tam in longitudine quam in latitudine, et thalamos ejus tres hinc, et tres inde, et frontem ejus, et vestibulum ejus secundum 543Bmensuram portae prioris. Similiter de australi porta scribitur: Et ecce porta quae respiciebat ad austrum, et mensus est frontem ejus, et vestibulum ejus juxta mensuras superiores, et fenestras ejus, et vestibula in circuitu sicut fenestras caeteras. Haec sane sententia in omnium sex porticuum descriptione semper repetitur, unde una omnium eademque mensura esse convincitur. Unumquodque igitur horum vestibulorum seu porticuum multipliciter divisum erat. Unde et Scriptura saepe unam eamdemque porticum, modo singulariter pro toto vestibulum, modo pluraliter pro partibus vestibula vocat. Principaliter tamen unaquaeque porticus in tria dividebatur, unde et in sequentibus una illarum 543Ctriplex nominatur, quae sententia quidem ad omnes juxta praedictam rationem jure refertur:

Ubi erat, inquit, porticus juncta porticui triplici. De toto itaque videamus prius, et postea de partibus. Frontes itaque exteriorum porticuum respiciebant versus atrium interius, sicut e converso interiorum respiciebant versus atrium exterius. Harum frontium mensura describitur cum dicitur: Et fecit frontes per sexaginta cubitos. Haec erat mensura longitudinis omnium porticuum, seu vestibulorum, cum tamen per haec nomina intelligimus non partem, sed totum. Hujus itaque orientalis portae porticus frontem habebat versus occidentem, cujus longitudo extendebatur versus aquilonem et meridiem. 543DExtendebatur autem sexaginta cubitis juxta mensuram frontis. Latitudo vero ab oriente in occidentem dilatabatur quinquaginta cubitis. Unde est illud: et ante faciem portae quae pertingebat usque ad faciem vestibuli portae interioris quinquaginta cubitos. Habebat sane haec porticus sicut caeterae portam in fronte, hoc est in introitu atrii contra portam muri. Alioquin frustra in muro aditus pateret extrinsecus, si ex opposito in fronte vestibuli obstrueretur intrinsecus. Habebat itaque vestibulum portam contra portam, in muro unam, in fronte aliam. Ad utriusque itaque distinctionem vocetur una porta muri, alia dicatur porta vestibuli. Portam itaque interiorem hoc loco dicit portam vestibuli, non portam interioris atrii. Nam inter exteriorem 544Aet illam (excepto eo quod muri occupabant) erant ducenti cubiti. Faciem autem portae dicit locum qui erat ante portam exteriorem, sed retro portam interiorem. Faciem vero vestibuli portae interioris, dicit latus illud quod vestibulum habebat ad portam interiorem. Unde manifeste colligitur quod antedicta mensura intra parietes sumebatur.

Bene ergo dicitur: Et ante faciem portae quae pertingebat usque ad faciem vestibuli portae interioris quinquaginta cubitos. Si quis autem id quod diximus de hujus vestibuli fronte sive de ejus longitudine velit in aliam partem accipere, et longitudinem ipsius non ab aquilone in meridiem, sed ab oriente in occidentem velit extendere, omnino non poterit 544Bstare, et frivolum duco in tam aperta re frustra laborare. Haec autem porticus sicut unaquaeque aliarum in tria principaliter dividitur. Unde, ut dictum est, triplex porticus recte nominatur. Harum autem partium, seu porticuum quae medium tenebat nihil aliud erat nisi spatium illud seu via illa, quae inter portam et portam jacebat, a porta videlicet muri usque ad portam vestibuli, cujus latitudo erat secundum latitudinem portae decem cubitorum. Longitudo vero secundum latitudinem totius porticus quinquaginta cubitorum. Hoc autem medium, et minimum circumjacebant duo alia ejusdem mensurae, unum in uno, et aliud in alio latere, utrumque quinquaginta cubitorum in longitudine, et in latitudine cubitorum viginti quinque. Horum utique vestibulorum 544Cin aliarum omnium portarum descriptione Scriptura mentionem facit, et nunc ea singulari, nunc plurali numero designans eorum mensuras describit. Hinc est quod de aquilonari porta legitur, quae statim post hanc subsequenter describitur:

Portam quoque quae respiciebat viam aquilonis atrii exterioris mensus est tam in longitudine quam in latitudine, et thalamos ejus tres hinc, et tres inde, et frontem, et vestibulum ejus secundum mensuram portae prioris, qninquaginta cubitorum longitudinem ejus, et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum. In hoc ergo quod dicitur secundum mensuram prioris portae, constat hanc de qua modo disserimus 544Dorientalem portam tale, hoc est tantae magnitudinis vestibulum habere. Sed ne unum tantum tale habuisse credatur, audi quid postmodum de australi porta scribitur: Et eduxit me ad portam australem, et mensus est frontem ejus, et vestibulum ejus juxta mensuras superiores, et fenestras ejus, et vestibula in circuitu sicut fenestras caeteras quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinem, et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum. Quod prius dicit vestibulum, hoc refertur ad totum, ad partes autem quod repetitur vestibula: unde et ibi additur in circuitu, eo quod medio vestibulo, seu ipsi portae adjacerent per duo ejus latera. Haec itaque tria vestibula unum medium, et duo lateralia erant sibi invicem in hunc modum conjuncta, et implebant cubitos sexaginta. 545A

CHAPTER IV. The testimonies of Scripture being gathered from this side and that, that is here established concerning one portico which may rightly be understood of all the rest.

Since we have now come in order to the description of the gates, or also of the porticoes, and find many things concerning them so obscurely set forth and so ambiguously said, it pleases me, both from this and from other places of Scripture, to establish here concerning one portico what must be understood beyond doubt of all the others. For 543Aall six were in all respects of like fabric and of the same measure, and, if I recall well, they had nothing else unlike, save that the porticoes of the inner gates lay on the outside, but those of the outer lay on the inside; and that the ascent of the former was by eight steps, but of the latter by seven. And that we say that all these six porticoes are of the same measure, we prove not by our own authority, but by the attestation of Scripture. After the dimensions of this gate of which he is now speaking, there is at once subjoined concerning the northern gate:

The gate also which looked toward the northern way of the outer court he measured, as well in length as in breadth, and its little chambers, three on this side and three on that, and its front, and its porch, according to 543Bthe measure of the former gate. Similarly it is written of the southern gate: And behold a gate which looked toward the south, and he measured its front, and its porch, according to the measures above, and its windows, and the porches round about, like the other windows. This same sentiment is assuredly always repeated in the description of all six porticoes, whence it is proved that the measure of them all is one and the same. Each one, then, of these porches or porticoes was divided in many ways. Whence Scripture also often calls one and the same portico now, in the singular, "porch," for the whole, and now, in the plural, "porches," for the parts. Yet each portico was principally divided into three, whence also in what follows one of them 543Cis named "threefold," which sentiment is indeed rightly referred to them all according to the aforesaid reasoning:

Where there was, he says, a portico joined to a threefold portico. Let us therefore see first concerning the whole, and afterward concerning the parts. The fronts, then, of the outer porticoes looked toward the inner court, just as, conversely, those of the inner ones looked toward the outer court. The measure of these fronts is described when it is said: And he made the fronts sixty cubits. This was the measure of the length of all the porticoes or porches, when nevertheless by these names we understand not a part but the whole. The portico, then, of this eastern gate had its front toward the west, and its length extended toward the north and the south. 543DAnd it extended sixty cubits, according to the measure of the front. But the breadth was widened from the east to the west by fifty cubits. Whence is that: and before the face of the gate, which reached to the face of the porch of the inner gate, fifty cubits. This portico assuredly had, like the rest, a gate in its front, that is, at the entrance of the court, over against the gate of the wall. Otherwise the approach in the wall would lie open on the outside in vain, if it were blocked on the inside opposite, at the front of the porch. And so the porch had a gate over against a gate: one in the wall, another in the front. For the distinguishing of the two, then, let the one be called the gate of the wall, and the other be called the gate of the porch. In this place, then, he calls the gate of the porch the "inner gate," not the gate of the inner court. For between the outer 544Aand that one (except for what the walls took up) there were two hundred cubits. And by the "face of the gate" he means the place which was before the outer gate, but behind the inner gate. But by the "face of the porch of the inner gate" he means that side which the porch had toward the inner gate. Whence it is manifestly gathered that the aforesaid measure was taken within the walls.

Well, then, is it said: And before the face of the gate, which reached to the face of the porch of the inner gate, fifty cubits. But if anyone should wish to take what we have said of the front of this porch, or of its length, in another sense, and should wish to extend its length not from the north to the south but from the east to the west, it will be altogether unable 544Bto stand, and I count it frivolous to labor in vain in a matter so open. Now this portico, like each of the others, is principally divided into three. Whence, as has been said, it is rightly named a threefold portico. And of these parts, or porticoes, that which held the middle was nothing else but that space, or that way, which lay between gate and gate, namely from the gate of the wall as far as the gate of the porch, whose breadth was according to the breadth of the gate, ten cubits; but the length, according to the breadth of the whole portico, fifty cubits. And this middle and least one two others of the same measure lay round about, one on the one side and the other on the other, each of them fifty cubits in length and twenty-five cubits in breadth. Of these porches, assuredly, 544CScripture makes mention in the description of all the other gates, and, designating them now by the singular and now by the plural number, describes their measures. Hence it is that of the northern gate it is read, which is described immediately after this one in what follows:

The gate also which looked toward the northern way of the outer court he measured, as well in length as in breadth, and its little chambers, three on this side and three on that, and the front, and its porch, according to the measure of the former gate: its length qninquaginta cubits, and its breadth twenty-five cubits. In this, then, that it is said "according to the measure of the former gate," it is agreed that this eastern gate of which we are now discoursing 544Dhas such a porch, that is, one of such magnitude. But lest it be believed to have had only one such, hear what is afterward written of the southern gate: And he led me out to the southern gate, and he measured its front, and its porch, according to the measures above, and its windows, and the porches round about, like the other windows: fifty cubits in length, and twenty-five cubits in breadth. What he first calls "porch" is referred to the whole, but what is repeated, "porches," to the parts; whence also there is added "round about," because they adjoined the middle porch, or the gate itself, along its two sides. These three porches, therefore — one middle and two lateral — were joined one to another in this manner, and filled out sixty cubits. 545A

CAPUT V. De his quae ad unam medietatem cujuslibet porticus pertinere videntur.

545B

Sed adhuc in illis duobus lateralibus vestibulis aliae sanae subdivisiones esse probantur, quae et ipsae quidem in Scriptura vestibula nominantur. Invenio ibi vestibulum octo cubitorum, invenio et aliud quinque cubitorum. Vestibulum octo cubitorum invenio apud portam orientalem atrii exterioris ubi legitur: Et mensus est vestibulum portae octo cubitorum. Vestibulum quinque cubitorum latitudinis invenio apud portam australem atrii interioris. De quo scribitur: Et mensus est vestibulum per gyrum, 545Clongitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum, et latitudinem quinque cubitorum. Sed superius Scripturae auctoritate probavimus quia quidquid de una qualibet harum porticuum in his quae ad mensuram pertinent, concedimus, de omnibus pariter pari ratione concedere debemus. Ergo apud omnes porticus erant vestibula octo cubitorum, similiter et apud omnes necesse est, inveniri vestibula quinque cubitorum. Sed nunc prius de situ thalamorum videamus, et tunc fortassis de istis rationem facilius inveniemus. Habet autem unaquaeque porticus sex thalamos, tres hinc, et tres inde ab utraque parte viae, quos, Scriptura docente, novimus intra ipsas porticus sitos fuisse. Porro thalami portae ad viam orientalem 545Dtres hinc, et tres inde. Haec de numero thalamorum, et in sequentibus de situ ipsorum. Et fenestras, inquit, obliquas in thalamis, et in frontibus eorum quae erant intra portam undique per circuitum. Si fenestrae, quae in thalamis erant, intra ipsam portam erant, ergo et ipsi thalami intra portam erant. Nam portam hic pro more suo portam ipsam cum tota porticu sua appellat. Nam quod dicit aliter omnino stare non posset. Tale est illud: Et mensus est portam a tecto thalami usque ad tectum ejus. Similiter hic porticum sub nomine portae designat. Constat autem quia unusquisque thalamus cum margine suo octo cubitos occupabat. Nam quilibet eorum sex cubitorum erat, et marginem unius cubiti non solum ante, sed et ab utroque latere habebat, Scriptura 546Aattestante, quae ait: Et mensus est marginem ante thalamos cubiti unius, et cubitus unus finis utrumque. Thalami autem sex cubitorum erant hinc et inde. Ter autem octo viginti quatuor reddunt, quibus X adjuncti XXXIV efficiunt. Idcirco autem praedictis viginti quatuor quos de tribus thalamis colligimus, adhuc alios decem cubitos adjicimus, quia quinque cubitos inter primum thalamum, et secundum, quinque vero alios inter secundum et tertium invenimus, secundum illud quod legimus: Et inter thalamos quinque cubitos. Erant itaque absque dubio triginta quatuor cubiti ab initio primi thalami usque ad extremitatem tertii. Debent autem esse juxta latera portae tres hinc, et tres inde. Sed vestibula quae in lateribus portae erant quae medio vestibulo decem cubitorum 546Bhinc inde adhaerebant, non plus quam viginti quinque cubitos in latitudine tenebant. Non potuit ergo vestibulorum latitudo trium thalamorum situm capere, quem superius docuimus cubitos triginta quatuor occupare. Longitudo autem vestibulorum quia quinquaginta cubitorum erat, facile eos, et eo amplius capere poterat. Locentur itaque tres thalami in uno vestibuli latere, eo videlicet loci ubi parieti tectum videtur incumbere, ita ut parieti ipsi videantur inhaerere, imo ipsum pro uno sui pariete, et quasi pro una fronte habere ubi intelligimus fenestras illas esse de quibus Scriptura loquitur, cum dicit: Fenestras obliquas in thalamis, et in frontibus eorum, quae erant intra portam undique per circuitum. Exceptis enim aliis absurditatibus quae videntur 546Cinde sequi, quas arbitror per se facile posse intelligi, non erat unde clarum lumen habere potuissent, nisi parietibus vestibuli hinc inde adhaererent, ubi fenestras haberent ut lumen reciperent. Unum igitur ex collateralibus vestibulis orientalis porticus quae medio vestibulo lateribus portarum hinc inde adhaerebant hic ponimus; ut quomodo thalami parietibus adhaererent, vel quomodo ipsum vestibulum in septem sectiones dividerent, sub oculis repraesentemus. 546D

CHAPTER V. On those things which seem to pertain to one half of any portico whatever.

545B

But still, in those two side porches other sound subdivisions are shown to exist, which themselves too are indeed named porches in Scripture. I find there a porch of eight cubits, and I find another of five cubits. The porch of eight cubits I find at the eastern gate of the outer court, where it is read: And he measured the porch of the gate, eight cubits. The porch of five cubits in breadth I find at the southern gate of the inner court. Of which it is written: And he measured the porch round about, 545Cthe length twenty-five cubits, and the breadth five cubits. But above we proved by the authority of Scripture that whatever we grant concerning any one of these porticoes in the matters which pertain to measurement, we ought by an equal reasoning to grant equally concerning them all. Therefore at all the porticoes there were porches of eight cubits, and likewise at all of them it is necessary that porches of five cubits be found. But now let us first look at the position of the little chambers, and then perhaps we shall more easily find the reason concerning these. Now every portico has six little chambers, three on this side and three on that, on either side of the way, which, by Scripture's teaching, we know were situated within the porticoes themselves. Moreover the little chambers of the gate toward the eastern way, 545Dthree on this side and three on that. These things concerning the number of the little chambers, and in what follows concerning their position. And windows, he says, slanting, in the little chambers and in their fronts, which were within the gate on every side round about. If the windows which were in the little chambers were within the gate itself, then the little chambers themselves also were within the gate. For here, after his custom, he calls "gate" the gate itself together with its whole portico. For what he says could otherwise not stand at all. Of the same kind is that: And he measured the gate from the roof of the little chamber unto the roof thereof. Similarly here he designates the portico under the name of the gate. Now it is agreed that each single little chamber with its margin took up eight cubits. For each of them was of six cubits, and had a margin of one cubit not only in front, but also on either side, Scripture 546Aattesting it, which says: And he measured the margin before the little chambers of one cubit, and one cubit the boundary, both. And the little chambers were of six cubits on this side and on that. [Vulg. utrimque, "on both sides"] Now three times eight yield twenty-four, to which 10 being added make 34. And for this reason to the aforesaid twenty-four which we gather from the three little chambers we still add another ten cubits, because we find five cubits between the first little chamber and the second, and five others between the second and the third, according to that which we read: And between the little chambers five cubits. And so there were beyond doubt thirty-four cubits from the beginning of the first little chamber to the end of the third. But they ought to be beside the sides of the gate, three on this side and three on that. But the porches which were at the sides of the gate, which adjoined the middle porch of ten cubits 546Bon this side and on that, held in breadth not more than twenty-five cubits. The breadth of the porches, therefore, could not take in the position of three little chambers, which we showed above to take up thirty-four cubits. But the length of the porches, because it was of fifty cubits, could easily take them in, and more besides. Let three little chambers therefore be placed at one side of the porch, namely in that place where the roof is seen to rest upon the wall, in such a way that they may seem to cleave to the wall itself — nay rather, to have it for one wall of their own, and as it were for one front, where we understand those windows to be of which Scripture speaks when it says: Slanting windows in the little chambers and in their fronts, which were within the gate on every side round about. For apart from the other absurdities which seem 546Cto follow from this, which I judge can easily be understood of themselves, there was no source from which they could have had clear light, unless they adjoined the walls of the porch on this side and on that, where they might have windows to let the light in. We therefore set down here one of the collateral porches of the eastern portico, which adjoined the middle porch at the sides of the gates on this side and on that, so that we may set before the eyes how the little chambers adjoined the walls, or how they divided the porch itself into seven sections. 546D

CAPUT VI. De caeteris quae de porticu restant, ad totius descriptionis completionem.

547A

Hoc facto constat procul dubio inter duas exteriorum thalamorum extremitates triginta quatuor esse cubitos, et adhuc ab utraque parte octo cubitos superesse. Nam constat triginta quatuor et bis octo quinquaginta implere. Hoc itaque expleto, si contra laterales thalamorum margines modico prius muro subtracto, deinde columnis erectis quemadmodum in claustris videmus, quasi tot parietes usque ad medium vestibulum erigimus, unum procul dubio vestibulum in septem dividimus. Ecce quomodo situs thalamorum vestibulum unum in septem dividit; nec tamen ejusdem quantitatis omnia, sed alia majora, 547Balia minora relinquit. Facta itaque hac divisione, quod superius quaesivimus hic possumus invenire, vestibula videlicet alia octo cubitorum in latitudine, alia autem cubitorum quinque, utraque tamen viginti quinque cubitorum in longitudine. Nam duo illa quae inter tres thalamos erant quinque cubitos in latitudine, sed viginti quinque in longitudine habebant. Duo autem illa quae hinc inde ultra thalamos fuerant, octo cubitos in latitudine, viginti quinque autem in longitudine tenebant. Sed et illa tria in quorum extremis tres thalami siti erant, sex tamen cubitis in latum se intra parietes expandebant. Quidquid autem de uno vestibulo ab una parte viae jam diximus, totum hoc de alio ex 547Calia parte viae sentire debemus. Totidem enim thalamos eodem modo sitos habebat: et per totidem paresque partes per omnia divisum erat. Duo itaque vestibula hinc inde in quatuordecim subdividuntur, quibus dum medium adjungitur unius porticus juxta unamquamque portam quindecim subdivisiones numerantur. Post ista quae de porticuum divisione jam diximus, restat hic illud adhuc quaerere, quid de liminibus illis quae Scriptura commemorat sentire debeamus. Tria enim limina Scriptura manifeste designat: et unumquodque illorum portae limen esse denuntiat. Sed non potuit, sicut nec debuit una porta nisi unum limen habere, etsi unum ex his portae muri, et alterum portae vestibuli volumus assignare; restat tamen adhuc de tertio quaerere. Quaeramus 547Ditaque prius quod horum propriae portae dicatur, et quod propriae portae non est cur portae esse asseratur. Sed nulla illarum quatuordecim subdivisionum quas superius ostendimus plusquam octo cubitos in latitudine habuit: nulla ergo illarum limen decem cubitorum latitudinis capere potuit, quarum introitus nonnisi a fronte earum juxta medium vestibulum fuit. Nulli ergo dubium illud limen proprie dici portae quod legitur decem cubitos latitudinis habuisse. Hoc utique Scriptura satis aperte innuit, cum, post descriptionem liminis decem cubitorum, statim de longitudine portae subintulit: Et mensus est latitudinem liminis portae decem cubitorum, et longitudinem portae tredecim cubitorum. Qui enim latitudinem portae ex latitudine liminis designaverat, restabat solam ejus 548Alongitudinem describere quam necdum expresserat. Sed cum una porta ex una parte vestibuli contra aliam ex alia parte erigatur, ita ut una porta videatur, unaque quamplures frequentius dicatur, quid causae, quaeso, erit cur una major, altera autem minor esse credatur? Constat sane duo alia limina nonnisi sex cubitos latitudinis habuisse: quorum unum si volumus alteri portae assignare, convincimur eam alia minorem astruere. Sed si hoc Scriptura sensit, seu nos sentire voluit, cur, quaeso, post descriptionem liminis statim de longitudine non subintulit, sicut de alia fecit? Cur utriusque portae latitudo ex liminum descriptione exprimitur, et alterius tantum longitudine diligenter expressa de alterius longitudine omnino tacetur? Sed tacendo de 548Buna, quod de altera tam diligenter expressit satis evidenter innuit, quia quod de una dixit, totum de alia intelligi voluit. Utraque itaque porta, vel si hoc melius sonat una utrinque porta limen decem cubitorum habebat, et decem cubitorum in latitudine erat et tredecim cubitos in longitudine tenebat. Restat nunc de aliis liminibus quaerere vel quorum sint, vel quae propriae portae non sunt, cur dicantur ejus esse? Sed secundum quod totam porticum portam saepe nominat, quid mirum si ipsius vocat quod intra ipsam erat? Sic sane, si sensum scriptoris cupimus intelligere, oportet procul dubio magis locutionis ejus modos notare et nosse, quam de locutionum proprietate disputare. Limina itaque ista quae sex 548Ccubitorum esse describuntur, quia intra portam erant, portae dicantur; propriae tamen aliquorum vestibulorum quae in novissima subdivisione assignavimus esse creduntur. Manifestum est autem quod illorum quae sex cubitorum erant dici non possint si tota fronte patebant; sin autem, jam omnino limen sex cubitorum capere non poterant. Sed nec illorum esse poterant quae inter thalamos jacebant; quia quinque cubitos tantum in latitudine tenebant, et sex cubitorum limina procul dubio omnino capere non poterant. Relinquitur ergo duo illa praedicta limina illorum vestibulorum fuisse quae superius probavimus in sua latitudine octo cubitos habuisse. Nam ex his quae superius dicuntur duo illa vestibula quae ultra thalamos hinc inde locantur sexdecim 548Dcubitos tenere convincuntur. Quae si quis velit per duo inaequalia dividere, et uni minus, alteri majus tribuere, in tantum ut alterum eorum possit etiam limen decem cubitorum capere, et in hunc modum conetur priorem illam assertionem nostram evacuare, jam profecto neutrum illorum vestibulorum octo cubitorum erit, nec jam inveniri poterit cui Scriptura illa recte aptari possit: Et mensus est vestibulum portae octo cubitorum. Si igitur unum istorum est octo cubitorum, profecto octo cubitorum erit et alterum. Quidquid enim ex sedecim numero uni detrahitur, procul dubio alteri relinquitur. Sed quia sufficienter, ut arbitror, de porticus descriptione jam diximus, et quae de thalamis seu liminibus hoc loco dicenda videbantur ordine prosecuti 549Asumus, liber hoc loco ad majorem dictorum dicendorumque evidentiam, omnium quae superius per partes prolata sunt formam in unam figuram colligere, ut intuentis animus eorum omnium locum, 550Asitum et numerum, qualitatem, quantitatem, proportionem facile possit ex ejus contemplatione colligere. 549

549BSed quia difficile, seu etiam impossibile est longitudinem, et latitudinem, et altitudinem aedificiorum in plano sub eadem figura repraesentare, sufficere arbitror horum omnium quae diximus situm, locumque conformasse, et lineis proportionalibus ductis omnium eorum quasi quoddam fundamentum jecisse. Cum enim lineas proportionali commensuratione modificatas aspicimus, et per eas nihil aliud quam quaedam parietum jacta seu designata fundamenta intelligimus, facile est subintelligere ab hac linearum deductione parietes in altum surgere, ipsisque parietibus tectum incumbere. Si quis me fortassis in hac nostra expositione prolixitatis arguat, videat, obsecro, quam recte id faciat. Sed ut verum fatear malo esse subtilioribus fastidiosus, quam tardioribus obscurus, praesertim in tam obscura re, ubi pene omnia quae dicuntur 550Bprobatione indigent potius quam expositione. Sed jam ad expositionem litterae redeamus, quam ex majori jam parte in his duntaxat quae de porticibus dicuntur planam esse confidimus.

CHAPTER VI. On the remaining things concerning the portico, for the completion of the whole description.

547A

This being done, it is agreed beyond doubt that between the two extremities of the outer little chambers there are thirty-four cubits, and that eight cubits still remain over on either side. For it is agreed that thirty-four and twice eight fill up fifty. And so, this being completed, if over against the side margins of the little chambers — a low wall being first drawn underneath, then columns erected as we see in cloisters — we raise as it were so many walls as far as the middle porch, we without doubt divide one porch into seven. See how the position of the little chambers divides one porch into seven; and yet it does not leave them all of the same size, but some greater, 547Bothers smaller. This division therefore being made, we can find here what we sought above: namely porches, some of eight cubits in breadth, others of five cubits, yet both of twenty-five cubits in length. For those two which were between the three little chambers had five cubits in breadth, but twenty-five in length. But those two which had been beyond the little chambers on this side and on that held eight cubits in breadth, and twenty-five in length. But also those three in whose ends the three little chambers were situated spread themselves in breadth within the walls by six cubits. But whatever we have now said of one porch on one side of the way, all this we ought to think of the other on 547Cthe other side of the way. For it had just as many little chambers situated in the same way, and was divided throughout into just as many and equal parts. And so the two porches on this side and on that are subdivided into fourteen; and when the middle one is joined to them, fifteen subdivisions of one portico are counted at each several gate. After these things which we have now said concerning the division of the porticoes, it remains here still to ask what we ought to think concerning those thresholds which Scripture mentions. For Scripture plainly designates three thresholds, and declares each one of them to be the threshold of the gate. But one gate could not — just as neither ought it — have more than one threshold; and even if we are willing to assign one of these to the gate of the wall and another to the gate of the porch, it still remains to ask concerning the third. Let us therefore first ask 547Dwhich of these is said to be properly the gate's, and, as for that which is not properly the gate's, why it is asserted to be the gate's. But none of those fourteen subdivisions which we showed above had more than eight cubits in breadth; none of them, therefore, could take in a threshold of ten cubits' breadth — subdivisions whose entrance was only from their front, beside the middle porch. To no one, therefore, is it doubtful that that threshold is properly called the gate's which is read to have had ten cubits of breadth. This at any rate Scripture hints plainly enough when, after the description of the threshold of ten cubits, it at once added concerning the length of the gate: And he measured the breadth of the threshold of the gate, ten cubits; and the length of the gate, thirteen cubits. For he who had indicated the breadth of the gate from the breadth of the threshold — it remained for him to describe its 548Alength alone, which he had not yet expressed. But since one gate is raised on one side of the porch over against another on the other side, in such wise that it seems one gate, and quite often several are called one, what reason, I ask, will there be why the one should be believed greater and the other smaller? It is certainly agreed that the two other thresholds had only six cubits of breadth; and if we wish to assign one of them to the other gate, we are convicted of maintaining that it is smaller than the other. But if Scripture meant this, or wished us to think it, why, I ask, did it not at once add concerning the length after the description of the threshold, as it did concerning the other? Why is the breadth of each gate expressed from the description of the thresholds, and — the length of the one alone being carefully expressed — is the length of the other passed over altogether in silence? But by keeping silence concerning 548Bthe one, it hints plainly enough by what it so carefully expressed concerning the other: that what it said of the one it wished to be understood wholly of the other. And so each gate — or, if this sounds better, one gate on both sides — had a threshold of ten cubits, and was of ten cubits in breadth and held thirteen cubits in length. It now remains to ask concerning the other thresholds: whose are they, and, as for those which are not properly the gate's, why are they said to be its? But according as he often names the whole portico "gate," what wonder is it if he calls that its own which was within it? Thus assuredly, if we desire to understand the writer's meaning, we must beyond doubt rather mark and know the modes of his speech than dispute about the propriety of expressions. And so let those thresholds which are described as being of six 548Ccubits be called the gate's, because they were within the gate; yet they are believed to be properly of certain of those porches which we assigned in the last subdivision. But it is manifest that they cannot be said to be of those which were of six cubits, if these lay open with their whole front; but if not, then they could by no means at all take in a threshold of six cubits. But neither could they be of those which lay between the little chambers, because these held only five cubits in breadth, and beyond doubt could not at all take in thresholds of six cubits. It remains, therefore, that those two aforesaid thresholds belonged to those porches which we proved above to have eight cubits in their breadth. For from those things which are said above, those two porches which are placed beyond the little chambers on this side and on that are proved to hold sixteen 548Dcubits. And if anyone should wish to divide these into two unequal parts, and to assign less to the one and more to the other, so far that one of them might even take in a threshold of ten cubits, and should in this manner attempt to void that earlier assertion of ours — then assuredly neither of those porches will be of eight cubits, nor will there any longer be able to be found one to which that saying of Scripture can rightly be fitted: And he measured the porch of the gate, eight cubits. If therefore one of these is of eight cubits, assuredly the other also will be of eight cubits. For whatever is taken away from the number sixteen for the one is beyond doubt left to the other. But since we have now spoken sufficiently, as I judge, concerning the description of the portico, and have pursued in order those things which seemed to be said in this place concerning the little chambers or the thresholds, I am free 549Ain this place, for the greater clearness of the things said and of the things to be said, to gather into one figure the form of all those which above were brought forth part by part, so that the mind of the beholder may easily be able, from the contemplation of it, to gather the place, 550Aposition and number, the quality, quantity and proportion of them all. 549

549BBut since it is difficult, or even impossible, to represent the length and the breadth and the height of buildings on a flat surface under one and the same figure, I judge it sufficient to have shaped the position and place of all these things which we have said, and, proportional lines being drawn, to have laid as it were a kind of foundation of them all. For when we look upon lines adjusted by a proportional commensuration, and understand by them nothing else than certain laid or marked-out foundations of walls, it is easy to understand besides that from this drawing out of lines walls rise on high, and that a roof rests upon those same walls. If anyone should perhaps accuse me of prolixity in this our exposition, let him see, I beg, how rightly he does so. But, that I may confess the truth, I had rather be wearisome to the more subtle than obscure to the slower, especially in so obscure a matter, where nearly all the things that are said 550Bneed proof rather than exposition. But let us now return to the exposition of the letter, which for the greater part now — at least in those things which are said concerning the porticoes — we trust to be plain.

CAPUT VII. Expositio litterae super descriptionem orientalis portae.

Et mensus est, inquit, limen portae calamo uno, latitudinem (id est limen unum), calamo uno in latitudine. Sicut superius ostendimus, quamvis hoc limen proprium portae non fuerit, idcirco tamen illud portae esse dicit, quia intra ipsam ejus porticum fuit, quam etiam saepe sub nomine portae designare consuevit. Quam autem vir ille qui portam ingreditur hoc limen primo loco, et ante thalamos metitur, 551Apatenter, ut arbitror, Scriptura innuit quod in primo praedictorum VII vestibulorum invenitur. Sed cum prius dictum sit Mensus est limen calamo uno latitudinem, quid est quod exponendo, vel quasi corrigendo quod quasi improprie dixerat, statim subintulit, id est, limen unum in latitudine? Sed diligentius consideranti quid aliud videtur esse haec expositio, vel correctio sententiae, nisi quaedam declaratio propheticae intelligentiae, ne nos lateret quid propheta dicere voluisset? Ac si aperte dicat se limen improprie quidem portae limen appellasse, sed quoddam limen seu unum limen dicere debuisse. Quod ergo secundum aliquem modum limen hoc dici possit portae, monet per primam expositionem, quod propriae dictum non sit portae, docet per subjunctam 551Bexpositionem. Quod ergo dicitur unum, permonet et alia limina ibi esse, nec hoc proprie dici portae. Quod autem latitudinem bis ponit per inculcationem attentius commendare voluit, ne quis quod de hoc limine, et post de decem cubitorum limine dicitur ad idem limen referre velit, cum utrobique latitudinem dici invenerit. Si tamen longitudo non latitudo poneretur, fortassis magis propriae dictum videretur. Denique ubi nos habemus latitudinem lxx, teste Hieronymo, posuerunt longitudinem. Sed saepe latitudo ponitur, ut non quantitatis speciem, sed simpliciter quantitatem significare videatur. Sequitur:

Et thalamum uno calamo in longum, et uno calamo 551Cin latum, et inter thalamos quinque cubitos, in quibus verbis repetendum est mensus est. Attende nunc quam competenti ordine incedit, et quomodo paulatim, et per partes scientiae semina spargit. Primo de limine dicit quod erat in primo vestibulo, postea de mensura thalami qui erat in secundo, deinde vero de mensura spatii quod erat inter thalamos addit, in quo jam quod plures sint ostendit, novissime autem ad ultimum vestibulum venit quod ultra omnes thalamos situm fuit, cujus limen prius, et postmodum ispius vestibuli mensuram describit, cum dicit, et limen portae juxta vestibulum portae intrinsecus calamo uno. Et mensus est vestibulum portae octo cubitorum, et frontem ejus duobus cubitis. Quod autem hoc limen item portae dicitur, eodem 551Dhic modo quo et superius accipitur. Manifeste autem determinat ubi hoc limen fuerit, juxta illud videlicet vestibulum quod intrinsecus erat, et inter duo circumadjacentia vestibula medium jacebat. Ex hoc loco, ut arbitror, manifeste ostenditur quod frontes minimorum vestibulorum medio vestibulo jungebantur.

Notandum sane quod postquam hoc limen, quemadmodum superius, portae limen nominavit, statim per subjunctam determinationem ostendit quod portae limen proprie dictum non sit. Si enim hoc limen esse portae limen proprie dictum fuisset, procul dubio ubi esset per determinationem subjungere non oporteret. Modo autem ubi fuerit, determinatur in eo quod dicitur, juxta vestibulum portae intrinsecus. 552AErat itaque illud limen cujus mensuram capiebat juxta illud vestibulum portae quod intrinsecus erat, et in triplici porticu medium locum tenebat. Post mensuram liminis, mensuram ipsius vestibuli adnectit, cum dicit: Et mensus est vestibulum portae octo cubitorum, et frontem ejus duobus cubitis. Haec utique mensura medio vestibulo convenire non poterat. Nihil aliud enim medium vestibulum erat, nisi transitus, seu via quae de porta ad portam per medium porticum patebat. Longitudinem itaque ejus secundum latitudinem totius porticus quinquaginta cubitorum esse oportuit. Latitudo autem non minor quam latitudo portarum esse potuit, quae procul dubio, ut postea liquebit, X cubitorum fuit. Liquet ergo quod praedicta mensura ejus 552Bvestibuli erat cujus limen prius descripserat. Sed cum vestibuli mensura sumitur, quid, quaeso, est, quod cujus sit longitudinis videlicet an latitudinis non determinatur? Sed solet saepe sine determinatione relinquere quod praevidet prudentem lectorem ex aliis circumstantiis per se posse intelligere. Postquam enim constiterit duo collateralia triplicis porticus vestibula hinc inde quinquagina cubitos longitudinis, et viginti quinque cubitos latitudinis habere, palam perpendi poterit minima vestibula viginti quinque cubitorum longitudinis fuisse. Nam minima vestibula nihil aliud quam maximorum vestibulorum quasdam sectiones per transversum oportet intelligere. Latitudo itaque ultimi vestibuli 552Cdescribitur in eo quod dicitur, Et mensus est vestibulum portae octo cubitorum. Illud autem notandum quod pene quidquid hucusque de mensura est disputatum, determinare videtur latitudinem minimorum vestibulorum. Ubi autem nos habemus, et frontem ejus duobus cubitis, Hieronymus rursus aliam litteram ponit, et in fronte ejusdem, seu in marginibus duos cubitos. Ex quibus verbis manifeste colligitur, quod illi duo cubiti hoc loco designant, qui ostio sex cubitorum unus hinc et alius inde in marginibus superesse videbantur. Nam, si constat vestibulum octo cubitos latitudinis habere, necesse est ejus ostio sex cubitorum latitudinis duos cubitos in fronte superesse. Sed in duobus cubitis frontis secundum nostram litteram forsitan dat melius intelligi illud 552Dspatium quod supra ipsam portam fuit usque ad tabulatum supereminentis coenaculi, et secundum hoc quod dicitur ejus, non ad vestibulum, sed ad portam debet referri: nam quomodo haec sententia ad totius altitudinem complendam valeat, in sequentibus patebit. Quod autem sequitur Vestibulum portae erat intrinsecus, ad totam porticum referre debemus. Haec itaque porticus intrinsecus erat, quia intra murum cui adhaerebat sita erat. Sequitur:

Porro thalami portae ad viam orientalem tres hinc, et tres inde. Mensura una trium, et mensura una frontium ex utraque parte. Ecce quomodo paulatim se promovet. Thalamorum numerum determinat de quo nondum constabat, et ad unius thalami mensuram qui superius describitur trium mensura subinfertur. 553ASed quia jam constat numerus thalamorum, constat aeque etiam de numero vestibulorum, sed nondum constat de mensura omnium. Situs namque thalamorum (secundum quod superius patuit) unum latus porticus in VII sectiones dividit quas Scriptura vestibula dicere consuevit, quarum ultimam jam superius vestibulum nominavit. Duo siquidem quinque cubitorum spatia tres thalami inter se incluserant, et duo alia ultra se seclusa reliquerant, et ipsi tres tria tenebant, quae omnia in septenarium redundant. Post ista limen ipsius portae describitur cum dicitur: Et mensus est latitudinem liminis portae X cubitorum, et longitudinem portae XIII cubitorum. Ecce limen hoc ut superiora portae limen dicitur, et tamen hic, ut in superioribus, nihil determinationis 553Badjungitur, et ex eo ipso innuitur quod vere et proprie portae dicatur. Quod autem vere et proprie portae dictum sit, facile convinci poterit, cum omnium vestibulorum mensura constiterit. Sed omnium sane mensura facile convincitur ex eo quod illico subinfertur: Et marginem ante thalamos cubiti unius, et cubitus unus finis utrinque. Thalami autem sex cubitorum erant hinc et inde. Quod dicit marginem cubiti unius ante thalamos fuisse, et cubitum unum finem utrinque, debemus sub eodem sensu accipere scilicet marginem cubiti unius non solum in thalamorum fronte fuisse, sed etiam in utroque latere. Nihil autem aliud intelligimus hunc marginem circa thalamum, nisi ejecturam quamdam muri, quemadmodum fieri solet vel ad sedendum, 553Cvel ad muri firmamentum. Saepe etenim paries in inferioribus latior fundatur, et cum paululum elevatus fuerit iterum restringitur. Si igitur thalami sex cubitorum erant, et adhuc cubitum unum utrinque sicut et ante in margine usque ad finem habebant, ergo a fine usque ad finem thalamorum octo cubiti erant. Tres itaque thalami ter octo, id est XXIV, cubitos occupabant, et inter primum et secundum thalamum V cubiti, et item inter secundum et tertium alii V erant, qui superioribus adjuncti XXXIV efficiunt. Sed et ultimum vestibulum octo cubitorum legitur, qui numerus si superiori adjungitur usque ad XLII aggregationis summa extenditur. Octonarius itaque qui adhuc superest de quinquaginta primo 553Dvestibulo remanet. Nam tota porticus a porta usque ad frontem vestibuli sicut postea legitur quinquaginta cubitos habet. Nullum ergo hujusmodi vestibularum limen X cubitorum capere potuit. Patet ergo quia proprie portae fuerit. Sed ex eo etiam, sicut superius dictum est, innuitur quod post mensuram liminis in qua et latitudo portae continetur, statim de ejus longitudine subinfertur: Et mensus est, inquit, latitudinem liminis portae X cubitorum, et longitudinem portae XIII cubitorum.

Notandum quomodo post dimensionem thalamorum, vestibulorum, liminumque eorum hujus portae describit mensuram, ex quo intelligi datur quod haec quam metitur interior porta fuerit. Vide ergo quam artificiose primae portae mensura de qua nihil 554Adicitur, ex hujus portae descriptione docetur. Ratio siquidem non sinit credere exteriorem portam interiore esse minorem, nec Scripturae auctoritas patitur eam esse latiorem. A lateribus namque portarum, et a lateribus medii vestibuli ubi frontes scilicet minimorum vestibulorum medio vestibulo adhaerebant, usque ad geminos hinc inde parietes totius porticus XXV cubiti erant. Vestibula namque illa quae hinc inde lateribus portarum medioque vestibulo adhaerebant, ut ex caeterarum porticuum descriptione plenius deprehenditur, XXV cubitos latitudine tantummodo habebant. Si igitur bis XXV cubitis hinc inde sumptis X cubitos (latitudinem scilicet portarum) adjungimus, procul dubio mensuram frontium totius porticus LX videlicet cubitos implemus. Si 554Bigitur frontes hinc inde tantummodo LX cubitos habuerunt, portae plus quam X cubitos omnino habere non poterunt. Superius ostendimus quam ordinate incederet dum unum latus per partes describeret donec ad interiorem portam ex ordine veniret. Descripto uno latere, breviter subinfert unam eamdemque mensuram esse thalamorum, tam hinc quam inde, ut compendiose doceret quidquid de uno latere dicitur de alio debere intelligere. Hinc est quod in aliarum porticuum descriptionibus dum brevitati studet, unum ex collateralibus vestibulis quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinis, et viginti quinque cubitorum latitudinis diligenter describit, et de alio tacite praeteriens eadem subintelligenda relinquit.

CHAPTER VII. Exposition of the letter upon the description of the eastern gate.

And he measured, he says, the threshold of the gate by one reed, the breadth (that is, one threshold), by one reed in breadth. As we showed above, although this threshold was not the gate's own, yet he says that it is the gate's for this reason, that it was within its portico, which also he was often accustomed to designate under the name of the gate. But how plainly, as I judge, Scripture hints that that man who enters the gate measures this threshold in the first place, and before the little chambers, 551Ain that it is found in the first of the aforesaid VII porches. But since it was first said, He measured the threshold by one reed, the breadth, what is it that, by expounding, or as it were by correcting what he had as it were improperly said, he at once subjoined: that is, one threshold in breadth? But to one considering more diligently, what else does this exposition or correction of the sentence seem to be, save a certain declaration of the prophetic understanding, lest it should escape us what the prophet had wished to say? As if he were openly saying that he had indeed improperly called the threshold the threshold of the gate, but ought to have said "a certain threshold" or "one threshold." That therefore this threshold can in some manner be called the gate's, he advises by the first expression; that it is not properly called the gate's, he teaches by the appended 551Bexposition. In that therefore "one" is said, it warns both that there are other thresholds there, and that this is not properly called the gate's. But in that he sets down "breadth" twice, he wished by the driving of it home to commend it more attentively, lest anyone should wish to refer what is said of this threshold, and afterward of the threshold of ten cubits, to the same threshold, when he had found "breadth" said in both places. If however "length," not "breadth," were set down, perhaps it would seem more properly said. In fine, where we have "breadth," the lxx, Jerome being witness, put "length." But often "breadth" is set down so that it seems to signify not the kind of quantity, but simply quantity. There follows:

And the little chamber, one reed in length, and one reed 551Cin breadth, and between the little chambers five cubits, in which words he measured is to be repeated. Mark now with how fitting an order he proceeds, and how little by little and part by part he scatters the seeds of knowledge. First he speaks of the threshold which was in the first porch; afterward of the measure of the little chamber which was in the second; then indeed he adds concerning the measure of the space which was between the little chambers, in which he now shows that there are several of them; and last of all he comes to the last porch, which was situated beyond all the little chambers, whose threshold first, and afterward the measure of ispius porch, he describes, when he says: and the threshold of the gate beside the porch of the gate within, one reed. And he measured the porch of the gate, eight cubits, and its front, two cubits. But that this threshold likewise is called the gate's, is here taken in the same 551Dway as above. But he plainly determines where this threshold was: namely beside that porch which was within, and lay in the middle between the two porches lying about it. From this passage, as I judge, it is plainly shown that the fronts of the smallest porches were joined to the middle porch.

It must indeed be noted that, after he had named this threshold the threshold of the gate, just as above, he at once showed by the appended determination that it is not properly called the threshold of the gate. For if this threshold had been properly called the gate's threshold, beyond doubt it would not have been needful to subjoin by a determination where it was. But now where it was is determined in that which is said, beside the porch of the gate within. 552AAnd so that threshold whose measure he was taking was beside that porch of the gate which was within, and held the middle place in the triple portico. After the measure of the threshold he joins on the measure of the porch itself, when he says: And he measured the porch of the gate, eight cubits, and its front, two cubits. This measure at any rate could not suit the middle porch. For the middle porch was nothing else than a passage, or way, which lay open from gate to gate through the middle of the portico. And so its length, according to the breadth of the whole portico, had to be of fifty cubits. But its breadth could not be less than the breadth of the gates, which beyond doubt, as will afterward be clear, was of X cubits. It is clear therefore that the aforesaid measure was of that 552Bporch whose threshold he had first described. But when the measure of the porch is taken, what is it, I ask, that it is not determined of which it is — of length, namely, or of breadth? But he is often wont to leave without determination what he foresees that a prudent reader can of himself understand from other circumstances. For after it has been established that the two collateral porches of the triple portico on this side and on that have quinquagina cubits of length and twenty-five cubits of breadth, it will be able to be plainly weighed that the smallest porches were of twenty-five cubits in length. For it behooves us to understand the smallest porches as nothing else than certain crosswise sections of the largest porches. And so the breadth of the last porch 552Cis described in that which is said: And he measured the porch of the gate, eight cubits. But this must be noted, that almost whatever has been disputed up to this point concerning measure seems to determine the breadth of the smallest porches. But where we have, and its front, two cubits, Jerome again sets down another reading: and in the front of the same, or in the margins, two cubits. From which words it is plainly gathered what those two cubits designate in this place — the two which seemed to remain over in the margins to a doorway of six cubits, one on this side and the other on that. For if it is agreed that the porch has eight cubits of breadth, it is necessary that for its doorway of six cubits' breadth two cubits remain over in the front. But in the two cubits of the front, according to our reading, he perhaps gives us better to understand that 552Dspace which was above the gate itself up to the planking of the overhanging upper chamber; and according to this, what is said, "its," ought to be referred not to the porch, but to the gate: for how this sentence avails for completing the height of the whole will appear in what follows. But what follows, The porch of the gate was within, we ought to refer to the whole portico. And so this portico was within, because it was situated within the wall to which it adjoined. There follows:

Moreover the little chambers of the gate toward the eastern way, three on this side and three on that. One measure of the three, and one measure of the fronts on either side. See how little by little he advances. He determines the number of the little chambers, concerning which it was not yet established; and to the measure of the one little chamber which is described above, the measure of the three is subjoined. 553ABut because the number of the little chambers is now established, it is equally established also concerning the number of the porches; but it is not yet established concerning the measure of them all. For the position of the little chambers (according as appeared above) divides one side of the portico into VII sections, which Scripture is wont to call porches, the last of which it has already named a porch above. For the three little chambers had shut in between them two spaces of five cubits, and had left two others shut off beyond them, and the three themselves held three; all which run up into the number seven. After these things the threshold of the gate itself is described, when it is said: And he measured the breadth of the threshold of the gate, X cubits, and the length of the gate, XIII cubits. Behold, this threshold, like the foregoing ones, is called the threshold of the gate, and yet here, as in the foregoing, nothing of determination 553Bis added; and from that very fact it is hinted that it is truly and properly called the gate's. But that it is truly and properly called the gate's will be able to be easily proved when the measure of all the porches has been established. But the measure of them all is indeed easily proved from that which is at once subjoined: And the margin before the little chambers of one cubit, and one cubit the boundary on both sides. And the little chambers were of six cubits on this side and on that. In that he says that there was a margin of one cubit before the little chambers, and one cubit the boundary on both sides, we ought to take it under the same sense: namely, that the margin of one cubit was not only in the front of the little chambers, but also on either side. But we understand this margin about the little chamber as nothing else than a certain projection of the wall, such as is wont to be made either for sitting on, 553Cor for the strengthening of the wall. For often a wall is founded broader in its lower parts, and when it has been raised a little it is drawn in again. If therefore the little chambers were of six cubits, and had still one cubit on both sides, as also in front, in the margin up to the boundary, then from boundary to boundary of the little chambers there were eight cubits. And so three little chambers took up three times eight, that is XXIV, cubits; and between the first and the second little chamber there were V cubits, and likewise between the second and the third another V, which, added to the foregoing, make XXXIV. But the last porch also is read to be of eight cubits, which number, if it is added to the former, the sum of the aggregation extends up to XLII. And so the eight which still remains over out of fifty remains for the first 553Dporch. For the whole portico from the gate to the front of the porch, as is afterward read, has fifty cubits. None of porches of this kind, therefore, could take in a threshold of X cubits. It is clear therefore that it was properly the gate's. But it is hinted also from this, as was said above, that after the measure of the threshold, in which the breadth of the gate also is contained, there is at once subjoined concerning its length: And he measured, he says, the breadth of the threshold of the gate, X cubits, and the length of the gate, XIII cubits.

It must be noted how, after the measurement of the little chambers, of the porches, and of their thresholds, he describes the measure of this gate; from which it is given to be understood that this which he measures was the inner gate. See therefore how skilfully the measure of the first gate, concerning which nothing 554Ais said, is taught from the description of this gate. For reason does not allow us to believe that the outer gate is smaller than the inner, nor does the authority of Scripture suffer it to be broader. For from the sides of the gates, and from the sides of the middle porch — where, namely, the fronts of the smallest porches adjoined the middle porch — up to the twin walls of the whole portico on this side and on that, there were XXV cubits. For those porches which on this side and on that adjoined the sides of the gates and the middle porch, as is more fully grasped from the description of the other porticoes, had only XXV cubits in breadth. If therefore, twice XXV cubits being taken on this side and on that, we add X cubits (namely the breadth of the gates), we without doubt fill up the measure of the fronts of the whole portico, namely LX cubits. If 554Btherefore the fronts on this side and on that had only LX cubits, the gates will not be able to have more than X cubits at all. Above we showed how orderly he proceeded while he described one side part by part until he came in order to the inner gate. One side being described, he briefly subjoins that the measure of the little chambers is one and the same, both on this side and on that, that he might teach compendiously that whatever is said of one side ought to be understood of the other. Hence it is that in the descriptions of the other porticoes, while he studies brevity, he carefully describes one of the collateral porches, of fifty cubits in length and of twenty-five cubits in breadth, and, passing over the other in silence, leaves the same things to be understood of it.

CAPUT VIII. De his quae pertinent ad descriptionem totius.

554C

Post partium descriptionem, tria sub una continuatione subinferuntur, in quibus mensura totius breviter comprehenditur, atque completur. Primum pertinet ad altitudinem, secundum complectitur totius porticus longitudinem, tertium totius latitudinem. Ad altitudinem pertinet quod dicitur: Et mensus est a tecto thalami usque ad tectum ejus latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum, portam ostium contra ostium. Ad longitudinem pertinet quod sequitur: Et fecit frontes per sexaginta cubitos. Ad latitudinem quod novissime ponitur: Et ante faciem portae quae 554Dpertingebat usque ad faciem vestibuli portae interioris, quinquaginta cubitos. Quomodo autem ex primo capitulo totius altitudo animadverti possit, ex novissimis hujus descriptionis plenius patebit. Per omnes autem coenacula seu solaria esse credimus, sicut in caeteris domorum porticibus, seu caeteris aedificiis pene ubique invenimus. Nihil itaque aliud intelligimus tectum thalami, nisi pavimentum solarii, ita ut idem tabulatum esset et thalami tectum, et coenaculi pavimentum, et forte secundum morem terrae Palaestinorum debemus intelligere tectum portae planum esse, non erectum. Erat itaque superius coenaculum altitudinis viginti quinque cubitorum, secundum altitudinem tecti ad tectum. Sed nota quod latitudinem posuit ubi altitudinem ponere debuit, 555Ausus enim latitudinis nomine saepe abuti solet: nam, cum sint tres dimensionis species, longitudo, latitudo et altitudo, sic saepe latitudo ponitur ut solum spatii quantitatem, non autem aliquam dimensionis speciem determinare videatur. Latitudinem itaque hoc loco improprie posuit, quemadmodum superius ubi limina descripsit. Quod autem dicitur ostium contra ostium, defectiva oratio est, sed habentem, sive aliquid ejusmodi subaudiri potest. Mensus est itaque portam habentem in ipsis thalamis ostium contra ostium, quia ostia trium thalamorum ab uno latere erant opposita ostiis trium aliorum. Quod autem frontes ponitur in eo quod subsequenter adjungitur: Et fecit frontes per sexaginta cubitos, ad geminam porticus faciem refertur, unam qua ad 555Binteriora, alia qua ad exteriora respiciebat. De interiori autem ejus fronte intelligit quod subsequenter adjungitur: Et ad frontem atrium portae undique per circuitum. Hoc itaque pavimentum, tria porticus latera ambiebat, sicut via altrinsecus jacens evidenter ostendit circa quod situs gazophylaciorum ordinatus fuit.

Notandum sane quod ex mensura frontium LX cubitorum intra parietes sumitur quemadmodum ex aliis locis facile colligitur, sicut ex eo loco ubi vestibulum XXV cubitorum longitudinis, et V cubitorum latitudinis describitur, sicut ibi monstrabitur. Debemus vel de mensura latitudinis sentire, intra parietes videlicet sumptam esse. In eo autem quod 555Cdictum est, et fecit frontes per LX cubitos, determinata est totius porticus longitudo, nunc autem in eo quod subinfertur, determinatur ejus latitudo, et ante faciem portae quae pertingebat usque ad faciem vestibuli portae interioris quinquaginta cubitos. Faciem portae, interiorem muri superficiem vocat illam videlicet quam ad se habebat qui intus erat, et mensuram capiebat. Mensus est ergo ab interiori parte non ab exteriori, quia si ab exteriori mensuram caperet, tanto plus L cubitis inveniret quantum spissitudinis murus tenebat. Faciem autem vestibuli portae interioris dicit illam partem vestibuli quam ad templum habuit, et portae interiori cohaesit. In eo autem porta usque ad faciem vestibuli pertingebat, quod porticum suam usque in id loci 555Dextendebat. Quod autem usque ad faciem vestibuli dicitur, ad duo respondere videtur, et usquequo videlicet porta pertingeret, et quousque spatium quinquaginta cubitorum se extenderet. Fecit ergo, sive mensus est ante faciem portae quae pertingebat usque ad faciem vestibuli portae interioris quinquaginta cubitos. Sequitur:

Et fenestras obliquas in thalamis, et in frontibus eorum quae erant intra portam undique per circuitum fecit a superioribus. In his verbis repetitur, fecit ergo fenestras obliquas, id est interius latiores, et exterius angustiores. Fecit eas in thalamis et in frontibus eorum, hoc est maxime in frontibus, quae fenestrae intra portam, hoc est intra porticum erant, quia thalami in quibus ipse fenestrae erant intra ipsam 556Aporticum consistebant. Nam de fenestris, quae ipsorum vestibulorum non autem thalamorum fuere, curavit consequenter adjungere: Similiter autem, inquit, erant et in vestibulis fenestrae per gyrum intrinsecus, ubi et additur: et ante frontes pictura palmarum, quod expositione non eget.

Notandum quod dicit pluraliter in vestibulis et intrinsecus; puto autem quod ex hoc loco facile innotescat, quod totam porticum sub portae appellatione quandoque comprehendat. Quamvis enim porta decem cubitorum unum thalamum facile comprehenderet, plures tamen comprehendere minime posset ut de eo dici debuisset: Et fenestras obliquas in thalamis quae erant intra portam. Sequitur:

Eduxit me ad atrium exterius. Ad interiora quidem 556Bdicitur, et tamen educitur qui porticum in qua prius erat egreditur. Educitur autem ad atrium exterius, quia erat et alius interius, et ecce gazophylacia, et pavimentum stratum lapide in atrio undique per circuitum. Triginta gazophylacia in circuitu pavimenti. De his interim gazophylaciis nihil aliud volumus dicere, nisi quod decem et quinque erant hinc, et decem et quinque erant inde. Pavimentum autem hoc habebat XV gazophylacia ab Aquilone, et decem et quinque a Meridie. Porticum atrii exterioris ab Oriente, porticum atrii interioris ab Occidente, unde est illud quod postea additur: Et pavimentum in fronte portarum secundum longitudinem portarum erat inferius, ideo autem inferius, quia portae, sive ipsa porticus 556Cmagna proceritate supra illud attollebantur sursum, et illud in imo inter illas jacebat deorsum. Ex hoc item loco patet in quam partem longitudo porticuum accipi debeat. Nam, si pavimentum erat in fronte portarum secundum longitudinem earum, constat quia longitudo porticuum erat quae se extendebat juxta pavimentum. Mensuram pavimenti deinceps determinat quam nondum expresserat: Et mensus est latitudinem a facie portae inferioris usque ad frontem atrii interioris extrinsecus centum cubitos ad Orientem, et ad Aquilonem. Portam inferiorem vocat portam atrii exterioris; nam in loco eminentiori erat porta atrii interioris. Aedificium siquidem supra montem excelsum nimis constitutum erat, sicut 556Din ipso descriptionis exordio Scriptura commemorat. Portae ergo quae erant exteriores, et ipsae inferiores, et quae erant interiores, eaedem et superiores. Per faciem itaque portae inferioris vult intelligi frontem vestibuli exterioris, per frontem atrii interioris extrinsecus faciem exteriorem superioris porticus intelligere debemus. Nam interior porticus convertebat faciem suam ad exteriora, et exterior nihilominus convertebat suam ad interiora

Notandum sane ex hoc loco quod locum in quo interiores porticus erant, nunc atrio interiori, nunc exteriori attribuat. Unde et in inferioribus de eadem porticu seu vestibulo dicet: Et vestibulum ejus, id est atrii exterioris. Erat autem ab Oriente porta inferior, ab Occidente vero porta interior. Quomodo 557Aergo mensus est a facie portae inferioris usque ad frontem atrii interioris centum cubitos ad Orientem, cum hoc sit ab Oriente in Occidentem, non ab Occidente versus Orientem? Quapropter distinctio ibi est facienda, et mensus est latitudinem a facie portae inferioris usque ad frontem interioris extrinsecus. Ibique alia sententia inchoanda, et a superioribus quidem mensus est, repetendum est ita: Mensus est autem centum cubitos ad Orientem, et ad Aquilonem, hoc est centum cubitos ab Occidente versus Orientem, et similiter centum cubitos a Meridie versus Aquilonem; vel quod dicit ad Orientem, et ad Aquilonem, sic intelligere possumus: Ad Orientem, id est ad orientalem plagam, mensus est centum cubitos inter portam et portam. Et ad Aquilonem, id 557Best aquilonarem plagam, similiter mensus est centum cubitos inter portam et portam, ita ut hoc ultimum dictum sit, per anticipationem. Jam sufficienter, ut arbitror, hucusque de unius porticus descriptione dictum est, et quod de reliquis sequitur, sine expositione pene per omnia ex praedictis facile intelligi potest. Sciat autem qui haec legerit omnia pene quae de porticibus disseruimus ex Scripturae auctoritate posse convinci, et multa a nobis etiam potuisse ampliore ratione probari, quae sponte praeterimus, dum fastidium vitamus.

CHAPTER VIII. On those things which pertain to the description of the whole.

554C

After the description of the parts, three things are subjoined under one continuation, in which the measure of the whole is briefly comprehended and completed. The first pertains to the height, the second embraces the length of the whole portico, the third the breadth of the whole. To the height pertains that which is said: And he measured from the roof of the little chamber unto the roof thereof, a breadth of twenty-five cubits, the gate, door over against door. To the length pertains what follows: And he made fronts of sixty cubits. To the breadth, what is set down last: And before the face of the gate which 554Dreached even unto the face of the porch of the inner gate, fifty cubits. But how from the first clause the height of the whole may be observed will appear more fully from the last parts of this description. And we believe that throughout them all there were upper chambers or lofts, as we find almost everywhere in the other porticoes of houses, or in other buildings. And so we understand the roof of the little chamber as nothing else than the floor of the loft, so that the same planking was both the roof of the little chamber and the floor of the upper chamber; and perhaps, according to the custom of the land of the Palestinians, we ought to understand that the roof of the gate was flat, not raised. And so the upper chamber above was of a height of twenty-five cubits, according to the height from roof to roof. But note that he set down "breadth" where he ought to have set down "height"; 555Afor in his usage he is often wont to misuse the name of breadth: for since there are three kinds of dimension — length, breadth, and height — breadth is often so set down that it seems to determine only the quantity of the space, and not any kind of dimension. And so in this place he set down "breadth" improperly, just as above where he described the thresholds. But in that it is said, door over against door, the expression is defective; but "having," or something of that kind, can be supplied. And so he measured the gate having in the little chambers themselves door over against door, because the doors of the three little chambers on one side were set opposite to the doors of the three others. But in that "fronts" is set down in what is subsequently added — And he made fronts of sixty cubits — it is referred to the twin face of the portico: the one by which it looked toward 555Bthe inner parts, the other by which it looked toward the outer. But of its inner front he understands what is subsequently added: And at the front, the court of the gate on every side round about. And so this pavement went about three sides of the portico, as the way lying on both sides evidently shows, about which the position of the chambers was ordered.

It must certainly be noted that the measure of the fronts of LX cubits is taken from within the walls, as is easily gathered from other places — as from that place where a porch of XXV cubits in length and of V cubits in breadth is described, as will be shown there. We ought also to think concerning the measure of the breadth, namely, that it was taken within the walls. But in that 555Cwhich was said, and he made fronts of LX cubits, the length of the whole portico was determined; but now in that which is subjoined its breadth is determined: and before the face of the gate which reached unto the face of the porch of the inner gate, fifty cubits. By "the face of the gate" he calls the inner surface of the wall, namely that which he had toward himself who was within and was taking the measure. He measured therefore from the inner side, not from the outer, because if he were taking the measure from the outer he would find so much more than L cubits as the wall held in thickness. But by "the face of the porch of the inner gate" he means that part of the porch which it had toward the temple, and which clung to the inner gate. But the gate reached unto the face of the porch in this, that it extended its portico as far as 555Dthat place. But in that it is said "unto the face of the porch," it seems to answer to two things: namely, how far the gate reached, and how far the space of fifty cubits extended itself. He made therefore, or measured, before the face of the gate which reached unto the face of the porch of the inner gate, fifty cubits. There follows:

And slanting windows in the little chambers, and in their fronts, which were within the gate on every side round about, he made from the upper parts. In these words it is repeated: he made, therefore, slanting windows, that is, broader within and narrower without. He made them in the little chambers and in their fronts, that is, chiefly in the fronts — which windows were within the gate, that is, within the portico, because the little chambers in which those same windows were stood within the 556Aportico itself. For concerning the windows which were of the porches themselves and not of the little chambers, he took care to add consequently: And likewise, he says, there were windows also in the porches round about within, where also is added: and before the fronts a painting of palm trees, which needs no exposition.

It must be noted that he says in the plural "in the porches," and "within"; but I think that from this place it easily becomes known that he sometimes comprehends the whole portico under the appellation of the gate. For although a gate of ten cubits might easily comprehend one little chamber, yet it could by no means comprehend several, so that it should have had to be said of it: And slanting windows in the little chambers which were within the gate. There follows:

He led me out to the outer court. It is said toward the inner parts indeed, 556Band yet he is led out who goes forth from the portico in which he was before. But he is led out to the outer court, because there was another also, the inner, and behold, chambers, and a pavement laid with stone in the court on every side round about. Thirty chambers round about the pavement. Concerning these chambers we wish for the present to say nothing else, save that fifteen were on this side and fifteen were on that. But this pavement had XV chambers on the north, and fifteen on the south.
