The portico of the outer court on the east, the portico of the inner court on the west; whence is that which is afterward added: And the pavement at the front of the gates, according to the length of the gates, was lower, and lower for this reason, that the gates, or the portico 556Citself, were raised up above it with great loftiness, and it lay down below in the depth between them. From this place likewise it is clear in which direction the length of the porticoes ought to be taken. For if the pavement was at the front of the gates according to their length, it is agreed that the length of the porticoes was that which extended itself beside the pavement. Next he determines the measure of the pavement, which he had not yet expressed: And he measured the breadth from the face of the lower gate unto the front of the inner court on the outside, a hundred cubits toward the east and toward the north. He calls the gate of the outer court the lower gate; for the gate of the inner court was in a more eminent place. For the building had been set upon an exceedingly high mountain, as 556DScripture mentions in the very beginning of the description. The gates, therefore, which were outer were themselves also lower; and those which were inner, the same were also upper. And so by "the face of the lower gate" he wishes the front of the outer porch to be understood; by "the front of the inner court on the outside" we ought to understand the outer face of the upper portico. For the inner portico turned its face toward the outer parts, and the outer none the less turned its own toward the inner parts

It must certainly be noted from this place that he attributes the place in which the inner porticoes were now to the inner court, now to the outer. Whence also in what follows he will say of the same portico or porch: And its porch, that is, of the outer court. But on the east was the lower gate, and on the west the inner gate. How 557Atherefore did he measure from the face of the lower gate unto the front of the inner court a hundred cubits toward the east, since this is from east into west, not from west toward the east? Wherefore a distinction is to be made there: and he measured the breadth from the face of the lower gate unto the front of the inner on the outside. And there another sentence is to be begun, and from the foregoing "he measured" indeed is to be repeated, thus: And he measured a hundred cubits toward the east and toward the north, that is, a hundred cubits from the west toward the east, and likewise a hundred cubits from the south toward the north; or what he says, toward the east and toward the north, we can understand thus: Toward the east, that is, toward the eastern region, he measured a hundred cubits between gate and gate. And toward the north, that 557Bis, the northern region, he likewise measured a hundred cubits between gate and gate, so that this last was said by anticipation. Now sufficiently, as I judge, has it been said thus far concerning the description of one portico; and what follows concerning the rest can easily be understood, almost throughout, from the aforesaid without exposition. But let him who shall have read these things know that almost all which we have discussed concerning the porticoes can be proved from the authority of Scripture, and that many things could also have been proved by us with fuller reasoning, which we willingly pass over while we avoid wearisomeness.

CAPUT IX. De aquilonari et australi porticu exterioris atrii.

557CPost descriptionem ergo portae orientalis describitur mensura portae aquilonaris, in his verbis: Portam quoque quae respiciebat viam Aquilonis atrii exterioris mensus est tam in longitudine quam in latitudine, et thalamos ejus tres hinc, et tres inde, et frontem ejus et vestibulum ejus secundum mensuram portae prioris, quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinem ejus, et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum. Portam hic accipit proprie quam mensus est, sicut superiorem tredecim cubitorum in longitudine, et decem cubitorum in latitudine, et thalamos ejus tres hinc, et tres inde, omnes sex cubitorum, et frontem ejus sexaginta cubitorum, et vestibulum ejus secundum mensuram portae prioris, 557Dquinquaginta cubitorum longitudinem ejus, et latitudinem ejus viginti quinque cubitorum. Tale vestibulum nusquam ibi aperte descripsit, et tamen tale ut ex hoc loco convincitur, ibi fuit, nec solum unum, sed duo, quia unum in latere uno, et aliud in latere alio, excepto eo, quod jacebat in medio: nec idcirco minus ibi fuit quale ibi describit unum magnum vestibulum, quia partes non auferunt, sed constituunt totum. Hinc est quod, descriptis partibus prius, postea de toto subinfertur, cum dicitur: Fenestrae autem ejus, et vestibulum, et sculpturae secundum mensuram portae quae respiciebat ad Orientem. Ecce hic vestibulum repetitur, sed prius ponitur ad partes quod postea ad totum refertur. Quod ergo prius vestibulum, non vestibula dicit, numerum 558Apro numero quemadmodum in sequentibus thalamum, non thalamos ponit. Possumus tamen ex superioribus hinc et inde repetere, et ad vestibulum referre. Post haec quiddam hic additur, quod de superiori porta non legitur, quod tamen de illa etiam recte intelligitur, ad cujus mensuram ista describitur: Et septem graduum, inquit, erat ascensus ejus. Commune est utrique quod sequitur, et vestibulum ante eam. Omnes sane portae exteriores porticus ante se habebant. Nam interiores, sicut jam diximus, post se porticus suas habebant. Sequitur:

Et porta atrii interioris contra portam Aquilonis. Haec enim interior porta Aquilonis frontem habebat contra exteriorem portam aquilonarem, et dextrum latus contra prius descriptam portam orientalem. 558BVel quod dicit portam atrii interioris esse contra portam Orientis et Aquilonis, possumus de diversis portis accipere, ut intelligamus quod porta atrii interioris sit contra portam Aquilonis, sicut porta atrii interioris erat contra portam Orientis, et tunc quod de porta Orientis dicit, repetitio sit ejus sententiae quam superius de eodem posuit. Quamvis autem nolit hic ejus mensuram describere, voluit tamen hic de ea breviter tangere, ut pavimenti mensuram, quod inter ipsam et exteriorem jacebat, posset congruentius subinferre: Et mensus est, inquit, a porta usque ad portam centum cubitos.

Notandum quod solam longitudinem centum cubitorum describit. Nam latitudo pavimenti quanta fuerit, vel quid de ea sentiendum sit, melius ex sequentibus 558Cpatebit. Descripta porta aquilonari, postea sequitur de porta australi: Et eduxit me ad viam australem, et ecce porta quae respiciebat ad austrum, et mensus est frontem ejus, et vestibulum ejus juxta mensuras superiores, et fenestras, et vestibula ejus in circuitu, sicut fenestras caeteras quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinem, et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum.

Notandum quod dicitur secundum mensuras superiores. Nam, si ejusdem mensurae sunt omnes, quidquid in una minus dicitur, ex alterius descriptionis adjectione suppleri videtur, quod prius ponitur vestibulum, referri debet ad totum. Ad partes autem, quod repetitur vestibula, quod liquido patet ex descriptionis eorum mensura. Ad secundum enim 558Dnon autem ad primum subinfertur, quod dicitur quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinem et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum.

Notandum in hac ultima sententia quomodo ad duo praemissa duo sunt subjuncta, et fenestras ejus, et vestibula ejus in circuitu. Ecce duo; sequuntur alia duo: Sicut fenestras caeteras quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinem, et latitudinem XXV cubitorum. Ad id quod prius praemittitur fenestras ejus, primo respondetur sicut fenestras caeteras, ad illud quod secundo praemittitur, et vestibula ejus, secundo respondetur, quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinem, et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum. Nullum tale vestibulum videtur descripsisse 559Aapud portam primam, nec nisi unum apud secundam. Ex hujus tamen descriptione ostenditur ibi sicut et hic plura fuisse. Sed plus quam duo talia fuisse apud singulas primae descriptionis ratio non patitur, propter illud quod de longitudinis, vel latitudinis mensura ibi legitur, de longitudine, et fecit frontes per quadraginta cubitos, de latitudine, et ante faciem portae usque ad faciem vestibuli portae interioris quinquaginta cubitos. Si autem constat duo talis magnitudinis vestibula apud omnes fuisse, relinquitur apud singulas et tertium quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinis et decem cubitorum latitudinis superesse, ut totum simul jure triplex porticus dici debeat, et praedictam mensuram possit implere. Sequitur:

559BEt in gradibus septem ascendebatur ad eam: quod quidem legitur et apud secundam portam · Et vestibulum, inquit, ante fores ejus, non post fores sicut in portis interioribus. Sequitur:

Et caelatae palmae erant, una hinc, et altera inde in fronte ejus. Palmae frontium modo dicuntur caelatae, modo depietae. Sed forte utrobique intelligendum est utrumque, hoc est, ut sint caelatae et coloribus superductae. Apud superiores etiam portas de caelatura palmarum dixerat. Sed quod una hinc, et altera esset inde, non determinaverat. Sicut superius vero post descriptionem exterioris portae mentionem facit de porta interiore, ut mensuram interjacentis pavimenti possit competentius inferre: Et porta, inquit, atrii interioris 559Cin via australi, et mensus est a porta usque ad portam in via australi centum cubitos. Notandum quod porticum per portam intelligit, et quod pavimenti solam longitudinem describit: nam ejus longitudo ex alio melius patebit.

CHAPTER IX. On the northern and southern portico of the outer court.

557CAfter the description, therefore, of the eastern gate, the measure of the northern gate is described, in these words: The gate also which looked toward the way of the north of the outer court he measured, both in length and in breadth; and its little chambers, three on this side and three on that; and its front and its porch, according to the measure of the former gate: fifty cubits its length, and its breadth twenty-five cubits. Here he takes "gate" properly, which he measured, like the former, of thirteen cubits in length and of ten cubits in breadth; and its little chambers, three on this side and three on that, all of six cubits; and its front, of sixty cubits; and its porch according to the measure of the former gate, 557Dfifty cubits its length, and its breadth twenty-five cubits. Such a porch he nowhere there described openly, and yet such a one, as is proved from this place, was there — and not one only, but two, because one was on the one side and the other on the other side, besides that one which lay in the middle; nor for that reason was there any the less there one great porch such as he describes there, because the parts do not take away the whole, but constitute it. Hence it is that, the parts being first described, afterward there is subjoined concerning the whole, when it is said: But its windows, and the porch, and the carvings, according to the measure of the gate which looked toward the east. Behold, here "porch" is repeated; but it is first set down with reference to the parts, and afterward is referred to the whole. In that therefore he first says "porch," not "porches," he puts number 558Afor number, just as in what follows he sets down "little chamber," not "little chambers." We can, however, repeat from the foregoing "on this side and on that," and refer it to the porch. After these things something is added here which is not read concerning the former gate, which nevertheless is rightly understood of that one also, according to whose measure this one is described: And of seven steps, he says, was its ascent. Common to both is what follows, and a porch before it. All the outer gates, indeed, had their porticoes before them. For the inner ones, as we have already said, had their porticoes behind them. There follows:

And the gate of the inner court over against the gate of the north. For this inner gate of the north had its front over against the outer northern gate, and its right side over against the eastern gate described before. 558BOr, in that he says that the gate of the inner court is over against the gate of the east and of the north, we can take it of different gates, so that we understand that the gate of the inner court is over against the gate of the north, just as the gate of the inner court was over against the gate of the east; and then what he says of the gate of the east is a repetition of that sentence which he set down above concerning the same. But although he is unwilling here to describe its measure, yet he wished here to touch briefly upon it, that he might more fittingly subjoin the measure of the pavement which lay between it and the outer gate: And he measured, he says, from gate to gate a hundred cubits.

It must be noted that he describes only the length of a hundred cubits. For how great the breadth of the pavement was, or what is to be thought concerning it, will appear better 558Cfrom what follows. The northern gate being described, there follows afterward concerning the southern gate: And he led me out to the southern way, and behold a gate which looked toward the south; and he measured its front and its porch according to the foregoing measures, and its windows and its porches round about, like the other windows: fifty cubits the length, and the breadth twenty-five cubits.

It must be noted that it is said "according to the foregoing measures." For if all are of the same measure, whatever is said less fully in one seems to be supplied from the addition of another's description. That "porch" which is set down first ought to be referred to the whole; but to the parts, that "porches" which is repeated — which is clearly plain from the measure of their description. For it is subjoined to the second, 558Dand not to the first, that which is said, fifty cubits the length and the breadth twenty-five cubits.

It must be noted in this last sentence how to the two things premised two are subjoined: and its windows, and its porches round about. Behold two; there follow two others: Like the other windows, fifty cubits the length, and the breadth XXV cubits. To that which is first premised, "its windows," there answers first "like the other windows"; to that which is premised in the second place, "and its porches," there answers in the second place, "fifty cubits the length, and the breadth twenty-five cubits." He seems to have described no such porch 559Aat the first gate, nor more than one at the second. Yet from the description of this one it is shown that there, as here also, there were several. But the reasoning of the first description does not suffer that there were more than two such at each of them, on account of that which is read there concerning the measure of the length or of the breadth: of the length, and he made fronts of forty cubits; of the breadth, and before the face of the gate unto the face of the porch of the inner gate, fifty cubits. But if it is agreed that there were two porches of such size at all of them, it remains that at each there is over a third also, of fifty cubits in length and of ten cubits in breadth, so that the whole together ought rightly to be called a triple portico, and may be able to fill up the aforesaid measure. There follows:

559BAnd by seven steps was the ascent to it: which indeed is read also at the second gate · And a porch, he says, before its doors, not behind the doors, as in the inner gates. There follows:

And there were carved palm trees, one on this side and another on that, on its front. The palm trees of the fronts are now called carved, now painted depietae. But perhaps in both places both are to be understood, that is, that they are carved and overlaid with colors. At the former gates also he had spoken of the carving of palm trees. But that one was on this side and another on that, he had not determined. But as above, after the description of the outer gate, he makes mention of the inner gate, that he may more fittingly bring in the measure of the pavement lying between: And the gate, he says, of the inner court 559Cin the southern way; and he measured from gate to gate in the southern way a hundred cubits. It must be noted that he understands the portico by the gate, and that he describes only the length of the pavement: for its length will appear better from another place.

CAPUT X. De tribus porticibus interioris atrii.

Post portas exteriores, ecce describit portas interiores. Post ultimam autem quam descripsit, ad sibi proximam transit, cum dicit: Et introduxit me in atrium interius ad portam australem, et mensus est portam juxta mensuras superiores, thalamum ejus, 559Det frontem ejus, et vestibulum ejus, eisdem mensuris, et fenestras ejus, et vestibulum ejus in circuitu quinquaginta cubitos longitudinis, et latitudinis viginti quinque cubitos. Quod de portis exterioribus superius legitur, hoc de portis interioribus repetitur: et mensus est, inquit, portam juxta mensuras superiores. Quod autem thalamum, non thalamos dicit, numerum pro numero ponit. Quod autem subjunctum est, et frontem ejus, et vestibulum ejus eisdem mensuris, ad totum referri potest, et tunc in sequentibus, habentia, vel aliquid ejusmodi subaudiendum est: Et mensus est fenestras ejus, et vestibulum ejus in circuitu habenta L cubitos longitudinis, et latitudinis XXV cubitos. Fenestras ejus, hoc est vestibuli, dicit fenestras parietum 560Atotum extrinsecus ambientium. Fenestras vero vestibulorum ejus, hoc est vestibuli, dicit fenestras parietum minima vestibula intersecantium. Nec illud debet praeteriri, quod partes totius vocat vestibula vestibuli. Si autem vestibuli, non vestibulorum legitur (nam utraque littera invenitur), ad eumdem sensum refertur, et hoc de vestibulo vestibuli unius lateris dicitur, quod de utroque intelligatur. Sciendum tamen quod ab Hieronymo non vestibuli, sed vestibulorum ponitur. Sequitur:

Et vestibulum per gyrum longitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum, et latitudinem quinque cubitorum. Videte quomodo in omni adhuc descriptione novi aliquid additur quod in praecedentibus non dixerat. Vestibulum quinque cubitorum latitudinis superius 560Bnusquam descripserat, et tamen si apud hanc portam, ergo apud omnes alias erat, quia omnium unam mensuram fuisse liquido constat. Sed ubi, quaeso, hujus vestibulum nisi inter thalamos fuit? Nam spatium quinque cubitorum inter thalamos Scriptura describit. Nec unum tantummodo, sed inter tres thalamos duo in latere uno, et duo in latere alio. Quomodo igitur de vestibulo octo cubitorum legitur in descriptione prima, quod de vestibulo quinque cubitorum legitur in descriptione ista, de singulis intelligendum est per omnia. Per istud vestibulum viginti quinque cubitorum longitudinis facile probari poterit, quod mensura illa frontium quadraginta cubitorum intra parietes sumpta fuerit. Sequitur illud in quo interiores porticus 560Cdifferunt a superioribus. Et vestibulum ejus, inquit, ad atrium exterius. Nam superiores respiciebant intrinsecus, et iste extrinsecus. Et palmas, inquit, ejus in fronte. Ubi subauditur habebat, sed et hoc ei cum caeteris commune erat. Sequitur:

Et octo gradus erant quibus ascendebatur per eam. In hoc iterum interiores a superioribus differunt, quae solummodo septem habuerunt. Post portam australem transit ad orientalem: Et introduxit me in atrium interius per viam orientalem, et mensus est portam secundum mensuras superiores, thalamum ejus, et frontem ejus, et vestibula ejus, sicut supra, et fenestras ejus, et vestibula in circuitu longitudinem quinquaginta cubitorum, et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum. In hac solum descriptione vestibula pluraliter bis ponitur, 560Det recte quidem, quia minimorum etiam vestibulorum descriptio ex descriptione praecedenti completur. Videtur itaque in hac descriptione geminae divisionis mentionem facere in eo quod voluit vestibula bis ponere. Una siquidem porticus primo dividitur in tria vestibula. Deinde duo ex his majora subdividuntur in quatuordecim alia. Post dimensionem itaque minimorum videtur ille qui metitur accedere ad dimensionem majorum. Primum ergo referatur ad minima, secundum ad maxima, quorum illic mensura subjungitur in eo quod adnectitur, longitudinem quinquaginta cubitorum, et latitudinem viginti quinque cubitorum. Sequitur: et vestibulum ejus, id est atrii exterioris, id ejus, 561Avestibulum, inquit, est atrii exterioris, ac si aperte diceret, id ipsum vestibulum quod est ejus, id est atrii interioris, secundum aliquid recte dicitur atrii exterioris, cujus utique omnino dici non debuisset, si juxta similitudinem exteriorum porticuum intra suam portam jacuisset; sed nunc recte ejus dicitur cui inhaeret, deinde subjungitur id quod in praecedenti legitur: Et palmae caelatae in fronte ejus hinc et inde, et in octo gradibus ascensus ejus. Deinde ultima portarum descriptio sequitur cum dicitur:

Et introduxit me ad portam quae respiciebat ad Aquilonem, et mensus est secundum mensuras superiores thalamum ejus, et frontem ejus, et vestibulum ejus, et fenestras ejus per circuitum longitudinem quinquaginta cubitorum, et latitudinem viginti quinque 561Bcubitorum. Vestibulum ejus respiciebat in atrium exterius, et caelatura palmarum in fronte illius hinc et inde, et in octo gradibus ascensus ejus. Hic quia nihil novi additur, expositio quidem superflua videtur. Sequitur: Et per singula gazophylacia ostium in frontibus portarum. Ibi lavabant holocaustum. Quod dicit gazophylacia, vel gazophylaciorum ostia, in frontibus portarum esse, debemus de interioribus porticibus intelligere. Nam quoniam ostia in frontibus gazophylaciorum erant, et ad pavimenta quae ante interiores porticus jacebant omnia respiciebant, recte nunc dicitur quia ostia gazophylaciorum in frontibus portarum erant. Situs ergo gazophylaciorum determinatur in eo, quod dicitur: Et per 561Csingula gazophylacia ostium in frontibus portarum. Linea ergo qua frontes portarum terminantur protrahatur, et ab ea usque ad murum exteriorem gazophylacia ordinentur. Nam quidquid post illam fuerit, in frontibus portarum non erit. In prima omnium descriptione de gazophylaciis locutus fuit, sed per omnes alias nullam de eis mentionem facit. Cur ergo hoc quod hic de gazophylaciis dicitur, huic ultimae descriptioni adnectitur, nisi ut per hoc idem de omnibus intelligi daretur. Similiter et quod de octo mensis adnectit, de omnibus tribus interioribus portis, ut arbitror, intelligi voluit. Omnes tres interiores portae octo mensas per vestibulorum suorum latera habuere, quamvis Scriptura hoc non videatur aperte determinare. Sed videte quam argute Scriptura 561Din quibusdam locis quaedam tacite praeterire voluit, quae ratio quidem diligentius requisita latere non sinit. Sane de gazophylaciis dicitur: Ibi lavabant holocaustum. Sed et de mensis scribitur: Super mensas autem carnes oblationis. Si quis ergo putet solam orientalem portam gazophylacia habere, vel apud aquilonarem portam solummodo mensas fuisse, videat, obsecro, utrum ratio patiatur orientalem portam non habere, ubi reponi debeant quae ibi lavantur? An aquilonarem portam non habere, ubi lavari queant quae ibi reponantur? Nunquid quae ad aquilonarem portam immolantur apud orientalem lavantur, et quae ad orientalem lavantur ad aquilonarem immolantur? Nunquidnam horum neutrum ad australem portam fit, quia in ejus descriptione 562Ade gazophylaciis mentio nulla facta fuit? Scio quidem Patres quaedam Scripturarum loca quae facile possent penetrare negligenter tamen praeterire, dum invenisse desiderarent, atque gauderent quae juxta litteram stare non possent, ut per hoc saltem convincerentur homines allegoriam recipi debere, quam illis adhuc temporibus admodum pauci vix voluissent recipere. Neminem ergo scandalizet si quid dicimus aliud, vel aliter quam in glossis invenerit. Nemo dedignetur nos remanentes spicas velle colligere, nemo miretur illis elabi aliquas potuisse, vel illos potius pauperibus eas sponte reliquisse, quibus hoc divinitus praeceptum est, quibus divinitus datum est tot voluminum horrea de Scripturarum messe replesse. Sed tu vis honorare, et 562Bdefendere veterum auctoritatem, sed nunquam verius honoramus veritatis amatores, quam quaerendo, inveniendo, docendo, defendendo, diligendo veritatem. Attende ergo non utrum dicam aliquid novum, sed verum. Parum timeo dicere aliquid diversum, dum tamen nihil dicam perversum. Sed ecce iterum ad expositionem litterae redeo, et magis quid ipsa dicere velit, quam quid alii de me dicturi sint attendo.

CHAPTER X. On the three porticoes of the inner court.

After the outer gates, behold, he describes the inner gates. And after the last one which he described, he passes to the one nearest to it, when he says: And he brought me into the inner court to the southern gate, and he measured the gate according to the foregoing measures, its little chamber, 559Dand its front, and its porch, by the same measures, and its windows, and its porch round about, fifty cubits in length, and in breadth twenty-five cubits. What is read above concerning the outer gates is repeated concerning the inner gates: and he measured, he says, the gate according to the foregoing measures. But in that he says "little chamber," not "little chambers," he puts number for number. But that which is subjoined, and its front, and its porch by the same measures, can be referred to the whole, and then in what follows "having," or something of this kind, must be understood: And he measured its windows, and its porch round about, habenta L cubits in length, and in breadth XXV cubits. "Its windows," that is, the porch's, he calls the windows of the walls 560Asurrounding the whole on the outside. But "the windows of its porches," that is, of the porch, he calls the windows of the walls cutting through the smallest porches. Nor ought this to be passed over, that he calls the parts of the whole "the porches of the porch." But if "of the porch," not "of the porches," is read (for either reading is found), it is referred to the same sense, and this is said of the porch of the porch of one side in such wise that it may be understood of both. It must be known, however, that by Jerome not "of the porch," but "of the porches" is set down. There follows:

And the porch round about, the length of twenty-five cubits, and the breadth of five cubits. See how in every description still something new is added which he had not said in the foregoing. A porch of five cubits in breadth he had nowhere above 560Bdescribed, and yet if it was at this gate, then it was at all the others, because it is clearly agreed that there was one measure of them all. But where, I ask, was the porch of this one, save between the little chambers? For Scripture describes a space of five cubits between the little chambers. And not one only, but between three little chambers two on the one side, and two on the other side. As therefore it is read in the first description concerning a porch of eight cubits, so what is read in this description concerning a porch of five cubits is to be understood of the several ones throughout. By this porch of twenty-five cubits in length it will easily be able to be proved that that measure of the fronts of forty cubits was taken within the walls. There follows that in which the inner porticoes 560Cdiffer from the foregoing. And its porch, he says, toward the outer court. For the foregoing looked inward, and this one outward. And its palm trees, he says, upon the front. Where "it had" is understood; but this too was common to it with the rest. There follows:

And there were eight steps by which it was ascended through it. In this again the inner ones differ from the foregoing, which had only seven. After the southern gate he passes to the eastern: And he brought me into the inner court by the eastern way, and he measured the gate according to the foregoing measures, its little chamber, and its front, and its porches, as above, and its windows, and the porches round about, the length of fifty cubits, and the breadth of twenty-five cubits. In this description alone "porches" is set down twice in the plural, 560Dand rightly indeed, because the description of the smallest porches also is completed out of the preceding description. And so he seems in this description to make mention of a twofold division in that he wished to set down "porches" twice. For one portico is first divided into three porches. Then two of these, the greater, are subdivided into fourteen others. And so after the measuring of the smallest, he who measures seems to proceed to the measuring of the greater. Let the first therefore be referred to the smallest, the second to the greatest, whose measure is there subjoined in that which is annexed, the length of fifty cubits, and the breadth of twenty-five cubits. There follows: and its porch, that is, of the outer court, that of it, 561Athe porch, he says, is of the outer court, as if he were openly saying: that very porch which is its own, that is, of the inner court, is in some respect rightly called the outer court's — whose it ought by no means at all to have been called, if after the likeness of the outer porticoes it had lain within its own gate; but now it is rightly called that one's to which it adheres. Then there is subjoined that which is read in the preceding: And palm trees carved upon its front on this side and on that, and its ascent by eight steps. Then the last description of the gates follows, when it is said:

And he brought me to the gate which looked toward the north, and he measured according to the foregoing measures its little chamber, and its front, and its porch, and its windows round about, the length of fifty cubits, and the breadth of twenty-five 561Bcubits. Its porch looked into the outer court, and a carving of palm trees upon its front on this side and on that, and its ascent by eight steps. Here, because nothing new is added, exposition indeed seems superfluous. There follows: And through the several chambers a door in the fronts of the gates. There they washed the holocaust. That he says the chambers, or the doors of the chambers, to be in the fronts of the gates, we ought to understand of the inner porticoes. For since the doors were in the fronts of the chambers, and all looked toward the pavements which lay before the inner porticoes, it is now rightly said that the doors of the chambers were in the fronts of the gates. The position therefore of the chambers is determined in that which is said: And through 561Cthe several chambers a door in the fronts of the gates. Let therefore the line by which the fronts of the gates are bounded be drawn out, and from it as far as the outer wall let the chambers be ranged. For whatever shall have been behind that line will not be in the fronts of the gates. In the first description of all he had spoken of the chambers, but throughout all the others he makes no mention of them. Why therefore is this which is here said of the chambers annexed to this last description, unless that through it the same might be given to be understood of them all. Likewise also what he annexes concerning the eight tables, he wished, as I judge, to be understood of all three inner gates. All three inner gates had eight tables along the sides of their porches, although Scripture does not seem openly to determine this. But see how shrewdly Scripture 561Din certain places wished to pass over certain things in silence, which reason indeed, more diligently sought out, does not suffer to lie hidden. Certainly of the chambers it is said: There they washed the holocaust. But of the tables also it is written: And upon the tables the flesh of the oblation. If anyone therefore thinks that the eastern gate alone has chambers, or that only at the northern gate there were tables, let him see, I beseech him, whether reason suffers that the eastern gate not have a place where the things which are washed there ought to be laid up? Or that the northern gate not have a place where the things which are laid up there may be washed? Are the things which are sacrificed at the northern gate washed at the eastern, and the things which are washed at the eastern sacrificed at the northern? Is neither of these done at the southern gate, because in its description 562Ano mention was made of the chambers? I know indeed that the Fathers pass negligently over certain places of the Scriptures which they could easily penetrate, while they desired to have found, and rejoiced in, things which could not stand according to the letter, that by this at least men might be convinced that allegory ought to be received, which in those times still very few would scarcely have been willing to receive. Let it therefore scandalize no one if we say anything other, or otherwise, than he shall have found in the glosses. Let no one disdain that we wish to gather the ears of grain that remain; let no one wonder that some could have slipped from them, or rather that they of their own accord left them to the poor — they to whom this was divinely enjoined, to whom it was divinely given to have filled the granaries of so many volumes out of the harvest of the Scriptures. But you wish to honor and 562Bdefend the authority of the ancients; yet never do we more truly honor the lovers of truth than by seeking, finding, teaching, defending, loving the truth. Attend therefore not to whether I say something new, but something true. I little fear to say something different, so long as I say nothing perverse. But behold, I return again to the exposition of the letter, and attend rather to what it itself wishes to say than to what others will say of me.

CAPUT XI. De mensis ubi immolabatur holocaustum, et de gazophylaciis cantorum.

Et in vestibulo portae duae mensae hinc, et duae 562Cmensae inde, ut immoletur super eas holocaustum et pro peccato, et pro delicto. Quid sit holocaustum pro peccato, et pro delicto, et caetera multa in hunc modum quae ad expositionem litterae pertinent, ex industria praetereo, quia in id solum interim quod alias non reperi totus intendo, quomodo, juxta litteram stare valeat, vel intelligi debeat ista prophetae tam subtilis descriptio. Sed si hoc, ut hic dicitur, mensarum officium erat, ut super eas holocaustum immolari debeat, eligat quisque quid magis oporteat sentire, an apud hanc solam portam victimas immolari debere, an alias etiam portas similes mensas in id officii habere. Si ad hanc solam immolabatur, quid fiet de victima principis qui ad solam 562Dorientalem portam ingrediebatur, et egrediebatur? Si ad solam orientalem victimae lavantur in diebus non festis, quid fiet de victima populi, quando orientalis porta claudebatur, quae soli principi aperiebatur? Ejusmodi allegationibus possumus colligere divinam Scripturam multa ex quadam industria tacite innuere. Dixit jam quatuor mensas, duas hinc, et duas inde esse intra vestibulum. Dicit adhuc et alias quatuor esse extra vestibulum, duas similiter ad latus unum, et duas ad alterum. Et ad latus, inquit, exterius, quod ascendit ostium portae quae pergit ad Aquilonem, duae mensae, et ad latus alterum ante vestibulum portae duae mensae. Quatuor mensae hinc, et quatuor mensae inde. Per latera portae octo mensae erant, super quas immolabant. Quoniam, 563Aut superius dictum est, templum summitatem montis tenebat, et ab omni parte mons ipse usque ad eum locum paulatim se ad altiora erigebat, haec porta (nec mirum) unum latus altius, alterum demissius habebat. Ab uno itaque ad portam ascendebatur, ab alio ad portam descendebatur. Nam quia aquilonaris porta haec erat, sicut Scriptura ipsa determinat, templum procul dubio ab occidentali latere habebat, quod quidem latus orientali lateri altius eminebat. Orientale latus determinatur, cum dicitur: Et ad latus exterius quod ascendit ad ostium portae quae pergit ad Aquilonem duae mensae. Nam quod sequitur, dicitur de alio latere: Et ad latus alterum ante vestibulum portae duae mensae. Erant itaque, ut subinfertur, quatuor mensae hinc, et quatuor mensae inde. Quatuor 563Benim erant ad latus unum, duo intra vestibulum, et duo extra vestibulum. Similiter duo interius, et duo exterius ad latus alterum. Ergo per latera portae octo mensae erant super quas immolabant. Quae sequuntur satis per se patent, et expositione non indigent. Quatuor autem mensae ad holocaustum de lapidibus quadris exstructae, longitudine cubiti unius et demidii, et latitudine cubiti unius et dimidii, et altitudine unius cubiti, super quas ponant vasa in quibus immolatur holocaustum, et victima; et labia earum palmi unius, reflexa intrinsecus per circuitum. Super mensas autem carnes oblationis. Hucusque de mensis. Sequitur autem de quibusdam gazophylaciis, de quibus nihil dictum est in praecedentibus.

563CEt extra portam interiorem gazophylacia cantorum in atrio interiori quod erat in latere portae respicientis ad Aquilonem, et facies eorum contra viam australem, una ex latere portae orientalis quae respiciebat ad viam Aquilonis. Mirum est quod dicitur: In atrio interiori quod erat in latere portae respicientis ad Aquilonem. Atrium namque interius non erat in latere hujus portae, sed potius ante ipsam situm fuit, quia inter tres portas interiores medium jacuit. Sed cum dicitur atrium interius, vel atrium exterius, non uniformiter hoc semper accipere debemus. Dictum est atrium interius quod centum cubitorum quaquaversum in medio locatur, et atrium exterius quo interius atrium ex omni parte cingebatur. In atrio 563Dautem interiore nihil aliud erat nisi unum altare, caetera autem omnia porticus, gazophylacia, templum, domus, erant in atrio exteriore. Secundum aliam tamen considerationem locus in quo templum et domus illae erant quae templo per tria ejus latera coadjacebant, atrium interius dicitur, sicut in sequentibus legitur. Ibi enim gazophylacia sanctuarii erant in quibus sacerdotes carnes oblationis reponebant atque comedebant, quas tamen in atrium exterius efferre non licebat, Scriptura prohibente, quae dicit: Iste est locus ubi coquent sacerdotes pro peccato atque delicto, ubi coquent sacrificium, ut non efferant in atrium exterius, et sanctificetur populus (Ezech. XLVI). Hae tamen carnes quae in exterius atrium efferri prohibebantur in praedictis gazophylaciis reponebant, 564Aet comedebantur, sicut de ipsis legitur: Haec sunt gazophylacia sancta in quibus vescuntur sacerdotes qui appropinquant in Sancta sanctorum ad Dominum. Ibi ponent Sancta sanctorum et oblationes pro peccato et pro delicto (Ezech. XLI). Alia itaque ratione dicitur atrium interius quod ante templum erat, quia, ut dictum est, in medio jacebat. Alia autem ratione illud quo templum per tria latera cingebatur, quia in ipsum per illud etiam quod proprie dictum est atrium interius intrabatur. Sic thalamus interior aula dici solet, non quod intra ipsam sit, sed quod secretiorem locum teneat, et quod per illam ad ipsum aditus pateat. Sic utique sic nullus locus secretior et ab aditu portarum nullus remotior exstitit, quam ille in quo templum constructum fuit. 564BSecundum hanc itaque considerationem in hujus loci comparatione potest dici atrium exterius etiam illud quod Scriptura pro more solet appellare atrium interius. Alia ergo ratione atrium interius dicitur locus in quo templum fuit, et alia ille qui medium tenuit, et altare holocausti habuit. Nihil itaque prohibet illum locum intelligere in quo templum, vel adjacentes domus fuere, in eo quod dicitur, et contra portam interiorem gazophylacia cantorum in atrio interiori, quod erat in latere portae respicientis ad Aquilonem. In hoc atrio quod praedictae portae ab occidentali latere adjacebat illud quod modo describitur gazophylacium cantorum erat, eidemque portae sive porticui lateraliter adhaerebat. De hoc gazophylacio in subsequentibus latius agitur, ipsumque, 564Cin fallor, in his verbis describitur: Et introduxit me in gazophylacium quod erat contra separatum aedificium, et contra aedem vergentem ad Aquilonem, et caetera de quibus latius suo loco dicemus. Hoc autem interim de hoc gazophylacio dicendum videtur, quod ejus latitudo ab hac porta incipiebat, et versus Occidentem se expandebat. Longitudo autem ab Aquilone in Austrum se extendebat. Unde est illud quod de eisdem gazophylaciis subsequenter adjungitur, cum dicitur: Et facies eorum contra viam australem. Habebat itaque porta hujusmodi gazophylacia cantorum, ut dictum est, ab occidentali latere. Habebat et alia ab orientali latere, quae utique sita erant e regione tam orientalis quam aquilonaris 564Dportae. Si itaque e regione portae orientalis frontem habebant ad portam Aquilonis, unde est quod eisdem subditur, una ex latere portae orientalis quae respiciebat ad viam Aquilonis? Dicit itaque quia hujusmodi gazophylaciorum una facies erat ex latere portae orientalis, quae facies respiciebat ad viam Aquilonis. Planior autem littera esset, si dictum fuisset, quorumdam facies, non quaedam facies, sive una facies eorum erat ex latere portae orientalis. Nunc autem non solum ibi una pro quorumdam facies ponitur, sed etiam facies eorum ex superioribus subauditur. Haec vero gazophylacia quae aquilonari portae ab Oriente erant ad viam, non contra viam Aquilonis faciem habebant, quia per ante ipsa in transversum ibat, et non contra ea, sicut australem viam 565Ailla quae ab Occidente erant contracuntem habebant. Debemus autem idem de utroque latere orientalis portae intelligere, similiter vero et de uno latere australis portae. Sed solet Scriptura ex industria praeterire quae scit per se facile intelligi posse. Sequitur:

Et dixit ad me: Hoc est gazophylacium, quod respicit ad viam meridianam, sacerdotum, qui excubant in custodiis templi. Porro gazophylacium quod respicit ad viam Aquilonis sacerdotum erit qui excubant ad ministerium altaris. Gazophylacium quod contra viam meridianam respiciebat, vicinius templo erat, et idcirco juste illorum sacerdotum esse debebat qui in custodiis templi excubabant: nam altari servientibus dignitate praeeminebant. Commune autem 565Best utrisque sacerdotibus quod sequitur cum dicitur: Isti sunt filii Sadoch qui accedunt de filiis Levi ad Dominum ut ministrent ei.

CHAPTER XI. On the tables where the holocaust was sacrificed, and on the chambers of the singers.

And in the porch of the gate two tables on this side, and two 562Ctables on that side, that upon them may be sacrificed the holocaust, and that for sin, and that for trespass. What the holocaust for sin and for trespass may be, and many other things after this manner which pertain to the exposition of the letter, I deliberately pass over, because for the present I am wholly intent upon that alone which I have not found elsewhere: how this so subtle description of the prophet may be able to stand according to the letter, or ought to be understood. But if this, as is here said, was the office of the tables, that upon them the holocaust ought to be sacrificed, let each man choose what it is rather fitting to think: whether that victims ought to be sacrificed at this gate alone, or that the other gates also have like tables for that office. If sacrifice was made at this one alone, what will become of the victim of the prince, who entered in and went out by the 562Deastern gate alone? If at the eastern gate alone the victims are washed on days that are not feast days, what will become of the victim of the people, when the eastern gate was shut, which was opened to the prince alone? By allegations of this kind we can gather that the divine Scripture silently intimates many things out of a certain deliberateness. He has already said that there are four tables, two on this side and two on that, within the porch. He says further that there are also four others outside the porch, two likewise at the one side, and two at the other. And at the outer side, he says, which the door of the gate that goes toward the north ascends, two tables, and at the other side before the porch of the gate two tables. Four tables on this side, and four tables on that side. Along the sides of the gate there were eight tables, upon which they sacrificed. Since, 563Aas was said above, the temple occupied the summit of the mountain, and on every side the mountain itself raised itself little by little to greater heights as far as that place, this gate (and no wonder) had one side higher, the other lower. And so from the one side one went up to the gate, from the other one went down to the gate. For because this was the northern gate, as Scripture itself determines, it had the temple beyond doubt on the western side, which side indeed rose higher than the eastern side. The eastern side is determined when it is said: And at the outer side which ascends to the door of the gate which goes toward the north, two tables. For what follows is said of the other side: And at the other side before the porch of the gate two tables. And so there were, as is subjoined, four tables on this side, and four tables on that side. For there were four 563Bat the one side, two within the porch and two outside the porch. Likewise two within and two without at the other side. Therefore along the sides of the gate there were eight tables upon which they sacrificed. The things which follow are sufficiently plain of themselves and need no exposition. And four tables for the holocaust built of squared stones, of the length of one cubit and a demidii, and of the breadth of one cubit and a half, and of the height of one cubit, upon which they may lay the vessels in which the holocaust and the victim is sacrificed; and their lips of one palm, turned back inward round about. And upon the tables the flesh of the oblation. Thus far concerning the tables. But there follows concerning certain chambers, of which nothing has been said in the foregoing.

563CAnd outside the inner gate the chambers of the singers in the inner court, which was at the side of the gate that looks toward the north, and their faces over against the southern way, one from the side of the eastern gate which looked toward the way of the north. It is a marvel that it is said: In the inner court which was at the side of the gate that looks toward the north. For the inner court was not at the side of this gate, but rather was situated before it, because it lay in the middle between the three inner gates. But when "inner court" or "outer court" is said, we ought not always to take this uniformly. That has been called the inner court which, of a hundred cubits every way, is placed in the middle; and that the outer court by which the inner court was girded on every side. But in the inner 563Dcourt there was nothing else save one altar; but all the rest — porticoes, chambers, the temple, the houses — were in the outer court. Yet according to another consideration the place in which the temple was, and those houses which lay alongside the temple on its three sides, is called the inner court, as is read in what follows. For there were the chambers of the sanctuary in which the priests laid up and ate the flesh of the oblation, which nevertheless it was not lawful to carry out into the outer court, Scripture forbidding it, which says: This is the place where the priests shall boil what is for sin and for trespass, where they shall boil the sacrifice, that they carry it not out into the outer court, and the people be sanctified (Ezech. XLVI). Yet these flesh-portions, which were forbidden to be carried out into the outer court, they laid up in the aforesaid chambers, 564Aand they were eaten, as is read concerning them: These are the holy chambers in which the priests eat who draw near into the Holy of Holies unto the Lord. There shall they lay the Holy of Holies and the oblations for sin and for trespass (Ezech. XLI). And so for one reason that is called the inner court which was before the temple, because, as has been said, it lay in the middle. But for another reason that by which the temple was girded on three sides, because it was entered into even through that which was properly called the inner court. So an inner little chamber is wont to be called a hall, not because it is within the hall, but because it holds a more secret place, and because through the hall the approach to it lies open. So indeed so no place was more secret, and none more remote from the entrance of the gates, than that in which the temple was built. 564BAnd so according to this consideration, in comparison with this place, even that which Scripture is wont by custom to call the inner court can be called the outer court. For one reason therefore the place in which the temple was is called the inner court, and for another that which held the middle and had the altar of holocaust. Nothing therefore forbids us to understand that place in which the temple, or the adjacent houses, were, in that which is said: and over against the inner gate the chambers of the singers in the inner court, which was at the side of the gate that looks toward the north. In this court, which lay alongside the aforesaid gate on the western side, was that chamber of the singers which is now being described, and it adhered laterally to the same gate or portico. Concerning this chamber it is treated more fully in what follows, and it is, 564Cunless I am mistaken in fallor, described in these words: And he brought me into the chamber which was over against the separate building, and over against the house looking toward the north, and the rest, of which we shall speak more fully in its place. But for the present this seems to be said concerning this chamber, that its breadth began from this gate and spread itself toward the west. But its length extended itself from the north into the south. Whence is that which is subsequently added concerning the same chambers, when it is said: And their faces over against the southern way. And so the gate had chambers of the singers of this kind, as has been said, on the western side. It had others also on the eastern side, which assuredly were situated over against both the eastern and the northern 564Dgate. If therefore, over against the eastern gate, they had their front toward the gate of the north, whence is that which is subjoined to the same: one from the side of the eastern gate which looked toward the way of the north? He says therefore that one face of chambers of this kind was from the side of the eastern gate, which face looked toward the way of the north. But the letter would be plainer if it had been said, "the face of certain of them," not "a certain face," or "one face of them was from the side of the eastern gate." But as it is, not only is "one" there set down for "the face of certain of them," but also "their faces" is understood from the foregoing. But these chambers, which were toward the way on the east of the northern gate, had their face not over against the way of the north, because it went crosswise before them and not over against them, as those which were on the west had the southern way 565Agoing over against them contracuntem. But we ought to understand the same of both sides of the eastern gate, and likewise also of one side of the southern gate. But Scripture is wont deliberately to pass over the things which it knows can easily be understood of themselves. There follows:

And he said to me: This is the chamber which looks toward the southern way, of the priests who keep watch in the wards of the temple. But the chamber which looks toward the way of the north shall be of the priests who keep watch for the ministry of the altar. The chamber which looked over against the southern way was nearer to the temple, and therefore it justly ought to belong to those priests who kept watch in the wards of the temple: for in dignity they excelled those who served the altar. But common 565Bto both sets of priests is that which follows, when it is said: These are the sons of Sadoc, who of the sons of Levi come near to the Lord that they may minister to him.

CAPUT XII. De coquinis quae subter porticus fabricatae erant et de atrio interiori.

Hoc loco considerare oportet quod ad viam orientalem hinc inde circa porticus tam exterioris quam interioris atrii quoddam adhuc spatium inter ipsas et gazophylacia vacuum remanet, sicut ex supra positis figuris oculis subjectum patet, in quibus locis coquinae illae esse poterant, de quibus Scriptura in subsequentibus commemorat, cum dicat: Et culinae 565Cfabricatae erant subter porticus per gyrum (Ezech. XLVI). Ad viam autem Aquilonis porticus exterior unum ejusdem quantitatis spatium tantummodo habebat, quod ei ab orientali latere adhaerebat. Nam ab occidentali latere gazophylacia cohaerentia habebat. Constat autem et australem porticum exterioris atrii ab orientali latere simile spatium ad idem officium habere, et gazophylacia ei ab alio latere adhaerere. De similibus ergo similia aestimamus, et idcirco duobus porticibus interioris atrii hinc inde utrum utrobique simile spatium in coquinarum officium relinquimus. Post haec gazophylacia cantorum quinquaginta cubitorum tam longitudinis quam latitudinis ordinamus, ut secundum longitudinem 565Deorum sic sit et latitudo eorum, sicut de quibusdam gazophylaciis legimus. Nam de longitudine omnium gazophylaciorum atrii exterioris una sententia datur, cum longe post in sequentibus dicitur: Quia longitudo erat gazophylaciorum atrii exterioris quinquaginta cubitorum. Post ista gazophylacia cantorum interjecta quadam via decem cubitorum latitudinis ordinantur hinc inde quindecim gazophylacia in via orientali, ut sint, sicut scriptum est, triginta in circuitu pavimenti. Erant autem similiter quindecim ab uno tantum latere, id est ab orientali parte tam in via aquilonari quam in via australi. Sunt vero a frontibus interiorum portarum, ut postea docebitur usque ad locum vulgi, centum quadraginta cubiti. Ex his si demus muro cubitos quinque caeteris 566Aper quindecim aequaliter divisis, inveniemus unumquodque gazophylacium novem cubitos latitudinis tenere. Sequitur:

Et mensus est atrium longitudine centum cubitorum, et latitudine centum cubitorum per quadrum, et altare ante faciem templi. Ex hoc loco satis patet quod interius atrium secundum latitudinem totum illud centum cubitorum spatium tenebat, quod erat medium in illis quingentis cubitis, quos ab Aquilone in Austrum murus ille quingentorum cubitorum includebat. Sed quia orientales porticus caeteris porticibus similes per omnia mensuras habebant, ejusdemque longitudinis atrium includebant, totitemque gradibus assurgebant, constat procul dubio eorum mensuram usque ad medium centenarium se extendere, 566Bet ab ejus initio interius atrium initium sumere. Sed quot ex illis centum cubitis versus Occidentem secundum plani considerationem dico intra se apprehenderit, nondum ex praedictis puto posse probare. Nam, licet versus illam etiam partem centum cubitos superficietenus haberet, quid tamen vel quantum de illo medio centenario ab Occiente secundum rationem plani tumor montis detraxerit, qui ibi ad eam partem attollebatur, necdum patet. Centum siquidem cubitorum linea una jacens, et alia oblique se erigens ab eodem puncto emergentes in eumdem cathetum non concurrunt, nec secundum planum aequale spatium includunt, sed haec quomodo se habeant ex sequentibus 566Cpatebunt. Et altare, inquit, ante faciem templi. Hoc idcirco hic adnectit, quia altare in hoc atrio fuit quod ante templum jacuit. De hujus mensura nihil hic dicitur, sed longe inferius subtiliter explicatur. In hoc atrio facimus aquam illam, quae de templo egrediebatur decurrere, et a dextro altaris descendere. Circa atrii vero inferiora in quatuor eam secamus, et per medias coquinas traducimus, ita ut per ante gazophylacia transeat, ut sit ubi holocaustum lavari queat, quemadmodum scriptum est: Et per singula gazophylacia ostium in frontibus portarum, et ibi lavabant holocaustum. Horum omnium quae diximus in novissima, et magna figura, sicut ibi videri potest, formam et exemplar latius expressimus.

CHAPTER XII. On the kitchens which were built beneath the porticoes, and on the inner court.

In this place it must be considered that toward the eastern way, on this side and on that, about the porticoes both of the outer and of the inner court, a certain space still remains empty between them and the chambers, as is plain, set before the eyes, from the figures placed above; in which places those kitchens could be, of which Scripture makes mention in what follows, when it says: And kitchens 565Cwere built beneath the porticoes round about (Ezech. XLVI). But toward the way of the north the outer portico had only one space of the same size, which adhered to it on the eastern side. For on the western side it had chambers adjoining it. But it is agreed that the southern portico of the outer court also has a like space on the eastern side for the same office, and that chambers adhere to it on the other side. From like things therefore we judge like things, and therefore to the two porticoes of the inner court, on this side and on that, to either on both sides, we leave a like space for the office of the kitchens. After these we range the chambers of the singers, of fifty cubits both in length and in breadth, so that according to their length 565Dso also may their breadth be, as we read concerning certain chambers. For concerning the length of all the chambers of the outer court one sentence is given, when far afterward in what follows it is said: Because the length of the chambers of the outer court was fifty cubits. After these chambers of the singers, a certain way of ten cubits in breadth being interposed, fifteen chambers are ranged on this side and on that in the eastern way, that there may be, as it is written, thirty round about the pavement. But there were likewise fifteen on one side only, that is, on the eastern part, both in the northern way and in the southern way. But there are from the fronts of the inner gates, as will afterward be taught, as far as the place of the common people, a hundred and forty cubits. Out of these, if we give five cubits to the wall, the rest 566Abeing equally divided among fifteen, we shall find that each single chamber holds nine cubits in breadth. There follows:

And he measured the court, in length a hundred cubits, and in breadth a hundred cubits foursquare, and the altar before the face of the temple. From this place it is sufficiently plain that the inner court, according to breadth, held that whole space of a hundred cubits which was the middle in those five hundred cubits which that wall of five hundred cubits enclosed from the north into the south. But because the eastern porticoes had measures like the other porticoes in all things, and enclosed a court of the same length, and rose by totitem steps, it is agreed beyond doubt that their measure extends as far as the middle hundred, 566Band that from its beginning the inner court takes its beginning. But how many of those hundred cubits toward the west it took in within itself — I speak according to the consideration of the plane — I do not yet think can be proved from the aforesaid. For although toward that part also it had a hundred cubits so far as the surface goes, yet what or how much of that middle hundred, from the Occiente, the swelling of the mountain took away according to the reckoning of the plane — the mountain which there rose up toward that part — is not yet plain. For one line of a hundred cubits lying flat, and another raising itself obliquely, emerging from the same point, do not meet in the same cathetus, nor do they enclose an equal space according to the plane; but how these things stand, from what follows 566Cit will be plain. And the altar, he says, before the face of the temple. This he therefore annexes here, because the altar was in this court which lay before the temple. Concerning its measure nothing is here said, but far below it is subtly explained. In this court we make that water, which went forth from the temple, to run down and to descend from the right side of the altar. But about the lower parts of the court we cut it into four, and lead it through the midst of the kitchens, so that it passes before the chambers, that there may be a place where the holocaust can be washed, according as it is written: And through the several chambers a door in the fronts of the gates, and there they washed the holocaust. Of all these things which we have said we have more fully expressed the form and pattern in the last and great figure, as can there be seen.

CAPUT XIII. De quantitate vel aedificiorum quae templo adjacebant.

566D

Quia igitur de anteriori hujus aedificii parte interim quae dicenda videbantur explevimus, nunc ad occiduam ejus partem describendam stylum vertimus, et quae de praedictis ad praedicta supplenda fuerint loco suo supplebimus. Libet hic, sicut et supra, in descriptione porticuum hinc inde Scriturae testimonia in unum colligere, et describendorum aedificiorum situm, modum et ordinem ex eorum collatione astruere, et quid ex his sentiendum sit, hinc auctoritate, hinc ratione probante monstrare. Sicut superius docuimus, templum cum sua porticu 567Apost atrium interius ab occidentali ejus latere eminebat, et per alia tria latera idem templum tres domos cum suis porticibus, interjecto tamen quodam atrio, habebat, quarum unaquaeque centum cubitos longitudinis tenebat. Nam de eo aedificio quod ad dorsum templi ab Occidente fuit, quod et separatum Scriptura vocare consuevit, ex eo quod nulli caeterorum cohaesit, legitur: Et mensus est domus longitudinem centum cubitorum, et quod separatum erat aedificium, et parietes ejus, longitudinis centum cubitorum. De caeteris autem aedificiis quae ex utroque latere erant subinfertur, cum dicitur: Et mensus est longitudinem aedificii contra faciem ejus quod separatum erat ad dorsum, ethecas ex utraque parte centum cubitorum. Dum 567Benim centum cubitorum longitudinis aedificium ex utraque parte mensurari dicitur, ejusdem longitudinis aedificium utrinque fuisse comprobatur. Ecce de tribus domibus; videamus nunc de earum porticibus. Quod apud aquilonare aedificium porticus esset, Scriptura manifestat, cum dicat: Ubi erant gazophylacia in superioribus humiliora, quia supportabant porticus quae ex illis eminebant de inferioribus et de mediis aedificii. Quod autem hoc de aquilonari aedificio, ejusque porticu dictum sit circumstantia litterae patenter ostendit, sicut postmodum in expositione litterae manifestum erit. Hanc quam praelocuti sumus porticum quinquaginta cubitorum fuisse possumus sane inde convincere, quod ejus peribolus, id est, murus qui ejus gazophylacia 567Cquae ipsam supportabant cingebat, legitur quinquaginta cubitorum fuisse: Et peribolus, inquit, exterior secundum gazophylacia, quae erant in via atrii exterioris ante gazophylacia, longitudo ejus quinquaginta cubitorum, quia longitudo erat gazophylaciorum atrii exterioris quinquaginta cubitorum. Hujus porticus latitudo postea exprimitur in eo quod alio loco scribitur: Secundum longitudinem eorum sic et latitudo eorum. Illud sane ex hujus porticus gazophylaciorumque ejus descriptione consequens est, quod et ampliori ratione ex aliis itidem locis probari potest, quia aquilonare aedificium gazophylacia in suis inferioribus, coenacula in superioribus haberet. Nam frustra ejus porticus quinquaginta 567Dcubitis a terra emineret, nisi et coenaculum post se haberet quod sibi congrua altitudine responderet. Quid enim ait Scriptura de hujus aedificii porticibus? Propterea, inquit, eminebant de inferioribus et de mediis aedificii a terrae cubitis quinquaginta. Denique horum gazophylaciorum quae in inferioribus aedificii erant comparatione, murus qui gazophylacia porticus amplectitur, peribolus exterior nominatur, eo quod atrio exteriori vicinius, imo etiam immediate conjungitur. Haec autem porticus juncta erat porticui aquilonari interioris atrii, unde et scriptum est: Ubi erat porticus juncta porticui triplici. Sed ab ea porticu atrii interioris si ei mensurae quam intra parietes habuit de qua sola superius locuti sumus quinque cubitos quasi pro 568Amuri spissitudine adjicimus, procul dubio usque ad vulgi locum ducentos quindecim cubitos invenimus. Ab eo itaque loco praedicta domus cum sua porticu versus Occidentem in centum quadraginta quinque cubitos se extendebat, ultra quod usque ad eumdem vulgi locum, adhuc septuaginta cubiti supererant. In ea enim parte, sicut postmodum docebitur, qua conjunctae erant, communem parietem habebant, et idcirco non centum quinquaginta, sed tantummodo centum quadraginta quinque cubitos tenebant. Inter hoc aedificium et templum erat quoddam spatium centum cubitorum longitudinis, et quinquaginta cubitorum latitudinis. Cujus mensura determinatur cum dicitur: In facie longitudinis centum cubitos ostii Aquilonis, et latitudinis quinquaginta cubitos. 568BQuidquid autem de aquilonaris aedificii modo atque mensura diximus, totum de australi aedificio juxta eamdem similitudinem sentire debemus, quod quidem ex his quae superius jam dicta sunt satis patere putamus. Habemus jam quousque haec aedificia cum porticibus suis extendebant, sed nondum constat quantum intercapedinis includebant. Sed hoc sane, ut arbitror, palam erit, cum constiterit quomodo illud aedificium, quod separatum dicitur, inter haec duo ordinatum fuit. Habebat sane centum cubitos longitudinis et octoginta cubitos latitudinis. Nam intra parietes nonaginta cubitos longitudinis, et septuaginta latitudinis habebat et paries ipse undique per circuitum quinque cubitorum erat, sicut Scriptura demonstrat: Et quod separatum erat aedificium, 568Cversumque ad viam respicientem ad mare latitudinis septuaginta cubitorum. Paries autem aedificii quinque cubitorum erat latitudinis per circuitum, et longitudo ejus nonaginta cubitorum; unde et infertur: Et mensus est domus longitudinem centum cubitorum, et quod separatum erat aedificium et parietes ejus centum cubitorum. Ad dorsum itaque templi prope murum qui dividebat vulgi locum situm erat, et longitudinem suam absque omni dubio in Aquilonem et Austrum extendebat. Sed latitudinem suam ab Occidente in Orientem expandebat. Quia igitur ultra praedicta aedificia altrinsecus jacentia spatium septuaginta cubitorum tantummodo fuit, necesse est ut latitudo aedificii quod octoginta cubitorum 568Dexstitit praedictorum aedificiorum intercapedinem ex magna sui parte occupaverit. Longitudo igitur separati aedificii inter praedictas illas domos extenditur, et adhuc utrumque spatium viginti cubitorum adjicitur, propter illud, quod de eorum gazophylaciis legitur: Et inter gazophylacia latitudinem viginti cubitorum in circuitu domus undique.

Constat igitur inter duo aedificia quae templo adjacebant hinc inde spatium centum quadraginta cubitorum secundum planum utique inclusum fuisse. Domus autem aquilonaris sicut et australis porticum suam non contra medium, sed contra exteriorem, sive inferiorem sui partem habuit, et ab Aquilone utraque ab una et eadem linea incoepit. Nam ubi gazophylacia quae hujus domus porticum supportabant 569Adescribuntur, statim de eorum introitu adnectitur: Et erat subter gazophylacia haec introitus ab Oriente ingredientium in ea de atrio exteriori in latitudine periboli atrii quod erat contra viam orientalem in facie aedificii separati. Hoc atrium, cujus hoc loco mentionem facit, aquilonaris domus profecto ab Aquilone habuit, a qua domo usque ad vulgi locum, latitudinem suam in Aquilonem extendit. Non igitur contra hujus atrii latitudinem quin potius contra domum dicta gazophylacia introitum haberent nisi porticus cum sua domo ab aquilonari plaga ex uno loco inciperet. Ab aquilonari autem latere ejusdem porticus usque ad interius atrium centum cubiti erant, quorum quinquaginta ejus latitudo tenebat. Deinde interjecta quadam 569Bdeambulatione decem cubitorum latitudinis praedictum illud gazophylacium cantorum incipiebat, et se usque ad vicesimum cubitum atrii interioris extendebat. Unde constat quia ejus longitudo quinquaginta cubitorum erat. Hoc gazophylacium in subsequentibus describitur, cum dicitur: Et induxit me in gazophylacium quod erat contra separatum aedificium, et contra aedem vergentem ad Aquilonem in facie longitudinis centum cubitos ostii Aquilonis, et latitudinis quinquaginta cubitos contra viginti cubitos atrii interioris, et contra pavimentum stratum lapide ubi erat porticus juncta porticui triplici. Palam esse puto quomodo hoc gazophylacium contra aedificium separatum erat, vel quomodo in facie interstitii centum cubitorum longitudinis, et 569Cquinquaginta cubitorum latitudinis erat. Quod templum et aquilonare aedificium interjacebat, si hoc gazophylacium usque ad vicesimum cubitum atrii interioris se extendebat. Palam nihilominus quomodo contra aedem vergentem ad Aquilonem erat, si a decimo cubito post illius aedis porticum incipiebat. Sed illud modo oportet considerare quod contra pavimentum atrii exterioris dicitur esse et ne aliud quam aquilonare pavimentum atrii exterioris intelligere possimus, litterae illius adjectione constringimur, in eo quod legimus: Ubi erat porticus juncta porticui triplici. Nam apud orientalem regionem nusquam invenimus porticum junctam porticui. Sed aquilonaris porticus atrii interioris, ut ex superioribus 569Dpatet, ab Oriente habebat gazophylacia cantorum, ab Austro interius atrium, ab Aquilone hoc de quo modo loquimur pavimentum. Patet ergo quod porticus, quae huic porticui juncta fuerat, ei procul dubio ab Occidente adhaerebat, et ex parte eam tangens, et ex majore sui relinquens, versus Aquilonem se extendebat, et hoc pavimentum ab Occidente limitabat. Ut igitur praedictum gazophylacium contra hoc pavimentum possit aliquam sui partem habere, oportet ipsum triplici porticum adhaerere, quin potius communem parietem cum ea in ipso conjunctionis loco habere. Post quem parietem si porticus aquilonaris domus incoepit, darietemque post parietem habuit, profecto gazophylacium in tantum dicto pavimento oppositum 570Afuit, quantum spissitudinis ejus murus habuit. Quod autem illa quam diximus deambulatio ante ipsum gazophylacium fuerit, Scriptura subsequenter ostendit, cum subjungit: Et ante gazophylacia deambulatio decem cubitorum latitudinis. Nec nos movere debet quod idem aedificium, modo gazophylacia, modo gazophylacium dicitur, sicut eadem porticus modo vestibula, modo vestibulum nominatur. Nam unum ad distinctiones, aliud ad totum referatur.

Notandum sane quod in eo loco ubi aquilonaris domus et ejus porticus faciebant angulum ante praedictum gazophylacium, et hunc deambulationis locum, locus quidam erat triginta cubitorum longitudinis et viginti cubitorum latitudinis, de quo quod videbitur 570Bsuo loco dicetur. Nam secundum quod ex superioribus patet, quoniam ab orientali latere ejus gazophylacii usque ad aquilonare aedificium quinquaginta cubiti tantummodo erant, ipsum quidem gazophylacium plus quam triginta cubitos latitudinis habere non poterat, ubi viginti cubitos superesse oportuit, ut constaret quod Scriptura de his gazophylaciis quae circa templum jacebant, dicit: Et inter gazophylacia latitudinem viginti cubitorum in circuitu domus undique. Constat autem quia ab eodem orientali latere ejusdem gazophylacii usque ad terminum sanctificati quingentorum cubitorum ducentum viginti cubiti erant, quorum triginta, ut dictum est, latitudo gazophylacii praedicti tenebat. Deinde spatium seu atrium quod inter ipsum et aedificium separatum 570Cjuxta longitudinem templi se extendebat, centum cubitos secundum praedictam Scripturae auctoritatem occupabat. Latitudo autem aedificii separati octoginta cubitos habuit, a quo loco decem cubitorum latitudo superfuit. Quorum quinque, ut postea monstrabitur, muri latitudo tenebat: nam caeteros quinque via quaedam habebat, quae inter ipsum murum et aedificium media jacebat. Sed si hi numeri in unum conveniunt, procul dubio in ducentos viginti consurgunt. Nunc est facile videre quousque templum se extendit, quod cum suo vestibulo, quemadmodum in posterioribus docebitur, centum cubitorum longitudinis fuit. Sed quia vestibulum templi sine dubio atrio interiori adhaerebat, 570Dab eadem linea ipsum et praescriptum gazophylacium incipiebat. Sed quia gazophylacium, ut dictum est, et ante positum atrium centum triginta cubitis usque ad aedificium separatum se extendebant, et templum cum vestibulo suo centum cubitos tantummodo tenebat, constat quia inter templum et aedificium separatum interstitium fuerit triginta cubitorum.

CHAPTER XIII. On the size or of the buildings which lay adjacent to the temple.

566D

Since therefore we have for the present completed the things which seemed to need saying concerning the front part of this building, we now turn our pen to describing its western part, and whatever of the aforesaid shall have to be supplied to the aforesaid we shall supply in its own place. It pleases me here, as also above in the description of the porticoes, to gather into one the testimonies of Scriturae from this side and from that, and from their comparison to establish the position, manner, and order of the buildings to be described, and to show what is to be thought concerning them, authority proving it on the one hand, reason on the other. As we taught above, the temple with its portico 567Arose beyond the inner court on its western side, and on the other three sides the same temple had three houses with their porticoes, a certain court nevertheless being interposed, each one of which held a hundred cubits in length. For concerning that building which was at the back of the temple on the west, which Scripture is also wont to call "separate," from the fact that it adhered to none of the rest, it is read: And he measured the length of the house, a hundred cubits, and that which was the separate building, and its walls, of a hundred cubits in length. But concerning the other buildings which were on either side it is subjoined, when it is said: And he measured the length of the building over against the face of that which was separate at the back, the ethecas on either part, of a hundred cubits. For while 567Ba building of a hundred cubits in length is said to be measured on either part, it is proved that there was a building of the same length on both sides. Behold, concerning the three houses; let us now see concerning their porticoes. That there was a portico at the northern building, Scripture makes manifest, when it says: Where the chambers were lower in the upper parts, because they supported the porticoes which rose out of them from the lower and from the middle parts of the building. But that this was said of the northern building and of its portico, the surrounding context of the letter plainly shows, as will afterward be manifest in the exposition of the letter. That this portico of which we have spoken before was of fifty cubits we can indeed prove from this, that its enclosure, that is, the wall which girded its chambers 567Cwhich supported it, is read to have been of fifty cubits: And the enclosure, he says, the outer one, along the chambers which were in the way of the outer court before the chambers, its length fifty cubits, because the length of the chambers of the outer court was fifty cubits. The breadth of this portico is afterward expressed in that which is written in another place: According to their length, so also their breadth. This indeed follows from the description of this portico and of its chambers, which can also with fuller reasoning be proved likewise from other places: namely, that the northern building had chambers in its lower parts, upper chambers in its upper parts. For in vain would its portico rise fifty 567Dcubits from the ground, unless it had also an upper chamber behind it which answered to it in a fitting height. For what does Scripture say concerning the porticoes of this building? Therefore, he says, they rose out of the lower and out of the middle parts of the building by fifty cubits of the earth. [Vulg. a terra cubitis quinquaginta, "from the ground, fifty cubits"] Finally, by comparison with these chambers which were in the lower parts of the building, the wall which embraces the chambers of the portico is named the outer enclosure, in that it is joined more nearly to the outer court, nay even immediately. But this portico was joined to the northern portico of the inner court, whence also it is written: Where there was a portico joined to the triple portico. But if, from that portico of the inner court, we add to that measure which it had within the walls — of which alone we have spoken above — five cubits as it were for 568Athe thickness of the wall, beyond doubt we find two hundred and fifteen cubits as far as the place of the common people. And so from that place the aforesaid house with its portico extended itself toward the west into a hundred and forty-five cubits, beyond which, as far as the same place of the common people, seventy cubits still remained over. For in that part, as will afterward be taught, in which they were joined, they had a common wall, and therefore they held not a hundred and fifty, but only a hundred and forty-five cubits. Between this building and the temple there was a certain space of a hundred cubits in length and of fifty cubits in breadth. Whose measure is determined when it is said: In the face of the length a hundred cubits of the door of the north, and of the breadth fifty cubits. 568BBut whatever we have said concerning the manner and measure of the northern building, the whole of it we ought to think concerning the southern building according to the same likeness, which indeed we suppose to be sufficiently plain from those things which have already been said above. We have now how far these buildings with their porticoes extended, but it is not yet agreed how much of an interval they enclosed. But this indeed, as I judge, will be manifest when it shall have been established how that building which is called separate was ranged between these two. It had indeed a hundred cubits in length and eighty cubits in breadth. For within the walls it had ninety cubits in length and seventy in breadth, and the wall itself was everywhere round about of five cubits, as Scripture shows: And that which was the separate building, 568Cand turned toward the way looking to the sea, of seventy cubits in breadth. But the wall of the building was of five cubits in breadth round about, and its length ninety cubits; whence also it is inferred: And he measured the length of the house, a hundred cubits, and that which was the separate building and its walls, a hundred cubits. And so it was situated at the back of the temple, near the wall which divided the place of the common people, and it extended its length without any doubt into the north and the south. But it spread its breadth from the west into the east. Since therefore beyond the aforesaid buildings lying on either side there was a space of only seventy cubits, it is necessary that the breadth of the building, which was of eighty 568Dcubits, occupied with a great part of itself the interval of the aforesaid buildings. The length therefore of the separate building is extended between those aforesaid houses, and there is still added each space of twenty cubits, on account of that which is read concerning their chambers: And between the chambers a breadth of twenty cubits round about the house on every side.

It is agreed therefore that between the two buildings which lay adjacent to the temple on this side and on that a space of a hundred and forty cubits was assuredly enclosed according to the plane. But the northern house, as also the southern, had its portico not over against the middle, but over against its outer, or lower, part; and on the north both began from one and the same line.
