For where the chambers which supported the portico of this house 569Aare described, there is at once annexed concerning their entrance: And beneath these chambers was the entry of those going into them from the east from the outer court, in the breadth of the enclosure of the court which was over against the eastern way, in the face of the separate building. This court, of which he makes mention in this place, the northern house assuredly had on the north; and from that house as far as the place of the common people it extends its breadth into the north. Therefore the said chambers would have their entry not over against the breadth of this court, nay rather over against the house, unless the portico with its house began from one place on the northern quarter. But from the northern side of the same portico as far as the inner court there were a hundred cubits, of which its breadth held fifty. Then, a certain 569Bwalk of ten cubits in breadth being interposed, that aforesaid chamber of the singers began, and extended itself as far as the twentieth cubit of the inner court. Whence it is agreed that its length was of fifty cubits. This chamber is described in what follows, when it is said: And he led me into the chamber which was over against the separate building, and over against the house looking toward the north, in the face of the length a hundred cubits of the door of the north, and of the breadth fifty cubits, over against the twenty cubits of the inner court, and over against the pavement laid with stone where there was a portico joined to the triple portico. I think it is plain how this chamber was over against the separate building, or how it was in the face of the interval of a hundred cubits in length and 569Cof fifty cubits in breadth. Which lay between the temple and the northern building, if this chamber extended itself as far as the twentieth cubit of the inner court. No less plain is it how it was over against the house looking toward the north, if it began from the tenth cubit after the portico of that house. But now it behooves us to consider that it is said to be over against the pavement of the outer court; and lest we be able to understand any other than the northern pavement of the outer court, we are constrained by the addition of that clause, in that which we read: Where there was a portico joined to the triple portico. For in the eastern region we nowhere find a portico joined to a portico. But the northern portico of the inner court, as is plain from the foregoing, 569Dhad on the east the chambers of the singers, on the south the inner court, on the north this pavement of which we now speak. It is plain therefore that the portico which had been joined to this portico beyond doubt adhered to it on the west, and, touching it in part and leaving it with the greater part of itself, extended itself toward the north and bounded this pavement on the west. In order therefore that the aforesaid chamber may be able to have some part of itself over against this pavement, it behooves it to adhere to the triple portico, nay rather to have a common wall with it in the very place of their junction. After which wall, if the portico of the northern house began, and had a wall darietemque after the wall, assuredly the chamber was set over against the said pavement 570Aby just so much as the thickness of its wall had. But that that walk of which we have spoken was before this chamber, Scripture subsequently shows, when it subjoins: And before the chambers a walk of ten cubits in breadth. Nor ought it to move us that the same building is called now "chambers," now "chamber," just as the same portico is named now "porches," now "porch." For let the one be referred to the distinctions, the other to the whole.

It must indeed be noted that in that place where the northern house and its portico made an angle before the aforesaid chamber and this place of the walk, there was a certain place of thirty cubits in length and of twenty cubits in breadth, concerning which what shall seem good 570Bwill be said in its own place. For according to what is plain from the foregoing, since from the eastern side of that chamber as far as the northern building there were only fifty cubits, that chamber indeed could not have more than thirty cubits in breadth, where it behooved twenty cubits to remain over, that what Scripture says concerning these chambers which lay about the temple might stand: And between the chambers a breadth of twenty cubits round about the house on every side. But it is agreed that from the same eastern side of the same chamber as far as the boundary of the sanctified space of five hundred cubits there were two hundred and twenty cubits, of which thirty, as has been said, the breadth of the aforesaid chamber held. Then the space or court which extended itself between it and the separate building 570Calongside the length of the temple occupied a hundred cubits according to the aforesaid authority of Scripture. But the breadth of the separate building had eighty cubits, from which place a breadth of ten cubits remained over. Of which five, as will afterward be shown, the breadth of the wall held: for the other five a certain way had, which lay in the middle between that wall and the building. But if these numbers come together into one, beyond doubt they rise to two hundred and twenty. Now it is easy to see how far the temple extended itself, which with its porch, according as will be taught in later places, was of a hundred cubits in length. But because the porch of the temple without doubt adhered to the inner court, 570Dit and the aforesaid chamber began from the same line. But because the chamber, as has been said, and the court set before it extended themselves by a hundred and thirty cubits as far as the separate building, and the temple with its porch held only a hundred cubits, it is agreed that between the temple and the separate building the interval was of thirty cubits.

CAPUT XIV. De situ vel ratione caeterorum aedificiorum quae ad occidentalem plagam erant.

Sed ut ad caetera quae adhuc restant describenda transeamus, ultra porticum jam saepedicti aquilonaris aedificii ab Aquilone erant decem gazophylacia 571Ae regione ipsius porticus versus Aquilonem usque ad murum exteriorem juxta pavimenti latus ordinata, longitudinis secundum longitudinem porticus cubitorum quinquaginta. Haec sunt illa gazophylacia atrii exterioris, de quibus Scriptura similitudinem trahit, ubi ipsius porticus, seu gazophylaciorum quae sub ipsa erant longitudinem describit, cum dicit: Et peribolus exterior secundum gazophylacia quae erant in via atrii exterioris ante gazophylacia, longitudo ejus quinquaginta cubitorum, quia longitudo erat gazophylaciorum atrii exterioris quinquaginta cubitorum. Secundum gazophylacia, inquit, quae erant ante gazophylacia. Ante quae gazophylacia, quaeso, nisi ante ea de quibus tunc agebat, et multa jam dixerat, quae sub porticibus erant, et ipsas, ut 571Bibi dictum est, aquilonaris aedificii porticus supportabant? Ante porticum igitur praedictam esse describuntur, et atrii exterioris eadem ipsa gazophylacia dicuntur, ut ubi fuerint aperte determinetur. Inter haec tamen gazophylacia, et adjacentem porticum, via quaedam interjecta erat latitudinis, ut arbitror, quinque cubitorum, ubi erat introitus ad gazophylacia quae erant ante aedificium separatum. Descriptis namque gazophylaciis quae porticus supportabant, de hoc statim introitu adnectitur, cum dicitur: Et erat subter gazophylacia haec introitus ab Oriente ingredientium in ea de atrio exteriori in latitudine periboli atrii quod erat contra viam orientalem in facie aedificii separati. Hic sane est ille introitus de quo superius jam aliqua locuti sumus. Ab hac 571Cvia usque ad murum exterioris atrii erant nonaginta cubiti. Qui si per X gazophylacia dividantur, unumquodque eorum novem cubitorum latitudinis invenitur. Quod autem dictum est de plaga aquilonari, intelligendum est per omnia juxta eamdem similitudinem de plaga australi. Erant itaque decem gazophylacia ex parte una, et decem ex parte alia. Haec sunt viginti illa gazophylacia, ut credimus, de quibus in sequentibus legimus. Ecce in istis gazophylaciis eamdem mensuram invenimus quam in superioribus illis, quae in novem cubitos extendimus, ut sint omnia unius latitudinis, sicut sunt, et unius longitudinis. Restat nunc de illa porticu seu vestibulo aedificii separati dicere 571Dquod inter aquilonarem domum et exteriorem murum ei adhaerebat ab Aquilone. Quam autem vestibulum habebat, Scriptura illa determinat. Quae via erat ante vestibulum separatum. Hoc itaque vestibulum, vel porticus tam longitudinis, quam latitudinis quinquaginta cubitorum erat, et sub se gazophylacia habebat, et super illa de inferioribus, et de mediis aedificiis eminebat, et a superiori ejus latere, id est ab Oriente, deambulationem, seu viam, decem cubitorum latitudinis habebat. Quod totum paucis quidem verbis Scriptura determinare videtur, ubi legitur: Et erant ante aedificium gazophylacia, et via ante faciem eorum juxta similitudinem gazophylaciorum quae erant in via Aquilonis. Si gazophylacia ista, et via ante posita per omnia similitudinem 572Ahabebant eorum quae ad viam aquilonis erant: ergo ista sicut et illa conjuncte sibi domui sub porticu, et simili modo adhaerebant, et quidquid de illis sibi in situ, qualitate et quantitate legimus juxta propositam similitudinis normulam ad ista accommodare debemus.

Et nota quod, quamvis alia gazophylacia ad viam Aquilonis essent, ad illa intelligenda circumstantia litterae constringit, de quibus proximo loco tractaverat, et non illa de quibus nihil dixit. Viam autem Aquilonis pro amore suo nominat viam quae ab Aquilone de porta per portam ad interiora tendebat, quae quidem via per ante situm praedictorum gazophylaciorum transibat. Sed ultra aedificium separatum decem cubitos superesse superius docuimus, 572Bquos partim muro, partim viae interjectae superius assignavimus. Porticus itaque ipsius eodem modo ultra se ab Occidente decem cubitos habebit, si ad similitudinem aquilonaris aedificii contra exteriora domus sita fuit. Et quia ultra aquilonarem domum septuaginta cubiti ad Occidentem fuerunt, inter ipsam utique, et separatum vestibulum decem cubiti erunt, ut ad similitudinem superioris porticus teneat, et haec porticus quinquaginta cubitorum spatium, et habeat a superiori seu interiori sui parte deambulationem decem cubitorum. Ab inferiori autem seu exteriori sui parte viam quinque cubitorum esse secundum superiorem modum debemus intelligere, ut hoc vestibulum habeat gazophylaciorum suorum introitum subter se, id est ab inferiori et exteriori 572Csui latere ad similitudinem porticus supradictae. Sed et ut in hoc quoque utrinque una similitudo concurrat, attende ubi separatum aedificium cum hoc ejus vestibulo angulum faciat, et invenies hic quemadmodum ibi spatium quoddam triginta cubitorum longitudinis, et viginti cubitorum latitudinis. Hic est ille locus de quo longe post legitur in subsequentibus: Et erat ibi locus vergens ad Occidentem, et dixit ad me: Iste est locus ubi coquent sacerdotes pro delicto et pro peccato, ubi coquent sacrificium ut non efferant in atrium exterius, et sanctificetur populus. Quia autem iste coquendi locus in atrio illo fuerit, quod gazophylacia sanctuarii habuerit, Scriptura sane diligenter requisita latere non sinit. Attende 572Dnamque quid ibi praemittitur ubi iste locus describitur: Et introduxit me, inquit, per ingressum qui erat ex latere portae in gazophylacia sanctuarii ad sacerdotes quae respiciebant ad Aquilonem. Et additur: Et erat ibi locus vergens ad Occidentem. De his autem gazophylaciis alias legitur: Gazophylacia Aquilonis, et gazophylacia Austri quae sunt ante aedificium separatum, haec sunt gazophylacia sancta in quibus vestiuntur sacerdotes qui appropinquant ad Dominum in Sancta sanctorum. Ibi ponent Sancta sanctorum, et oblationem pro peccato et pro delicto. Profecto si hos, vel alios Scripturae locos diligenter discutimus, nullum alium praedictae coquinae locum invenire poterimus, nisi quem antea designavimus.

573ANotandum sane quod de hoc loco dicitur, quia esset vergens ad Occidentem. Ex quibus verbis manifeste colligitur quod mons quidem post tergum templi ad inferiora vergebat, ubi aedificium separatum gestabat. Diximus jam quod designatus ille locus triginta cubitos habuerit longitudinis, et viginti cubitos latitudinis. Horum medietas triginta cubitorum longitudinis, et decem cubitorum latitudinis, quae aedificio separato adhaerebat coquinas habebat, reliquam medietatem illa scilicet decem cubitorum latitudinis deambulatio tenebat, ita ut via illa per ante faciem coquinarum transiret, et se in ea parte in atrium sanctuarii reflecteret. Juxta hanc similitudinem per omnia credimus, et illud superius designatum ejusdem magnitudini spatium 573Bcoquinas habere, et illam decem cubitorum deambulationem per ante earum faciem se in atrium sanctuarii reflectere, et ad templum respicere.

Sed quidquid de aquilonari latere diximus totum hoc de australi latere in aedificiorum situ, et modo juxta eamdem similitudinis congruentiam intelligere debemus, secundum quod de eo legimus: Et omnis introitus eorum, et similitudines, et ostia eorum secundum ostia gazophylaciorum quae erant in via respiciente ad Notum. Ostium in capite viae, quae via erat ante vestibulum separatum per viam orientalem ingredientibus. Habebat itaque australis plaga simile vestibulum ante aedificium separatum, similem domum, similem porticum, simile 573Cgazophylacium juxta interius atrium. Erat itaque una extremitas praedicti gazophylacii contra viginti extremos cubitos atrii interioris, sicut superius dictum est, ex parte una, erat eodem modo et aliud contra viginti extremos cubitos atrii interioris ex parte alia, et inter illa duo interstitium cubitorum sexaginta. In hoc interstitio vestibulum templi situm erat, quod ab Oriente atrio interiori adhaerebat, ab Occidente, ut dictum est, templum adhaerens habebat.

CHAPTER XIV. On the site or the plan of the remaining buildings which were toward the western quarter.

But that we may pass on to the other things which still remain to be described: beyond the portico of the oft-mentioned northern building there were, on the north, ten chambers 571Aover against that same portico, set in order toward the north as far as the outer wall, alongside the side of the pavement, of a length, according to the length of the portico, of fifty cubits. These are those chambers of the outer court from which Scripture draws a likeness, where it describes the length of that portico, or of the chambers which were beneath it, when it says: And the outer enclosure, according to the chambers which were in the way of the outer court before the chambers, its length was of fifty cubits, because the length of the chambers of the outer court was of fifty cubits. "According to the chambers," he says, "which were before the chambers." Before what chambers, I ask, save before those of which he was then treating, and of which he had already said many things — those which were beneath the porticoes, and which supported, as 571Bwas there said, the porticoes of the northern building? They are therefore described as being before the aforesaid portico, and those same chambers are called chambers of the outer court, that where they were may be openly determined. Yet between these chambers and the adjacent portico a certain way was interposed, of a breadth, as I judge, of five cubits, where was the entrance to the chambers which were before the separate building. For when the chambers which supported the porticoes have been described, concerning this entrance it is straightway subjoined, when it is said: And beneath these chambers was an entrance from the east for those entering into them from the outer court, in the breadth of the enclosure of the court which was over against the eastern way, in the face of the separate building. This assuredly is that entrance of which we have already said something above. From this 571Cway as far as the wall of the outer court there were ninety cubits. And if these be divided among the X chambers, each one of them is found to be of nine cubits in breadth. But what has been said of the northern quarter is to be understood in all things, according to the same likeness, of the southern quarter. There were, therefore, ten chambers on the one side and ten on the other. These are those twenty chambers, as we believe, of which we read in what follows. Behold, in these chambers we find the same measure as in those above, which we extended to nine cubits, so that all are of one breadth, as they are, and of one length. It remains now to speak of that portico or porch of the separate building 571Dwhich, between the northern house and the outer wall, adhered to it on the north. But which [way] the porch had, that Scripture determines. "Which way was before the separate porch." This porch, therefore, or portico, was of fifty cubits both in length and in breadth, and had chambers beneath itself, and rose above those of the lower and of the middle buildings, and on its upper side, that is, on the east, had a walk, or way, of ten cubits in breadth. All of which Scripture seems to determine in few words indeed, where it is read: And before the building there were chambers, and a way before their face, according to the likeness of the chambers which were in the way of the north. If these chambers, and the way set before them, had in all things the likeness 572Aof those which were at the way of the north, then these, like those also, adhered to the house joined to them beneath the portico, and in like manner; and whatever we read of those, of themselves, in site, in quality and in quantity, we ought to accommodate to these according to the proposed little rule of the likeness.

And note that, although there were other chambers at the way of the north, the circumstance of the letter constrains us to understand those of which he had treated in the last place, and not those of which he said nothing. But by "the way of the north" he names, for his own love, that way which from the north went from gate through gate toward the inner parts — which way indeed passed before the site of the aforesaid chambers. But we have taught above that beyond the separate building ten cubits are left over, 572Bwhich above we assigned partly to the wall, partly to the interposed way. Its portico, therefore, will in the same manner have ten cubits beyond itself on the west, if it was situated, after the likeness of the northern building, over against the outer parts of the house. And because beyond the northern house there were seventy cubits to the west, between it certainly and the separate porch there will be ten cubits, so that, after the likeness of the former portico, this portico also may hold a space of fifty cubits, and may have on its upper or inner part a walk of ten cubits. But on its lower or outer part we ought to understand, according to the former manner, that there was a way of five cubits, so that this porch may have the entrance of its chambers beneath itself, that is, on its lower and outer 572Cside, after the likeness of the portico aforesaid. But also, that in this too one likeness may run together on both sides, attend where the separate building makes an angle with this its porch, and you will find here, just as there, a certain space of thirty cubits in length and of twenty cubits in breadth. This is that place of which, far afterward, we read in what follows: And there was there a place turning toward the west, and he said to me: This is the place where the priests shall cook for trespass and for sin, where they shall cook the sacrifice, that they may not carry it out into the outer court, and the people be sanctified. But that this place of cooking was in that court which had the chambers of the sanctuary, Scripture, if it be diligently questioned, assuredly does not suffer to lie hidden. For attend 572Dto what is premised there where this place is described: And he brought me in, he says, by the entrance which was at the side of the gate, into the chambers of the sanctuary belonging to the priests, which looked toward the north. And it is added: And there was there a place turning toward the west. But of these chambers we read elsewhere: The chambers of the north, and the chambers of the south, which are before the separate building — these are the holy chambers in which the priests are vested who draw near to the Lord into the Holy of holies. There they shall lay the Holy of holies, and the oblation for sin and for trespass. Assuredly, if we diligently discuss these or other places of Scripture, we shall be able to find no other place for the aforesaid kitchen save that which we have designated before.

573AIt must indeed be noted what is said of this place, that it was turning toward the west. From which words it is manifestly gathered that the mountain indeed sloped downward behind the back of the temple, where it bore the separate building. We have already said that that designated place had thirty cubits of length and twenty cubits of breadth. The half of these, of thirty cubits in length and of ten cubits in breadth, which adhered to the separate building, had the kitchens; the remaining half — that, namely, of ten cubits in breadth — the walk held, so that that way passed before the face of the kitchens and bent itself back, in that part, into the court of the sanctuary. According to this likeness we believe in all things that that space designated above, of the same magnitude, 573Balso had kitchens, and that that walk of ten cubits bent itself back, before their face, into the court of the sanctuary and looked toward the temple.

But whatever we have said of the northern side, all this we ought to understand of the southern side, in the site and manner of the buildings, according to the same congruence of likeness, in keeping with what we read of it: And every entrance of them, and their likenesses, and their doors, according to the doors of the chambers which were in the way looking toward the south. A door at the head of the way, which way was before the separate porch, for those entering by the eastern way. The southern quarter, therefore, had a like porch before the separate building, a like house, a like portico, a like 573Cchamber alongside the inner court. There was, therefore, one extremity of the aforesaid chamber over against the last twenty cubits of the inner court, as was said above, on the one side; and in the same manner there was another over against the last twenty cubits of the inner court on the other side; and between those two an interval of sixty cubits. In this interval the porch of the temple was situated, which on the east adhered to the inner court, and on the west, as has been said, had the temple adhering to it.

CAPUT XV Quomodo, vel unde colligitur quae sit arduitas montis.

573DSed quoniam ea quae de aedificiorum situ interim dicenda videbantur explevimus, locus postulat ut ea quae ad superiorum expletionem addenda promisimus modo vel ex parte loquamur, et sic ad expositionem litterae revertemur. Primo itaque consideremus, quot cubitos interius atrium ab Oriente in Occidentem secundum considerationem plani habeat, quod superficietenus in omnem partem absque omni ambiguo centum cubitorum erat, sed illud: Et mensus est atrium longitudine centum cubitorum, et latitudine centum cubitorum, per quadrum. Sed, sicut superius monstratum est, ab Aquilone versus Austrum etiam secundum planum centum cubitos hoc atrium includebat. Ducentos autem cubitos hinc, et ducentos 574Ainde, duae porticus cum interjecto atrio tenebant, et in hunc modum ab una extremitate in aliam quingenti cubiti erant. Propter eamdem autem similitudinem, et quantitatem porticuum animadvertimus, et orientales porticus cum interjecto atrio ducentos aeque cubitos secundum planum occupare, et usque ad centenarium medium se extendere. Omnes ergo interiores porticus juxta unam similitudinem illi medio centenario, et contra ejus medium adhaerebant, et quia sexaginta cubitorum mensuram intra parietes tenebant, ab hac mensura in utroque latere viginti cubiti de illo medio centenario supererant.

Sed superius docuimus gazophylacia sanctuarii duobus porticibus interioris atrii, aquilonari 574Bvidelicet et australi, immediate adhaerere, et atrii interioris latus occidentale limitare. Unde constat atrium interius ab Oriente in Occidentem secundum rationem plani non plus quam octoginta cubitos occupare. Sed si orientalis via ab exteriori porta usque ad templi situm uniformiter ascendit, profecto pavimentum quod inter duas porticus centum cubitorum jacuit, non plus quam octoginta cubitos secundum considerationem plani obtinebit, quamvis superficietenus centum absque ambiguo habuerit.

Hinc ergo datur intelligere, parietes porticuum quemadmodum trium praedictarum domorum quinque cubitorum latitudinis esse. Nam 574Cconstat unamquamque quinquaginta cubitos tantum intra parietes habere. Haec erat causa cur superius quinque cubitos muro deputandos censuimus, caeteros quinque viae deputavimus ex illis decem quos ultra aedificium separatum esse deprehendimus.

Hoc loco sane illud admonere volumus, quod ubique pro certo, et quasi pro regula tenere debemus, quoniam sive in plano, sive in pendulo quodlibet aedificium situm sit, nihil ejus mensurae confert, vel detrahit. Quidquid enim de aedificiorum mensura Scriptura docet, etiam secundum plani considerationem accipere oportet. Quoniam ergo aquilonares porticus, seu etiam australes orientalibus 574Dporticibus in mensura et qualitate per omnia similes fuere, oportet de interjectis pavimentis similia aestimare, et nonnisi octoginta cubitos, secundum planum occupasse. Hoc tamen inter ista et orientale et pavimentum fuit, quod iliud secundum unam dimensionem octoginta cubitos, ut dictum est, habuerit, secundum aliam vero dimensionem centum cubitos, etiam secundum planum possedit. Istorum autem mensura secundum unam dimensionem octoginta cubitorum, secundum aliam autem octoginta quinque cubitorum, juxta plani considerationem fuit. Nam haec duo pavimenta ab una et eadem linea qua et interius atrium videntur incipere ab Oriente, quinque tamen cubitis, ultra ejus terminum videntur se extendere ab Occidente, quod ex superioribus 575Aevidentissimum est intelligere. Ad similitudinem tamen interioris atrii possunt ipsa etiam in eam partem centum cubitos superficietenus habere. Elevantur itaque a gemino latere aquilonare, et australe pavimentum, sed orientale quidem et interius atrium ab uno latere tantum. Si autem quaeritur de interiori atrio quantum ab una parte elevabatur, facile hoc et absque scrupulo a geometricae disciplinae perito deprehenditur. Absque dubio atrium, quod octoginta cubitos habuit in base, et centum in superficie sexaginta cubitis excelsius fuit in eminentiori parte. Hujus rei certissimas regulas tradere possumus, sed simplicioribus onerosi esse timemus. Dabimus interim hujus rei aliquod documentum, per quod possit quivis quantumlibet simplex 575Bassertionis nostrae capere experimentum.

575CProtrahatur cujuslibet magnitudinis linea in quinque aequas sectiones divisa, et ad singulas sectiones singulis litteris insignita. Sitque linea jacens A B C D E F. Supra hanc ad quartam sectionem, id est, super E alia linea erigatur, ita ut in nullam partem pronior inclinetur, sed hinc inde rectos, et adinvicem pares angulos efficiat, et tres mensuras praedictis sectionibus singulas singulis aequales contineat. Sitque linea super E erecta E G H K. Protrahatur adhuc tertia linea de A in K oblique elevata. Hoc 575Ditaque facto accepto circino, si primam et tertiam lineam, id est, A F et A K aequales inveneris, assertionis nostrae fidem proprio experimento tenebis. Ponatur itaque ut unaquaeque sectio sit viginti cubitorum. Erit ergo A F, id est, prima linea cubitorum centum, et A K ejus videlicet aequalis, id est, tertia linea erit totidem cubitorum. Linea vero erecta, id est, E K, habebit cubitos sexaginta. Atrium igitur, quemadmodum dictum est, quod octoginta cubitos habuit in base, et centum cubitos in superficie absque dubio sexaginta cubitis elevatior fuit in eminentiori parte, cujus rei formam manifeste habes in triangulo A K E. Hujus autem trianguli basim, id est, lineam jacentem, ejus quae hypotemisam, id est, lineam inclinatam si quantumlibet protraxeris, 576Aet eis cathetum, id est, lineam orthogonaliter erectam applicaveris, cujuscunque magnitudinis triangulos effeceris, tres ejus lineas eadem sibi semper proportione respondere videbis. Nam semper erit hypotemisa ad basim sexquiquarta, et basis ad cathetum sexquitertia. Ab exteriori itaque latere orientalis portae usque ad frontem vestibuli templi altitudo montis ducentis decem cubitis excrevit, si ejusdem montis arduitas in eam partem uniformiter ascendit. Quoniam igitur quaelibet porticus ab exteriori latere usque ad interius latus sexaginta cubitos tenebat, terra ab interiori latere secundum praedictum arduitatis tenorem quadraginta quinque cubitis eminentior erat. Septem autem, vel octo ascensionis gradus, de quibus superius legimus, 576Bsolam muri latitudinem, quae quinque cubitorum, vel septem pedum et semis erat tenere credimus, ita ut ad singulos pedes gradus sit, et octavus gradus aliis septem connumerari, vel non connumerari possit.

Hinc est, quod de australi porticu exteriori legitur: Et in gradibus septem ascendebatur ad eam; et item de australi interiori: Et octo gradus erant quibus ascendebatur per eam. Nunquid extra eam erant in quibus ascendebatur ad eam et intra eam erant in quibus ascendebatur per eam, ita ut latitudo quinquaginta cubitorum, quae intra parietes, erat octo gradus tantummodo habeat? quis sic sapiat? sed eis ipsis gradibus ascendebatur ad eam 576Cet per eam quos muri latitudo tenebat. In ipsis ascendebatur per eam, quia murus de ipsa erat, et in ipsis ascendebatur ad eam, quia per ipsos ad ejus interiora aditus erat.

Nunc itaque quanta sit altitudo unius porticus ex praedictis conjicere possumus. Porta namque interior, ut legimus, tredecim cubitos altitudinis habuit, quibus si illos duos cubitos adjungimus, quos porta, ut superius dictum est, in fronte usque ad coenaculum habuit, in decimum quintum numerum summa consurgit. A tecto autem thalami, id est, a pavimento coenaculi usque ad tectum portae fuerunt, ut legitur, cubiti viginti quinque. Jungantur isti viginti quinque superioribus, 576Det quadraginta habemus. His omnibus et adhuc supra tectum ipsius portae quasi ad propugnacula facienda quinque cubitos adjicimus, et sic quadraginta quinque cubitos altitudinis ab interiori parte implemus. Erat autem, ut superius dictum est, quadraginta quinque cubitis demissior in exteriori latere, et secundum hoc tota ejus altitudo ab exteriori parte videtur in nonaginta cubitos consurgere. Videtur vero ab exteriori parte duo coenacula, unum autem ab interiori parte habere, et de tertio et intimo thalamo dicitur esse:

Et mensus est portam a tecto thalami usque ad tectum ejus. 577579

CHAPTER XV How, or from what, it is gathered what the steepness of the mountain is.

573DBut since we have completed those things which meanwhile seemed to be said concerning the site of the buildings, the place demands that we now speak, at least in part, of those things which we promised were to be added for the completion of the foregoing; and so we shall return to the exposition of the letter. First, therefore, let us consider how many cubits the inner court has from east to west according to the consideration of the plane — which, so far as the surface goes, was on every part, without any ambiguity, of a hundred cubits; but that: And he measured the court, a hundred cubits in length and a hundred cubits in breadth, foursquare. But, as was shown above, from the north toward the south also this court enclosed a hundred cubits according to the plane. But two hundred cubits on this side and two hundred 574Aon that the two porticoes with the court interposed held, and in this manner from the one extremity to the other there were five hundred cubits. But on account of the same likeness and quantity of the porticoes we observe that the eastern porticoes also, with the court interposed, occupy equally two hundred cubits according to the plane, and extend as far as the middle hundred. All the inner porticoes, therefore, adhered according to one likeness to that middle hundred, and over against its middle; and because they held a measure of sixty cubits within the walls, from this measure there remained over, on either side, twenty cubits of that middle hundred.

But we taught above that the chambers of the sanctuary adhere immediately to two porticoes of the inner court, that is, to the northern 574Band to the southern, and bound the western side of the inner court. Whence it is agreed that the inner court, from east to west, according to the reckoning of the plane, occupies not more than eighty cubits. But if the eastern way ascends uniformly from the outer gate as far as the site of the temple, assuredly the pavement which lay between the two porticoes, of a hundred cubits, will obtain not more than eighty cubits according to the consideration of the plane, although so far as the surface goes it had a hundred without ambiguity.

Hence, therefore, it is given to understand that the walls of the porticoes, just as those of the three aforesaid houses, are of five cubits in breadth. For 574Cit is agreed that each one has only fifty cubits within the walls. This was the cause why above we judged five cubits to be assigned to the wall, and assigned the other five to the way, out of those ten which we found to be beyond the separate building.

In this place indeed we wish to give that warning which everywhere we ought to hold for certain, and as it were for a rule: that whether any building whatsoever be situated on the level or on a slope, it contributes nothing to its measure, nor takes anything away. For whatever Scripture teaches concerning the measure of the buildings, it behooves us to take according to the consideration of the plane also. Since, therefore, the northern porticoes, or the southern also, were in all things like to the eastern 574Dporticoes in measure and in quality, it behooves us to estimate the like concerning the pavements interposed, and that they occupied no more than eighty cubits according to the plane. This, however, was between these and the eastern and the pavement, that iliud, according to one dimension, had eighty cubits, as has been said, but according to the other dimension possessed a hundred cubits, even according to the plane. But the measure of these, according to one dimension, was of eighty cubits, and according to the other of eighty-five cubits, according to the consideration of the plane. For these two pavements seem to begin from the east from one and the same line as the inner court also, yet by five cubits they seem to extend beyond its boundary on the west — which from the foregoing 575Ait is most evident to understand. Yet after the likeness of the inner court they too can have on that part a hundred cubits so far as the surface goes. The northern and the southern pavement, therefore, are raised from a twin side, but the eastern indeed and the inner court from one side only. But if it is asked concerning the inner court how much it was raised on the one part, this is easily and without scruple discovered by one skilled in the discipline of geometry. Without doubt the court, which had eighty cubits at the base and a hundred at the surface, was higher by sixty cubits in its more eminent part. We can deliver most certain rules of this matter, but we fear to be burdensome to the simpler sort. We shall meanwhile give some proof of this matter, by which anyone, however simple, may 575Bgrasp a trial of our assertion.

575CLet a line of any magnitude be drawn out, divided into five equal sections, and marked at the several sections with several letters. And let the lying line be A B C D E F. Above this, at the fourth section, that is, upon E, let another line be erected, in such wise that it lean more to neither part, but make on this side and on that right angles, and equal to one another, and contain three measures, each of them severally equal to the aforesaid sections. And let the line erected upon E be E G H K. Let yet a third line be drawn out from A to K, obliquely raised. This 575Dtherefore being done, taking a compass, if you find the first and the third line, that is, A F and A K, equal, you will hold by your own experiment the credit of our assertion. Let it be supposed, then, that each section is of twenty cubits. A F, therefore, that is, the first line, will be of a hundred cubits; and A K, equal, namely, to it, that is, the third line, will be of just as many cubits. But the erected line, that is, E K, will have sixty cubits. The court, therefore, as has been said, which had eighty cubits at the base and a hundred cubits at the surface, was without doubt higher by sixty cubits in its more eminent part; the form of which matter you have manifestly in the triangle A K E. But this triangle's base, that is, the lying line, and of it which the hypotenuse, that is, the inclined line, if you draw them out to whatever length, 576Aand apply to them the cathetus, that is, the line erected orthogonally, of whatsoever magnitude you make the triangles, you will see its three lines always answer to one another in the same proportion. For always the hypotenuse will be to the base sexquiquarta, and the base to the cathetus sexquitertia. From the outer side of the eastern gate, therefore, unto the front of the porch of the temple the height of the mountain grew by two hundred and ten cubits, if the steepness of the same mountain rises uniformly in that direction. Since therefore any portico whatsoever held sixty cubits from the outer side unto the inner side, the earth on the inner side, according to the aforesaid tenor of the steepness, was more eminent by forty-five cubits. But the seven or eight steps of ascent, of which we read above, 576Bwe believe to occupy the breadth of the wall alone, which was of five cubits, or of seven feet and a half, so that there is a step to each foot, and the eighth step can be numbered, or not numbered, with the other seven.

Hence it is that concerning the outer southern portico we read: And by seven steps was the ascent to it; and likewise concerning the inner southern: And there were eight steps by which the ascent was through it. Were those on which the ascent was made to it outside it, and those on which the ascent was made through it inside it, so that the breadth of fifty cubits, which was within the walls, should have only eight steps? who would think so? But the ascent was made to it 576Cand through it by those very steps which the breadth of the wall held. On them the ascent was through it, because the wall was of it; and on them the ascent was to it, because through them there was access to its inner parts.

Now, therefore, from the aforesaid we can conjecture how great the height of one portico is. For the inner gate, as we read, had thirteen cubits of height; to which if we add those two cubits which the gate, as was said above, had in front as far as the upper chamber, the sum rises to the fifteenth number. But from the roof of the little chamber, that is, from the floor of the upper chamber, unto the roof of the gate there were, as is read, twenty-five cubits. Let these twenty-five be joined to the foregoing, 576Dand we have forty. To all these we add yet five cubits above the roof of the gate itself, as it were for making battlements, and thus we fill up forty-five cubits of height on the inner part. But it was, as was said above, lower by forty-five cubits on the outer side; and according to this its whole height on the outer part seems to rise to ninety cubits. It seems indeed to have two upper chambers on the outer part, but one on the inner part; and of the third and innermost little chamber it is said to be:

And he measured the gate from the roof of the little chamber unto its roof. 577579

CAPUT XVI. Descriptio templi ipsiusque vestibuli.

Et introduxit me in vestibulum templi et mensus est vestibulum quinque cubitis hinc et quinque cubitis inde. Quod dicit hinc et inde, non circa templi, sed vestibuli portam debemus intelligere. Latitudo autem portae, ut postea legitur, decem cubitorum fuit. Unde constat quia tota longitudo vestibuli, ut postea 580determinatur, viginti cubitorum fuerit. Sequitur:

Et latitudinem portae trium cubitorum hinc, et trium cubitorum inde. Si usum loquendi consulimus, nomen portae ad triplicem sensum accommodare consuevimus. Nomine enim portae aliquando designamus ipsum ostium quod aperitur et clauditur. Aliquando ipsum hiatum seu aditum per quem transitur. Aliquando ipsum ambitum quo hiatus ipse cingitur, 581Aea videlicet pars muri quae ostium amplectitur, et reliquo parieti in materia vel forma ornatus causa dissimilis et supereminens cernitur. Aliter enim atque aliter intelligimus cum audimus, vel legimus portas civitatis tulit, vel in porta civitatis stetit, vel portas civitatis vario marmore et variis caelaturis ornavit. Hujusmodi aequivocatione et hoc loco sensus confunditur, nisi subtiliter discernatur. Sed constat ex sequentibus latitudinem portae decem cubitorum fuisse, et latera portae (id est utramque valvam) quinque cubitos latitudinis tenere. Quod ergo hoc loco legitur, mensus est latitudinem portae trium cubitorum hinc, et trium cubitorum inde, secundum tertiam acceptionem oportet intelligere. Mensus est itaque latitudinem portae trium cubitorum, 581Bid est ipsum murum variis caelaturis usque in id spatii ornatum. Quem etiam sensum Scriptura ipsa videtur determinare in eo quod dicit hinc et inde. Longitudinem autem vestibuli viginti cubitorum, et latitudinem undecim cubitorum. Longitudo vestibuli juxta latitudinem templi extendebatur, et idcirco utriusque una mensura invenitur, et octo gradibus ascendebatur ad eam, hoc est ad portam, et columnae erant in frontibus una hinc et una inde. Has columnas credimus sitas hinc inde, e regione contra tres praedictos cubitos portae ubi mensus est latitudinem trium cubitorum hinc, et trium cubitorum inde. Et introduxit me in templum, et mensus est frontes sex cubitos latitudinis hinc, et 581Csex cubitos altitudinis inde latitudinem tabernaculi (Ezech. XLI). Videant illi quomodo hunc locum de tabernaculi latitudine intelligant, qui de tabernaculi constructione disputant. Nam id hoc loco determinare non puto praesentis negotii esse. Quod autem dicitur frontes mensurasse sex cubitorum latitudinis hinc, et sex cubitorum altitudinis inde, possumus hoc modo intelligere: Primo mensus est quantum latitudinis in latere portae fuerit usque ad parietem ligneum qui latera quae postea describuntur ab interiori portae portavit. Secundum metitur non quid latitudinis ex alio latere exstiterit: nam hoc ex jam sumpta mensura perpendit, sed quid altitudinis a superioribus portae usque ad primum coenaculum fuerit; sed si explicatio valvarum decem cubitos 581Dhabuit, et latitudo templi intra parietas solummodo viginti cubitorum fuit, consequens est ut ejecturas illas hinc et inde ad quas valvae reverberabantur, quando porta claudebatur, intelligamus unius cubiti latitudinis esse, et ipsum hiatum inter ejecturas ipsas octo cubitos habere. Alioquin non possumus hinc inde sex cubitos invenire. Sequitur: Et latitudo portae decem cubitorum et latera portae quinque cubitorum hinc, et quinque cubitorum inde. Ecce quod de portae, seu valvarum latitudine superius diximus. Post haec longitudo et latitudo ipsius templi determinatur cum dicitur: Et mensus est longitudinem quadraginta cubitorum, et latitudinem viginti cubitorum. Descripta exteriori domo, ad interiorem domum transit, et quod dicitur sanctum sanctorum in 582Ahis verbis describit: Et introgressus intrinsecus mensus est in fronte duos cubitos, et portam sex cubitorum. Sicut in superiori descriptione primo loco mensus est latitudinem portae trium cubitorum hinc et trium cubitorum inde, sic hic primo omnium mensus est duos cubitos in fronte, eam videlicet muri partem quae ad portae ornatum videbatur pertinere.

Notandum autem quod ejusmodi ornamenti ambitus tam hujus quam superioris portae videtur eas per tres partes cingere, hoc est a superioribus, et ab utroque latere. Sed ad superiorem portam in utroque latere mensura sumitur, et quid de superioribus portae sentiendum sit tacite innuitur. Hic autem videtur qui metitur in superioribus portae 582Bmensuram capere, et quid de utroque latere sentiri debeat innuendo ostendere, hoc esse videtur quod ibi metiri dicitur hinc et inde, hic autem in fronte. Sequitur:

Et portam sex cubitorum, et latitudinem portae, septem cubitorum. Si ejecturae hinc et inde circa hiatum ad quas valvae reverberabantur non unius cubiti, sicut superius, sed dimidii esse intelligantur, manifestum erit quod dicitur. Quod igitur dicit portam sex cubitorum, ipsum hiatum inter ejecturas intelligere. Quod autem adjungit, et latitudinem portae septem cubitorum, ad latitudinem valvarum oportet referre. Si enim hiatus inter ipsas ejecturas sex cubitorum fuit, et ejecturam dimidii cubiti hinc inde habuit, manifestum est quia copula valvarum 582Cseptem cubitos latitudinis tenuit. Superius dixit quid latitudinis in latere portae fuerit, sed quantum latitudinis hiatus habuerit aperte non dixit, quod tamen subintelligendum reliquit. Hic autem e converso mensuram hiatus determinavit, et quid latitudinis in utroque latere habuerit subintelligendum dedit. Nam, si hiatus ipse in sex tantum cubitos extenditur, procul dubio ab utroque latere septem cubiti latitudinis inveniuntur. Quae autem eadem latitudo in superioribus portae, et in latere fuerit superius ostendit. Quam vero hoc loco diversa fuerit, omnino non dicit. Quid ergo aliud videtur tacendo innuere, nisi quod debeamus de similibus similia aestimare? Datur ergo intelligi septem cubitos 582Dlatitudinis esse in superioribus hujus portae, sicut et in utroque latere.

Notandum sane quomodo alia aperte dicit, alia tacendo innuit. Et quomodo hunc ipsum dicendi modum alternat, et artis suae peritiam, ut sic dicam, ostentat. Nam quod primo loco aperte exprimit, hoc in sequentibus cum ad similem locum venerit tacite innuit, et e converso quod in superioribus tacendo innuerat, hoc in sequentibus manifeste determinat. Hoc est quod in caelaturis miramur, ubi nodorum perplexa texuntur. Nam quod in una perplexionum copula superponitur ut appareat, in subsequenti supponitur ut dispareat. Sequitur:

Et mensus est longitudinem viginti cubitorum, et latitudinem viginti cubitorum ante faciem templi. 583AQuam autem longitudo et latitudo interioris domus in his verbis describitur, subsequenti determinatione ostenditur, cum dicitur: Et dixit ad me: Hoc est sanctum sanctorum.

Notandum autem quod haec mensura longitudinis seu latitudinis, tam exterioris quam interioris domus, aequalis est illi mensurae quam de templo Salomonis legimus. Dupla vero ad illam quam de tabernaculo invenimus. Sed quoniam Scriptura hoc loco omnino de altitudine tacet, dubitari de exteriori domo potest, cuinam respondere debeat, an templo Salomonis juxta aequalitatem, an tabernaculo foederis juxta dictam proportionem. Habebit namque cubitos viginti si fuerit dupla ad mensuram tabernaculi, triginta vero si fuerit aequalis ad mensuram 583Bpraedicti templi. Sed, si dicimus triginta cubitos altitudinis usque ad primum coenaculum fuisse, oportet ut concedamus portam decem cubitorum latitudinis nimis proceram, et latitudini valde incongruam, id est viginti quatuor cubitorum altitudinem habere. Nam ex praedictis habemus sex cubitos altitudinis tantum supra portam fuisse. Si vero dicatur quod viginti cubitorum altitudo usque ad primum coenaculum fuerit, consequenter tam exterioris quam interioris domus porta altitudinem aptam et suae latitudini congruam habebit. Nam exterior decem et quatuor, interior decem et trium cubitorum erit. Ecce quomodo ex altitudine sex cubitorum quam supra portam superius sumpserat, 583Ctam utriusque portae, quam coenaculi altitudo colligitur, quas aperte non dixerat. Quantum autem spissitudinis murus templi habuerit, determinat cum subjungit: Et mensus est parietem domus sex cubitorum. Sequitur: Et latitudinem lateris quatuor cubitorum undique per circuitum domus. Per latera ista quae modo describit, nihil aliud intelligo, quam quaedam deambulatoria intus et exterius in duobus templi lateribus per parietem ordinata, erantque ab inferioribus usque ad superiora unum post unum sibi invicem immediate conjuncta. Unde est illud quod sequitur:

Latera autem latus ad latus, bis triginta tria. Et forte latera inde dicta sunt, quod in solis templi lateribus, non autem in frontibus essent. Sed quoniam 583Dviginti cubitorum solummodo altitudo sub primo templi coenaculo fuit, non plus quam tria latera capere potuit. Sicut enim in sequentibus legitur, a fundamento unius usque ad fundamentum alterius, solum sex cubitorum spatium fuit. Erant itaque tria interius ab imo usque ad coenaculum, et triginta exterius ab imo usque ad summum. Interiora autem templi in suis superioribus ejusmodi latera non habebant, sicut sequentia manifestant. Bene ergo dicitur bis triginta tria, eo quod triginta tria essent ex parte una, et triginta tria ex parte altera. Adhuc autem de horum laterum descriptione addit, cum dicit: Et erant eminentia quae ingrederentur per parietem domus in lateribus per circuitum ut continerent, et non attingerent parietem templi. 584ATrabes itaque de parietibus proeminebant, et latera supportabant; sed idcirco tabulata quae super ipsas fundata erant non usquequaque usque ad parietem pertingebant, ut videlicet visum admitterent; et intuentium obtutus ab imis usque ad superiora libere percurreret, et totum sub uno aspectu comprehendens de toto judicare potuisset. Sequitur:

Et platea erat in rotundum ascendens sursum per cocleam, et in coenaculum templi deferebat per gyrum. Idcirco satius erat templum in superioribus, et sic de inferioribus ascendebatur ad superiora in medium. Hujusmodi viam gradibus ascendentem, et se in gyrum flectentem videmus quotidie in ascensionibus turrium, vel ejusmodi sublimium aedificiorum. Quod autem ait, idcirco latius erat templum 584Bin superioribus, manifeste determinat, quod descripta latera usque ad coenaculorum suprema non usquequaque pertingebant, vel in superioribus templi omnino non erant, et idcirco, templi amplitudo ibi latior erat, ubi ejusmodi obstacula inter parietes nulla habebat. Quod autem dicitur, et sic de inferioribus ascendebatur ad superiora in medium, videtur ostendere templum hoc duo coenacula habere, et ipsum in tres mansiones dividere. In eo autem quod dicitur medium, intelligimus primum coenaculum, eo quod ipsum primum coenaculum esset medium trium mansionum. Superius latitudinem laterum descripserat in eo quod dicebat: Et latitudinem lateris quatuor cubitorum undique per circuitum domus. Nunc autem eorum altitudinem describit 584Cin eo quod hic adjungit: Et vidi in domo altitudinem, fundata latera ad mensuram calami sex cubitorum spatio. Non dicit sex cubitorum et palmo, cum tamen mensuram calami praemiserit. Unde et ex hoc item loco notare possumus quod superius de calami mensura jam diximus, et vidi, inquit. Sed ubi ea vidit? In domo. Quemadmodum autem vidit altitudinem per circuitum fundata latera; ergo in domo erant, et secundum altitudinem domus alia supra alia fundata erant. Sequitur:

Et latitudinem per parietem lateris forinsecus quinque cubitorum. Videtur mihi locus idem designari in his verbis qui postea dicitur locus orationis. Erat itaque quinque cubitorum latitudinis quasi 584Dquoddam appendicium juxta parietem primi, et infimi lateris factum, ut illic orationis gratia convenirent qui templum ingredi vel non possent, vel non auderent. Mensus est itaque quinque cubitorum latitudinem primo lateri a fine usque ad finem per omnem ejus parietem forinsecus adhaerentem. Sequitur: Et interior domus in lateribus domus. Hoc idcirco dicitur ne circa interiorem domum latera deesse putarentur, et inter gazophylacia latitudinem viginti cubitorum in circuitu domus undique. De his gazophylaciis nihil adhuc dixit, sed quibusdam de loco orationis interpositis, statim eorum descriptionem adnectit. Et ostium, inquit, lateris ad orationem, ostium unum ad viam Aquilenis, et ostium unum ad viam australem, et latitudinem loci ad orationem 585Aquinque cubitorum in circuitu. Ecce de loco orationis repetit, ut addat. Nam superius ipsum locum, nec locum orationis dixerat, nec quod in circuitu, hoc est per tria latera templum ambiebat. Congregemus modo in unum quae de templi mensura audivimus, et prius de ejus latitudine videamus. Primo loco ipsum templum intra parietes, imo intra latera viginti cubitos latitudinis tenuit. Latera autem interiora hinc inde quatuor cubitorum, et parietes sex cubitorum latitudinis habuit. Horum summa in quadraginta surgit. Latera autem exteriora habuerunt similiter utrinque quatuor cubitos latitudinis, quinque vero utrobique habuit locus orationis. Quae omnia si in unum colligis, procul dubio quinquaginta octo invenis. Remanet 586Aergo utrinque via cubiti unius inter templum et gazophylacia, quae contra viginti cubitos atrii interioris esse superius legimus. Haec autem via sub ejectura tecti quae locum orationis operuit esse potuit, et tunc frons templi sexaginta cubitos implevit. Longitudo autem templi centum cubitorum fuisse legitur, sicut in consequentibus exprimitur, et ex praecedentibus colligitur. Nam quadraginta cubitos longitudinis intra parietes habuit ipsum templum, viginti vero sanctum sanctorum, undecim autem latitudinis templi vestibulum. Quatuor autem parietes viginti quatuor cubitos tenuere, et locus orationis cubitos quinque. Haec omnia, si in unum redigis, centenarii summam implebis. 585

CHAPTER XVI. A description of the temple and of its porch.

And he brought me into the porch of the temple, and he measured the porch, five cubits on this side and five cubits on that. In that he says "on this side and on that," we ought to understand it not concerning the gate of the temple, but of the porch. But the breadth of the gate, as is read afterward, was of ten cubits. Whence it is agreed that the whole length of the porch, as is afterward 580determined, was of twenty cubits. There follows:

And the breadth of the gate, of three cubits on this side and of three cubits on that. If we consult the usage of speech, we are accustomed to accommodate the name of "gate" to a threefold sense. For by the name of gate we sometimes designate the door itself, which is opened and shut. Sometimes the opening itself, or the approach, through which passage is made. Sometimes the compass itself by which the opening is girt, 581Athat is to say, that part of the wall which embraces the door, and is discerned to be unlike the rest of the wall in material or in form for the sake of ornament, and to overtop it. For we understand one thing and another when we hear or read "he carried off the gates of the city," or "he stood in the gate of the city," or "he adorned the gates of the city with varied marble and varied carvings." By an equivocation of this kind the sense is confounded in this place also, unless it be subtly discerned. But it is agreed from what follows that the breadth of the gate was of ten cubits, and that the sides of the gate (that is, either leaf) hold five cubits of breadth. That therefore which is read in this place, "he measured the breadth of the gate, of three cubits on this side and of three cubits on that," it behooves us to understand according to the third acceptation. He measured, therefore, the breadth of the gate, of three cubits, 581Bthat is, the wall itself, adorned with varied carvings up to that much of space. Which sense Scripture itself also seems to determine in that it says "on this side and on that." "But the length of the porch, of twenty cubits, and the breadth, of eleven cubits." The length of the porch extended along the breadth of the temple, and therefore one measure of both is found; "and by eight steps was the ascent to it," that is, to the gate; "and there were pillars at the fronts, one on this side and one on that." These pillars we believe to have been placed on this side and on that, over against the aforesaid three cubits of the gate, where he measured the breadth of three cubits on this side and of three cubits on that. And he brought me into the temple, and he measured the fronts, six cubits of breadth on this side, and 581Csix cubits of height on that, the breadth of the tabernacle (Ezech. XLI). [Vulg. reads "of breadth" in the second member also, not "of height"] Let those see how they understand this place concerning the breadth of the tabernacle, who dispute concerning the construction of the tabernacle. For to determine that in this place I do not think to be of the present business. But that he is said to have measured the fronts, of six cubits of breadth on this side and of six cubits of height on that, we can understand in this manner: First he measured how much of breadth there was in the side of the gate as far as the wooden wall which carried the sides which are afterward described, on the inner part of the gate. Secondly he measures not what breadth there was on the other side — for this he weighs from the measure already taken — but what height there was from the upper parts of the gate as far as the first upper chamber; but if the unfolding of the leaves 581Dhad ten cubits, and the breadth of the temple within the walls was of twenty cubits only, it is consequent that we understand those projections on this side and on that, against which the leaves were beaten back when the gate was shut, to be of one cubit in breadth, and the opening itself between those projections to have eight cubits. Otherwise we cannot find six cubits on this side and on that. There follows: And the breadth of the gate, of ten cubits; and the sides of the gate, of five cubits on this side and of five cubits on that. Behold what we said above concerning the breadth of the gate, or of the leaves. After these things the length and breadth of the temple itself is determined, when it is said: And he measured the length, of forty cubits, and the breadth, of twenty cubits. The outer house being described, he passes to the inner house, and describes in 582Athese words that which is called the holy of holies: And having entered within, he measured in the front two cubits, and the gate, of six cubits. Just as in the former description he first of all measured the breadth of the gate, of three cubits on this side and of three cubits on that, so here first of all he measured two cubits in the front, that is to say, that part of the wall which seemed to pertain to the ornament of the gate.

But it must be noted that the compass of ornament of this kind, as well of this gate as of the former, seems to gird them on three parts, that is, from above and from either side. But at the former gate the measure is taken on either side, and what is to be thought concerning the upper parts of the gate is silently intimated. But here he who measures seems to take the measure in the upper parts of the gate, 582Band to show by intimating what ought to be thought of either side; this seems to be that which there he is said to measure "on this side and on that," but here "in the front." There follows:

And the gate, of six cubits, and the breadth of the gate, of seven cubits. If the projections on this side and on that about the opening, against which the leaves were beaten back, be understood to be not of one cubit, as above, but of half a one, what is said will be manifest. In that therefore he says the gate of six cubits, [is] to understand the opening itself between the projections. But that which he adds, "and the breadth of the gate, of seven cubits," it behooves us to refer to the breadth of the leaves. For if the opening between those projections was of six cubits, and had a projection of half a cubit on this side and on that, it is manifest that the coupling of the leaves 582Cheld seven cubits of breadth. Above he said what of breadth there was in the side of the gate, but how much of breadth the opening had he did not openly say, which nevertheless he left to be understood along with it. But here, conversely, he determined the measure of the opening, and gave to be understood along with it what of breadth it had on either side. For if the opening itself extends to six cubits only, without doubt on either side seven cubits of breadth are found. But that the same breadth was in the upper parts of the gate and in the side, he showed above. But how different it was in this place, he does not at all say. What else therefore does he seem to intimate by keeping silence, save that we ought to estimate like things of like? It is given therefore to be understood that there are seven cubits 582Dof breadth in the upper parts of this gate, as also on either side.

It must indeed be noted how he says some things openly and intimates others by keeping silence. And how he alternates this very manner of speaking, and displays, so to say, the skill of his art. For what in the first place he expresses openly, this in what follows, when he has come to a like place, he silently intimates; and conversely, what in the foregoing he had intimated by keeping silence, this in what follows he manifestly determines. This is what we marvel at in carvings, where the intricacies of knots are woven. For what in one coupling of the interlacings is laid over that it may appear, in that which follows is laid under that it may disappear. There follows:

And he measured the length, of twenty cubits, and the breadth, of twenty cubits, before the face of the temple. 583ABut how the length and breadth of the inner house is described in these words, is shown by the determination that follows, when it is said: And he said to me: This is the holy of holies.

But it must be noted that this measure of length or of breadth, as well of the outer as of the inner house, is equal to that measure which we read concerning the temple of Solomon. But double to that which we find concerning the tabernacle. But since Scripture in this place is altogether silent concerning the height, it can be doubted concerning the outer house to which it ought to answer: whether to the temple of Solomon according to equality, or to the tabernacle of the covenant according to the said proportion. For it will have twenty cubits if it be double to the measure of the tabernacle, but thirty if it be equal to the measure 583Bof the aforesaid temple. But if we say that there were thirty cubits of height as far as the first upper chamber, it behooves us to concede that the gate, of ten cubits of breadth, has a height too tall and very incongruous to its breadth, that is, of twenty-four cubits. For from the aforesaid we have that there were only six cubits of height above the gate. But if it be said that the height as far as the first upper chamber was of twenty cubits, consequently the gate as well of the outer as of the inner house will have a height apt and congruous to its own breadth. For the outer will be of fourteen, the inner of thirteen cubits. Behold how from the height of six cubits which he had above taken above the gate, 583Cthe height as well of either gate as of the upper chamber is gathered, which he had not openly said. But how much of thickness the wall of the temple had, he determines when he subjoins: And he measured the wall of the house, of six cubits. There follows: And the breadth of the side, of four cubits, on every part round about the house. By these sides which he now describes I understand nothing else than certain walks arranged along the wall, within and without, on the two sides of the temple; and they were, from the lower parts as far as the upper, one after another immediately joined to one another. Whence is that which follows:

But the sides, side to side, twice thirty-three. And perhaps they are for this reason called sides, because they were on the sides of the temple only, and not on the fronts. But since 583Dthere was a height of twenty cubits only beneath the first upper chamber of the temple, it could not hold more than three sides. For as is read in what follows, from the foundation of the one unto the foundation of the other there was a space of six cubits only. There were therefore three within, from the bottom as far as the upper chamber, and thirty without, from the bottom as far as the top. But the inner parts of the temple in their upper portions did not have sides of this kind, as the things that follow make manifest. Well therefore is it said "twice thirty-three," in that there were thirty-three on the one side and thirty-three on the other. But he adds yet more concerning the description of these sides, when he says: And there were projections which entered through the wall of the house, on the sides round about, that they might hold, and might not touch the wall of the temple. 584ABeams, therefore, projected from the walls and supported the sides; but for this reason the plankings which were founded upon them did not everywhere reach as far as the wall, namely, that they might admit sight, and that the gaze of those looking on might run freely from the lowest parts as far as the upper, and, comprehending the whole under one view, might be able to judge of the whole. There follows:

And there was a pavement rising up in a round by a winding stair, and it carried into the upper chamber of the temple by a circuit. Therefore the temple was preferable in the upper parts, and thus from the lower parts the ascent was made to the upper into the middle. [Vulg. latius, "wider"] A way of this kind, ascending by steps and bending itself into a circuit, we see daily in the ascents of towers, or of lofty buildings of this kind. But in that he says, "therefore the temple was wider 584Bin the upper parts," he manifestly determines that the sides described did not everywhere reach as far as the topmost parts of the upper chambers, or were altogether not in the upper parts of the temple; and therefore the amplitude of the temple was wider there, where it had no obstacles of this kind between the walls. But that which is said, "and thus from the lower parts the ascent was made to the upper into the middle," seems to show that this temple had two upper chambers, and to divide it into three dwellings. But in that which is called "the middle," we understand the first upper chamber, in that that first upper chamber was the middle of the three dwellings. Above he had described the breadth of the sides in that he said: And the breadth of the side, of four cubits, on every part round about the house. But now he describes their height 584Cin that which he here subjoins: And I saw in the house the height, the sides founded to the measure of a reed, by a space of six cubits. He does not say "of six cubits and a palm," although he has premised the measure of the reed. Whence from this place also we can note what we have already said above concerning the measure of the reed: "and I saw," he says. But where did he see them? In the house. But in what manner he saw the height, [so also] the sides founded round about; therefore they were in the house, and according to the height of the house they were founded one above another. There follows:

And the breadth by the wall of the side without, of five cubits. It seems to me that the same place is designated in these words which is afterward called the place of prayer. It was, therefore, of five cubits in breadth, as it were 584Da certain appendage made alongside the wall of the first and lowest side, that there might assemble there for the sake of prayer those who either could not or dared not enter the temple. He measured, therefore, a breadth of five cubits adhering to the first side from end to end along its whole wall on the outside. There follows: And the inner house in the sides of the house. This is said for this reason, lest the sides should be thought to be lacking about the inner house; "and between the chambers a breadth of twenty cubits round about the house on every side." Concerning these chambers he has as yet said nothing, but, certain things concerning the place of prayer being interposed, he straightway subjoins their description. And the door, he says, of the side for prayer: one door toward the northern way Aquilenis, and one door toward the southern way, and the breadth of the place for prayer 585Aof five cubits round about. Behold, he repeats concerning the place of prayer, that he may add. For above he had neither called that place the place of prayer, nor [said] that it was round about, that is, that it encompassed the temple on three sides. Let us now gather into one the things we have heard concerning the measure of the temple, and let us first see concerning its breadth. In the first place the temple itself, within the walls, nay rather within the sides, held twenty cubits of breadth. But the inner sides on this side and on that it had of four cubits, and the walls of six cubits of breadth. The sum of these rises to forty. But the outer sides had likewise on both sides four cubits of breadth, and the place of prayer had five on either side. All which if you gather into one, without doubt you find fifty-eight. There remains 586Atherefore on both sides a way of one cubit between the temple and the chambers, which we read above to be over against the twenty cubits of the inner court. But this way could be beneath the projection of the roof which covered the place of prayer, and then the front of the temple filled up sixty cubits. But the length of the temple is read to have been of a hundred cubits, as is expressed in what follows and is gathered from what precedes. For the temple itself had forty cubits of length within the walls, but the holy of holies twenty, and the porch eleven of the breadth of the temple. But the four walls held twenty-four cubits, and the place of prayer five cubits. All these, if you reduce them into one, you will fill up the sum of the hundred. 585

CAPUT XVII. Descriptio aedificiorum quae templo adjacebant.

587A

Nunc autem de gozophylaciis illis dicendum videtur, de quibus dicitur:

Et inter gazophylacia latitudinem viginti cubitorum in circuitu domus undique (Ezech. XLI).

Docuimus autem longe superius de his gazophylaciis, quae circa templum jacebant, quomodo ab invicem viginti cubitis distabant. Sed quia aedificium separatum, ut superius monstratum est, centum cubitis se inter aquilonarem et australem domum extendebat, et spatium viginti cubitorum adhuc utrinque interjacebat, patet quod inter aquilonarem et australem domum centum quadraginta 587Bcubiti secundum planum erant, unde constat inter templum et utramque domum hunc inde quadraginta cubitos fuisse: nam caeteros sexaginta monstratum est templi latitudinem tenere.

Quomodo autem poterit quadraginta cubitorum spatium capere illud, de quo superius dictum est, quinquaginta cubitorum latitudinis atrium? Sed plane quod secundum planum locus non habuit, ei ex tumore montis accrevit. Si igitur intelligimus illud atrium quadraginta cubitos secundum planum habere, et quinquaginta in superficie, videbimus montis devexitatem in hoc loco, et in supradictis atriis eadem sibi proportione respondere. Nam eadem proportione se habent centum ad octoginta, et quinquaginta 587Cad quadraginta.

Post descriptionem templi adjungitur descriptio aedificii separati, et aedificium quod erat separatum versumque ad viam respicientem ad mare latitudinis septuaginta cubitorum. Paries autem aedificii quinque cubitorum latitudinis per circuitum, et longitudo nonaginta cubitorum. Ista omnia, ut arbitror, satis ex superioribus patent, et idcirco alia expositione non indigent.

Mare autem hic dicit illud quod longe post vocat magnum quod erat ab Occidente: nam aliud erat ab Oriente, quod vocat orientale.

Postquam autem dixerat hoc aedificium versum ad mare, statim infert de ejus latitudine, ut ex eo ipso innueret, secundum quam dimensionem ad mare 587Dversum esset.

Notandum autem quod quia templum aedificium separatum, atrium adjunctum, tam aquilonare quam australe aedificium unius longitudinis erant, omnium longitudinem sub una continuatione commemorat:

Et mensus est, inquit, domus (id est templi) longitudinem centum cubitorum, et quod separatum erat aedificium, et parietes ejus longitudinis centum cubitorum.

Latitudo ante faciem domus, et ejus quod erat separatum contra Orientem centum cubitorum.

Et mensus est longitudinem aedificii contra faciem ejus quod erat separatum ad dorsum ethecas ex utraque parte centum cubitorum.

588ANotandum sane quod latitudo illa centum cubitorum, quam metitur ante faciem domus, id est templi, possit dici latitudo, et longitudo, sed diversis respectibus. Si enim totum illud atrium consideres in quo templum situm fuit inter aquilonarem et australem domum, ab Austro in Aquilonem centum quadraginta cubitorum longitudinis erit, et centum cubitorum latitudinis contra Orientem, quemadmodum hic dicit. Si autem non totum attendas, sed unam quamlibet a quovis templi latere partem, erit, ut in sequentibus legitur, longitudinis centum cubitorum, et latitudinis quinquaginta cubitorum.

Sub sequenti autem sententia situm aedificiorum determinat. Nam aedificium superius descriptum ad dorsum templi separatum erat, et contra faciem 588Bsuam hinc inde ex utraque parte templi quoddam aedificium habebat.

Et mensus est, inquit, longitudinem aedificii contra faciem ejus quod erat separatum ad dorsum ethecas ex utraque parte centum cubitorum.

Vides certe quod ex utraque parte aedificium fuit, et utrumque ethecas habuit, et eo ipso quo ethecas mensus fuit, etiam domorum mensuras accepit.

Ante solaria quaedam intelligimus esse istas ethecas, quas, ut Hieronymus in his verbis testatur, Romae vocant solaria de coenaculorum parietibus eminentia, quae nonnulli Graecorum exedras vocant.

Recapitulatio cum additione videtur esse quod sequitur:

588CEt templum interius, et vestibula atrii, limina et fenestras obliquas, ethecas in circuitu per tres partes contra uniuscujusque limen, stratumque ligno per gyrum in circuitu.

Patet autem cur templum interius esse dixerit, quia inter praedicta aedificia medium locum tenuit. Vestibula atrii nominat, quia atrio adjacebant.

Superius autem designaverat ethecas ex utraque parte, hic autem dicit per tres partes esse, unde constat aedificium etiam separatum ethecas habuisse. Qua ex re convincimus eamdem in tribus similitudinem esse tam in qualitate quam in quantitate. Ethecae autem, ut dicitur, contra limen unius cujusque erant, quia de ipsis coenaculis extrinsecus proeminebant. 588DNe enim altiores vel demissiores ipsis coenaculis putarentur, per limina solariorum situs antesolariorum exprimuntur.

Stratum lignorum per gyrum dicit tabulatum quo ethecarum inferiora sternebantur, quae tres templi partes, ut dictum est, cingere videbantur: Terra autem, inquit, usque ad fenestras erat.

Unde convinci potest quanta devexitas montis fuerit, quandoquidem parietes aedificiorum quae ex parte templi erant, terram usque ad fenestras habebant.

Quod autem dicit cherubin, et palmas usque ad interiorem domum, hoc sanctum sanctorum fuisse, videtur determinare velle interius sanctuarium ejusmodi caelaturas non habere. Erant itaque, ut dicitur, 589Ausque ad interiorem domum, in exteriori videlicet templo, et forinsecus, id est in vestibulo quod eadem consideratione videtur dici posse forinsecus, qua domus interior dicitur intrinsecus. Erant, inquam, in templo et in vestibulo per omnem parietem in circuitu non solum interius, sed etiam extrinsecus fabrefacta ad mensuram et cherubin, et palmae.

Sequitur:

Et palma inter cherub et cherub, duasque facies habebat cherub.

Faciem hominis juxta palmam ex hac parte, et faciem leonis juxta palmam ex alia parte expressam per omnem domum in circuitu.

De terra usque ad superiora portae cherubin, et 589Bpalmae caelatae erant in pariete templi.

Limen quadrangulum, et facies sanctuarii aspectus contra aspectum altaris lignei.

Haec omnia per se, ut arbitror, patent, et nostra expositione non indigent.

Ultima autem sententia situm altaris interioris exprimit, eo quod in medio templi contra portam sanctuarii positum fuerit. Hoc erat altare incensi. Nam illud de quo superius breviter tetigit, et de quo in sequentibus latius agit, erat altare holocausti.

Sequentibus autem verbis videtur exprimere non tantummodo modum quantitatis, sed etiam facturam 589Coperis

Ait itaque:

Trium cubitorum altitudo, et longitudo ejus duorum cubitorum.

Haec ad quantitatem, sequentia referuntur ad qualitatem:

Et anguli ejus, et longitudo ejus, et parietes ejus lignei.

Forte idcirco de latitudine tacuit, quod longitudo et latitudo eadem fuerit, et in utramlibet partem longitudo, sicut et in utramlibet latitudo dici potuit. Videtur autem quatuor ligna per quatuor angulos erecta habere secundum altitudinem, quibus alia quatuor copulabantur tam superius quam inferius, 589Ddirecta secundum longitudinem et latitudinem, quibus tabulae affigebantur, et conjungebantur in unam compagem. Angulos itaque vocat, eo qnod angulos tenerent ligna illa, quae in angulis erigebantur. Longitudinem vero nominat ligna illa quae altaris longitudinem metiebantur.

Parietes autem dicit juncturam tabularum quibus earum hiatus texebatur.

Sequitur:

Et locutus est ad me haec est mensa coram Domino.

Et post haec additur descriptio ostiorum tam templi quam sanctuarii:

590AEt duo ostia erant in templo, et in sanctuario.

Et in duobus ostiis ex utraque parte bina erant ostiola quae in se invicem plicabantur. Bina enim ostia erant ex utraque parte ostiorum.

Puto ex hoc loco manifestum esse, quod idem nomen ponitur in diversa acceptione. Alioquin quomodo stabit, quod dicit: Bina ostia erant ex utraque parte ostiorum? Quod igitur prius dicit ostia, referendum est ad fabricam quae erat ex ligno. Quod postea dicit ostiorum, referendum est ad aditum, qui patebat in muro. Murus autem sex cubitorum spissitudinis erat, et ipsa ejus spissitudo tam in templo quam in sanctuario duo ostia tenebat. Nam unum erat in muro ex parte interiori et alterum ab 590Bexteriori. Pendebat autem ex his duobus ostiis unum a dextris et alterum a sinistris, juxta quem modum per omnia erat et in templo Salomonis.

Hoc est quod ibi dicitur: « Et duo ostia de lignis abiegnis altrinsecus, utrumque autem ostium erat in gemina divisum (III Reg. VI, 34), » hoc est quod superius dixerat: Latera quinque cubitorum. Quod ibi dicit latera, hic dicit ostiola. Haec latera ferreis nexibus copulabantur, et cum portae aperirentur invicem plicabantur. Quemadmodum et hic dicitur, et ibi dictum est, quod se invicem tenendo aperiebantur. Idcirco autem unum ostium in duo dividebatur, et invicem plicabatur, ne muri latitudinem excederet dum aperiretur.

590CEt celata erant in ipsis ostiis templi cherubin, et sculptura palmarum sicut in parietibus quoque expressa erant.

Ista omnia per se satis patent.

Recte autem subjungitur:

Quamobrem erant grossiora ligna in vestibuli fronte forinsecus super quae fenestrae obliquae.

Ibi namque grossiora ligna esse res exigebat, ubi ornatus causa digniores et excellentes caelaturas esse oportebat. Datur autem intelligi ex his verbis, parietes opertos esse lignis quemadmodum in templo Salomonis.

Sequitur:

Et similitudo palmarum hinc atque inde in humerulis 590Dvestibuli secundum latera domus, latitudinemque parietum.

Patent ista:
