Et eduxit me in atrium exterius per viam ducentem ad Aquilonem, et introduxit in gazophylacium, quod erat contra separatum aedificium.

De vestibuli fronte templi constat eum novissimo loco egisse, de quo loco videtur modo per aquilonarem portam atrii interioris in descriptum gazophylacium transire. Recte sane. Nam hoc utique gazophylacium ad praedictam decem cubitorum latitudinis deambulationem faciem habebat, quae ab eo loco incipiebat ubi sta porticus porticui aquilonaris domus 591Ajuncta erat, et ubi, ut ex hoc loco colligitur, in latere videlicet triplicis porticus aditus ad illud gazophylacium patebat:

Et induxit me, inquit, in gazophylacium quod erat contra separatum aedificium, et contra aedem vergentem ad Aquilonem.

In facie longitudinis centum cubitos ostii Aquilonis et latitudinis quinquaginta cubitos.

Contra viginti cubitos atrii interioris, et contra pavimentum stratum lapide atrii exterioris, ubi erat porticus juncta porticui triplici.

Et ante gazophylacia deambulatio decem cubitorum latitudinis ad interiora respiciens viae cubiti unius, et ostia eorum ad Aquilonem.

591BHorum omnium descriptionem superius tam diligenter exposuimus, ut nesciamus utrum aliquid (nisi forte pro litterae expositione) ad praedicta addere debeamus. Ostium Aquilonis dicit illud ostium templi de quo superius dixerat, et ostium lateris ad orationem. Ostium unum ad Aquilonem. Nondum sane, quia quod prius quasi singulariter posuit, postea pluraliter designavit. Et induxit me, inquit, in gazophylacium, et in sequentibus, et ante gazophylacia deambulatio decem cubitorum latitudinis ad interiora respiciens viae cubiti unius. Quod autem sequitur: Et ostia earum ad Aquilonem, si earum ibi legitur, referendum est ad porticus; si vero eorum, referendum est ad gazophylacia. Nam utrobique poterit esse sententia vera.

591CSequitur:

Ubi erant gazophylacia in superioribus humiliora.

Quia supportabant porticus quae ex illis eminebant de inferioribus et de mediis aedificii. Praemissa descriptione deambulationis, bene additur ubi erant gazophylacia; nam juxta deambulationem erant, et ei ab Aquilone adhaerebant.

Illud quoque notandum, quia pluraliter porticus dicit, quod prius porticum dixit. Sane ut his verbis facile perpenditur, tam domus quam ejus porticus in triplicem mansionem distinguebatur. Si enim aedificium habebat inferiora et media, habebat utique et suprema. Medium namque non dicitur nisi ad duo extrema. Habebat itaque domus sicut et 591Dsua porticus gazophylacia in imo, coenaculum unum in medio, et coenaculum aliud in summo. Habebat et porticus gazophylacia in inferioribus contra gazophylacia domus, quae porticus supportabant. Erant namque porticus in horum superioribus, una contra coenaculum primum, et alia contra coenaculum secundum, quae a gazophylaciis supportabantur. Inferior porticus de interioribus aedificii eminebat, quia supra gazophylacia sita erat, quae inferioribus aedificiis respondebant. Superior porticus de mediis aedificii eminebat, quia supra primam porticum sita erat, quae mediis aedificii respondebat. Illud nunc quale sit consideremus, quod gazophylacia dicuntur esse humiliora in superioribus. Sane si arduitatem montis circa hunc locum secundum 592Apriorem modum aestimemus, terram in uno aedificii latere ex parte templi sexaginta cubitis excelsiorem, quam in alio latere inveniemus. Sed quis nesciat, ratione id exigente, pavimenta aedificiorum in aequalem quamdam superficiem sternere oportere? Ne ergo putaremus terram ab una parte sexaginta cubitis cavatam fuisse, dicit Scriptura gazophylacia in superioribus humiliora esse, ut intelligamus unum gazophylacium divisum in plura, et singula habere pavimenta, in seipsis quidem aequalia, sed alia aliis eminentiora, et secundum ascensum montis graduum more alia supra alia elevata. Ubi ergo pavimenta elevatiora, ibi gazophylacia erant humiliora, id est minus altitudinis habentia, hoc idem intelligimus de gazophylaciis illis quae erant sub porticibus. 592BHoc idem de divisione illa sex porticuum quas in septem portiones secundum latitudinem, montisque ascensum distinximus. Hoc idem de gazophylaciis, quae circa pavimenta erant sentimus. Idcirco autem hae distinctiones erant utique longae, sed parum latae. Nam gazophylacia eadem leguntur quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinis fuisse, quae tamen non plus quam novem cubitorum latitudinis videntur habere. Hae tamen gazophylaciorum divisiones non tam videntur parietibus interseptae, quam columnis distinctae, ita tamen ut has columnas (quemadmodum in claustris fieri solet) nonnullius altitudinis murus supportaret, qui pavimentorum elevationes susciperet, elevationumque degradationes dispararet. In hoc itaque gazophylacia exterioris atrii ab 592Cistis gazophylaciis discrepabant, quod illa columnas habebant, in his vero nullae erant.

Tristega enim, inquit, erant et columnas non habebant, sicut erant columnae atriorum.

Bene autem de his porticibus additur, propterea eminebant. Quoniam supportabantur a gazophylaciis, idcirco eminebant, et subditur quidem quomodo, vel quantum eminebant a terra.

Eminebant, inquit, de inferioribus, et de mediis aedificii a terra cubitis quinquaginta.

Si ista porticuum supereminentia a terra sursum quinquaginta et quinquaginta cubitis distinguitur, tota altitudo domus centum quinquaginta cubitos 592Dhabere videtur. Inferior autem porticus non ubique a terra quinquaginta cubitis eminebat, sed in eo latere ubi terra dimissior erat. Sed quia secundum praedictam rationem terra in uno latere domus ex parte templi elevatior erat cubitis sexaginta, et primum coenaculum domus secundum descriptionem porticus eminebat, ex parte alia tantummodo cubitis quinquaginta videtur terra decem cubitis exterius eminere ex parte templi supra pavimentum primi coenaculi. Quod igitur superius legimus, quia terra usque ad fenestras erat: de fenestris primi coenaculi intelligere debemus, ita ut quibusdam gradibus ejus ostia descenderetur, quemadmodum in cellariis videmus. Ethecas autem contra superius coenaculum esse perpendimus. 593

593BSed quoniam gazophylacia quae sub porticibus erant cum eis ipsis domui adhaerebant, ab interiori parte parietem unum cum ipsa domo communem habebant; qui quidem gazophylaciis porticuum tanto longior erat, quanto longitudinem illorum latitudo domus superabat. Per exteriorem ergo parietem qui atrio exteriori adhaerebat, longitudo gazophylaciorum exprimitur, cum adjungitur: Et peribolus exterior secundum gazophylacia quae erant in via atrii exterioris ante gazophylacia longitudo ejus quinquaginta cubitorum.

Notandum sane quod non dicit porticum ipsam 594Bquinquaginta cubitos longitudinis habere, sed peribolum ejus exteriorem, id est parietem illum quem ad exteriora habebat quinquaginta cubitorum longitudinis fuisse, ne putetur haec mensura intra parietes, quemadmodum in superioribus porticibus sumpta fuisse. Patet ergo, quod superius jam diximus, quia unum parietem cum domo communem habebat, ita ut utriusque mensurae annumerari debeat, ut constet de hujus porticus gazophylaciis, quod illis gazophylaciis legitur quae ad horum similitudinem describuntur. Secundum longitudinem eorum, inquit, sic et latitudo eorum. Generalis autem 595Asententia esse videtur quae subinfertur, cum dicitur: Quia longitudo erat gazophylaciorum atrii exterioris quinquaginta cubitorum. Cum enim dicatur, non eorum, sed gazophylaciorum, non videtur ad praedicta relatio facta, sed generalis sententia prolata, ut omnium gazophylaciorum exterioris atrii intelligatur longitudo una.

CHAPTER XVII. A description of the buildings which lay next to the temple.

587A

But now it seems that we must speak of those chambers concerning which it is said:

And between the chambers a breadth of twenty cubits round about the house on every side (Ezech. XLI).

Now we taught far above concerning these chambers, which lay about the temple, how they were distant from one another by twenty cubits. But because the separate building, as was shown above, extended itself a hundred cubits between the northern and the southern house, and a space of twenty cubits still lay between on either side, it is plain that between the northern and the southern house there were a hundred and forty 587Bcubits according to the level; whence it is agreed that between the temple and each house there were, this one thence, forty cubits: for it was shown that the remaining sixty are taken up by the breadth of the temple.

But how will a space of forty cubits be able to contain that court, of which it was said above, of fifty cubits in breadth? But plainly what the place did not have according to the level accrued to it from the swelling of the mountain. If therefore we understand that court to have forty cubits according to the level and fifty upon the surface, we shall see that the slope of the mountain in this place and in the aforesaid courts answers to itself in the same proportion. For a hundred stands to eighty in the same proportion as fifty 587Cto forty.

After the description of the temple there is joined the description of the separate building: and the building which was separate and turned toward the way looking to the sea, of seventy cubits in breadth. But the wall of the building was of five cubits in breadth round about, and its length ninety cubits. All these things, as I judge, are sufficiently plain from what has gone before, and therefore they do not need another exposition.

But by "the sea" he here means that which far afterward he calls the great sea, which was on the west: for there was another on the east, which he calls the eastern.

But after he had said that this building was turned toward the sea, he at once brings in something concerning its breadth, that by that very thing he might hint according to which dimension it was turned toward the 587Dsea.

But it must be noted that, because the temple, the separate building, the adjoining court, and the building both northern and southern were of one length, he records the length of them all under a single continuation:

And he measured, he says, of the house (that is, of the temple) the length, a hundred cubits; and that which was the separate building, and its walls, of a hundred cubits in length.

The breadth before the face of the house, and of that which was separate toward the East, of a hundred cubits.

And he measured the length of the building over against the face of that which was separate at the back, the ethecae on either side, of a hundred cubits.

588AIt must indeed be noted that that breadth of a hundred cubits, which he measures before the face of the house, that is of the temple, can be called both breadth and length, but in different respects. For if you consider that whole court in which the temple was situated between the northern and the southern house, from the south to the north it will be of a hundred and forty cubits in length, and of a hundred cubits in breadth toward the East, just as he here says. But if you attend not to the whole, but to any one part on whichever side of the temple, it will be, as is read in what follows, of a hundred cubits in length and of fifty cubits in breadth.

But under the following sentence he determines the site of the buildings. For the building described above was separate at the back of the temple, and over against its own face 588Bit had, on this side and on that, on either side of the temple, a certain building.

And he measured, he says, the length of the building over against the face of that which was separate at the back, the ethecae on either side, of a hundred cubits.

You see surely that on either side there was a building, and that each had ethecae, and that by that very thing whereby he measured the ethecae he also took the measures of the houses.

We understand these ethecae to be certain fore-lofts Ante solaria, which, as Jerome testifies in these words, at Rome they call lofts projecting from the walls of the upper chambers, which some of the Greeks call exedrae.

What follows seems to be a recapitulation with an addition:

588CAnd the inner temple, and the porches of the court, the thresholds and the oblique windows, the ethecae round about on three sides over against the threshold of each, and paved with wood round about in a circuit.

But it is plain why he said the temple was inner, because it held the middle place among the aforesaid buildings. He names them porches of the court, because they lay next to the court.

But above he had marked out ethecae on either side, while here he says they are on three sides; whence it is agreed that the separate building also had ethecae. From which fact we prove that there is the same likeness in the three, as much in quality as in quantity. But the ethecae, as is said, were over against the threshold of each one, because they projected outwardly from the upper chambers themselves. 588DFor lest they should be thought higher or lower than the upper chambers themselves, the positions of the fore-lofts are expressed by the thresholds of the lofts.

By "the paving of woods round about" he means the planking with which the lower parts of the ethecae were floored, which, as has been said, seemed to gird the three sides of the temple: But the earth, he says, was up to the windows.

Whence it can be proved how great the slope of the mountain was, since the walls of the buildings which were on the temple's side had the earth up to the windows.

But in that he says cherubim and palm trees up to the inner house — this having been the holy of holies — he seems to wish to determine that the inner sanctuary did not have carvings of this kind. They were therefore, as is said, 589Aup to the inner house, that is, in the outer temple, and outside, that is, in the porch, which by the same consideration seems able to be called "outside" by which the inner house is called "within." They were, I say, in the temple and in the porch, throughout every wall round about, not only within but also outside, wrought to measure, both the cherubim and the palm trees.

There follows:

And a palm tree between cherub and cherub, and the cherub had two faces.

The face of a man beside the palm tree on this side, and the face of a lion beside the palm tree on the other side, set forth throughout all the house round about.

From the ground up to the upper parts of the gate cherubim and 589Bpalm trees were carved on the wall of the temple.

A four-square threshold, and the face of the sanctuary, sight over against sight of the wooden altar.

All these things, as I judge, are plain of themselves, and do not need our exposition.

But the last sentence expresses the site of the inner altar, in that it was placed in the middle of the temple over against the gate of the sanctuary. This was the altar of incense. For that one which he touched on briefly above, and of which he treats more fully in what follows, was the altar of holocaust.

But by the words that follow he seems to express not only the manner of quantity, but also the fashioning 589Cof the work

And so he says:

Three cubits its height, and its length two cubits.

These things are referred to quantity; the following to quality:

And its corners, and its length, and its walls, of wood.

Perhaps he was silent concerning the breadth for this reason, that the length and the breadth were the same, and that in whichever direction it could be called length, just as in whichever it could be called breadth. But it seems to have four timbers set upright at the four corners according to the height, to which four others were coupled both above and below, 589Dlaid out according to the length and the breadth, to which boards were fastened and joined together into a single frame. He calls them corners, therefore, for this reason that qnod those timbers which were raised at the corners held the corners. But he names as "length" those timbers which measured the length of the altar.

And by "walls" he means the joining of the boards by which their gaping was covered.

There follows:

And he spoke to me this is the table before the Lord.

And after these things is added the description of the doors both of the temple and of the sanctuary:

590AAnd there were two doors in the temple and in the sanctuary.

And in the two doors on either side there were two little doors apiece, which folded one into the other. For there were two doors on either side of the doors.

I think it is manifest from this place that the same noun is set down in a different acceptation. Otherwise how will it stand, that he says: "There were two doors on either side of the doors"? That which he first calls "doors," then, is to be referred to the fabric which was of wood. That which he afterward calls "of the doors" is to be referred to the entrance which lay open in the wall. But the wall was of six cubits' thickness, and that very thickness of it held two doors, as much in the temple as in the sanctuary. For one was in the wall on the inner side and the other on the 590Bouter. And of these two doors one hung on the right and the other on the left, after which manner it was in all things also in the temple of Solomon.

This is what is said there: « And two doors of fir wood on either side, and each door was divided in twain (III Reg. VI, 34), » this is what he had said above: "Sides of five cubits." What there he calls "sides," here he calls "little doors." These sides were coupled with iron fastenings, and when the gates were opened they were folded one upon the other. Just as it is said here, so it was said there, that by holding one another they were opened. And for this reason one door was divided into two and folded together, lest it should exceed the breadth of the wall while it was being opened.

590CAnd there were carved on the doors themselves of the temple cherubim, and the sculpture of palm trees, just as they were set forth on the walls also.

All these things are sufficiently plain of themselves.

But rightly is it subjoined:

Wherefore there were thicker timbers on the front of the porch outside, over which were the oblique windows.

For the matter required thicker timbers to be there, where for the sake of adornment there had to be worthier and excellent carvings. And it is given to be understood from these words that the walls were covered with timbers, just as in the temple of Solomon.

There follows:

And the likeness of palm trees on this side and on that, on the little shoulders 590Dof the porch, according to the sides of the house and the breadth of the walls.

These things are plain:

And he led me out into the outer court by the way leading to the north, and brought me into the chamber which was over against the separate building.

It is agreed that he treated of the front of the porch of the temple in the last place, from which place he now seems to pass through the northern gate of the inner court into the chamber described. Rightly indeed. For this chamber certainly had its face toward the aforesaid walk of ten cubits in breadth, which began from that place where sta portico was joined to the portico of the northern house, 591Aand where, as is gathered from this place, namely on the side of the triple portico, an approach lay open to that chamber:

And he brought me, he says, into the chamber which was over against the separate building, and over against the edifice verging to the north.

In the face of the length, a hundred cubits of the door of the north, and of the breadth, fifty cubits.

Over against the twenty cubits of the inner court, and over against the pavement laid with stone of the outer court, where there was a portico joined to the triple portico.

And before the chambers a walk of ten cubits in breadth, looking toward the inner parts, of a way of one cubit; and their doors toward the north.

591BWe have expounded the description of all these things above so diligently that we do not know whether we ought to add anything (unless perhaps for the exposition of the letter) to what has been said. By "the door of the north" he means that door of the temple of which he had spoken above, and "the door of the side toward prayer. One door toward the north." Not yet indeed, because what he first set down as if in the singular, he afterward marked out in the plural. "And he brought me," he says, "into the chamber," and in what follows, "and before the chambers a walk of ten cubits in breadth, looking toward the inner parts, of a way of one cubit." But that which follows, "And their doors toward the north" — if earum is read there, it is to be referred to the porticoes; but if eorum, it is to be referred to the chambers. For in either case the sentence will be able to be true.

591CThere follows:

Where the chambers were lower in the upper parts.

Because they supported the porticoes which projected from them, from the lower and from the middle parts of the building. The description of the walk having been premised, it is well added where the chambers were; for they were beside the walk, and clung to it on the north.

This too must be noted, that he says "porticoes" in the plural, which before he called "portico." Indeed, as is easily weighed by these words, both the house and its portico were divided into a threefold storey. For if the building had lower parts and middle parts, it certainly had highest parts also. For a middle is not spoken of save with reference to two extremes. And so the house had, as also 591Ddid its portico, chambers at the bottom, one upper chamber in the middle, and another upper chamber at the top. The portico too had chambers in the lower parts over against the chambers of the house, which chambers supported the porticoes. For there were porticoes in the upper parts of these, one over against the first upper chamber and another over against the second upper chamber, which were supported by the chambers. The lower portico projected from the inner parts of the building, because it was situated above the chambers, which answered to the lower parts of the buildings. The upper portico projected from the middle parts of the building, because it was situated above the first portico, which answered to the middle parts of the building. Let us now consider of what sort that is, that the chambers are said to be lower in the upper parts. Indeed, if we estimate the steepness of the mountain about this place according to 592Athe former manner, we shall find the earth on one side of the building, on the temple's side, higher by sixty cubits than on the other side. But who would not know that, reason requiring it, the pavements of the buildings must be laid upon some level surface? Lest therefore we should think that the earth had been hollowed out on one side by sixty cubits, Scripture says that the chambers are lower in the upper parts, that we may understand one chamber divided into several, and that each has its own pavements — level indeed in themselves, but some more elevated than others, and, according to the ascent of the mountain, raised one above another after the manner of steps. Where therefore the pavements were more elevated, there the chambers were lower, that is, having less of height; and this same thing we understand of those chambers which were beneath the porticoes. 592BThe same of that division of the six porticoes which we distinguished into seven portions according to the breadth and the ascent of the mountain. The same we judge concerning the chambers which were about the pavements. And for this reason these divisions were indeed long, but little broad. For the same chambers are read to have been of fifty cubits in length, which nevertheless seem to have not more than nine cubits in breadth. Yet these divisions of the chambers seem not so much fenced off by walls as distinguished by columns — yet so that a wall of no small height (as is wont to be done in cloisters) supported these columns, a wall which would receive the elevations of the pavements and set apart the gradations of the elevations. And so in this the chambers of the outer court differed from 592Cthese chambers, that those had columns, but in these there were none.

For they were three-storeyed, he says, and had not columns, such as were the columns of the courts.

But well is it added concerning these porticoes, "therefore they projected." Because they were supported by the chambers, therefore they projected; and it is indeed subjoined how, or how much, they projected from the earth.

They projected, he says, from the lower and from the middle parts of the building, from the earth by fifty cubits.

If this over-projection of the porticoes is marked off from the earth upward by fifty and fifty cubits, the whole height of the house seems to have a hundred and fifty 592Dcubits. But the lower portico did not everywhere project fifty cubits from the earth, but on that side where the earth was lower. But because according to the aforesaid reckoning the earth on one side of the house, on the temple's side, was higher by sixty cubits, and the first upper chamber of the house, according to the description of the portico, projected on the other side by only fifty cubits, the earth seems to project outwardly by ten cubits on the temple's side above the pavement of the first upper chamber. What therefore we read above, that the earth was up to the windows, we ought to understand of the windows of the first upper chamber, so that one went down to its doors by certain steps, as we see in cellars. But we weigh the ethecae to be over against the higher upper chamber. 593

593BBut since the chambers which were beneath the porticoes clung, together with those very porticoes, to the house, they had on the inner side one wall common with the house itself; which wall indeed was longer than the chambers of the porticoes by as much as the breadth of the house exceeded their length. By the outer wall therefore, which clung to the outer court, the length of the chambers is expressed, when it is added: And the outer enclosure along the chambers which were in the way of the outer court, before the chambers, its length fifty cubits.

It must indeed be noted that he does not say that the portico itself 594Bhas fifty cubits in length, but that its outer enclosure, that is, that wall which it had toward the outer parts, was of fifty cubits in length — lest this measure be thought to have been taken within the walls, as in the porticoes above. It is plain therefore, as we have already said above, that it had one wall common with the house, so that it ought to be counted into the measure of both, so that there may be settled concerning the chambers of this portico that which is read of those chambers which are described after the likeness of these. According to their length, he says, so also their breadth. But that seems to be a general 595Asentence which is brought in beneath, when it is said: "That the length of the chambers of the outer court was fifty cubits." For since it is said, not "of them," but "of the chambers," a reference to the aforesaid does not seem to have been made, but a general sentence uttered, so that one length may be understood of all the chambers of the outer court.

CAPUT XVIII. De caeteris quae restant usque ad finem descriptionis.

Post descriptionem gazophylacii quod erat contra viginti cubitos atrii interioris, et contra pavimentum atrii exterioris, et post descriptionem porticus tam pavimento quam triplici porticui adhaerentis, vide quam miranda ratione repetit quod longe superius dixit. Et longitudo, inquit, ante faciem templi 595Bcentum cubitorum. Nonne hoc idem expressit, quando atrii interioris tam longitudinem quam latitudinem definivit? Sed intellexit propheta secundum praedictorum descriptionem consequens esse, longitudinem ante templum centum cubitos, secundum planum habere non posse. Sed idem iterum repetendo per inculcationem admonuit quod absque dubio aliquo modo habuit quod secundum plani considerationem omnino habere non potuit. Sequitur:

Et erat subter gazophylacia haec introitus ab Oriente ingredientium in ea de atrio exteriori in latitudine periboli atrii quod erat contra viam orientalem in facie aedificii separati. Quae sint gazophylacia ante gazophylacia ista, et quis sit introitus subter ista et super illa, id est inter utraque gazophylacia 595Csuperius jam diximus, et eadem replicare vitamus; atrium autem de quo hic commemorat, non solum in facie aedificii separati, sed etiam in facie aquilonaris domus, et ex majori parte erat. Sed quia aedificium separatum ab eo longius distabat, de eo maxime dicitur de quo minus videbatur. Descriptis gazophylaciis, quae sub porticibus aquilonaris domus in via Aquilonis erant, quorum similitudinem illa quae sub porticibus separati aedificii fuerant per omnia tenebant, compendiose satis eorum descriptionem ex similitudinis adjunctione subintulit, cum dicit: Et erant ante aedificium gazophylacia, et via ante faciem eorum juxta similitudinem gazophylaciorum quae erant in via Aquilonis. Si ista 595Dgazophylacia per omnia eorum similitudinem habebant quae in via Aquilonis ante aquilonarem domum erant, ergo porticus supportabant quae de illis eminebant de inferioribus, et de mediis aedificii. Similiter in caeteris unam in utrisque similitudinem intelligimus, sicut superius docuimus, et eadem replicare non debemus. Unum quod superius praeteriisse videbatur, latitudinem videlicet eorum, hoc loco determinare videtur, et tam istorum quam illorum mensuram uno sermone complectitur, cum dicitur: Secundum longitudinem eorum, sic et latitudo eorum.

Completa descriptione omnium aedificiorum quae ad Aquilonem erant, eorum quae ad Austrum consistebant brevi sententia comprehendit, cum dicit: 596AEt omnis introitus eorum, et similitudines, et ostia eorum secundum ostia gazophylaciorum, quae erant in via respiciente ad Notum. Ostium in capite viae, quae via erat ante vestibulum separatum per viam orientalem ingredientibus. Quidquid de gazophylacio quod erat contra viginti cubitos atrii interioris diximus, quidquid de aede vergente ad Aquilonem, vel ejus porticibus, quidquid de ambulatione, vel de introitu superius docuimus, totum de his quae ad austrum erant secundum situs sui modum intelligere debemus, ut sit in istis et illis introitus secundum introitum, et similitudines secundum similitudines, et ostia secundum ostia, ut sit in omnibus per omnia utrobique similitudo una. Vide tamen quam argute unum nominatim expressit, 596Bde quo maxime dubitari potuit. Forte enim aliquis difficile crederet quod aedificium separatum duo vestibula haberet, ne duo habendo aliarum in hoc aedium formam excederet, quod in caeteris omnibus eamdem cum eis similitudinem tenuisset. Ait ergo: Ostium in capite viae, quae via erat ante vestibulum separatum. Si via erat ante vestibulum separatum ex parte una, erit procul dubio, et via, et vestibulum ex parte altera, ut utrobique sit similitudo una. Illud autem palam est, quod caput viae illius ad Orientem respiciebat, quae ab atrio exteriore juxta porticum aquilonaris aedificii incipiebat. Manifesta autem sunt caetera quae sequuntur, usque ad eum locum, ubi dicitur: Cumque complesset mensuras domus interioris, eduxit me per viam portae quae 596Crespiciebat ad viam orientalem, et mensus est eam undique per circuitum. Domum interiorem dicit templum quod inter caetera aedificia locum tenebat medium. Quod dicit, mensus est eam, intelligendum est aut eam interiorem domum, aut eam viam, aut eam portam. Sed quomodo mensuras domus interioris compleverat, si adhuc eam metiri oportebat? Sed quod murum dividentem inter sanctuarium, et vulgi locum hoc loco metiatur, manifestissime determinatur. Portam itaque orientalis viae exteriorem undique in eo mensurabat, quod muri ipsius hinc inde mensuras capiebat. Mensus est autem contra ventum orientalem calamo mensurae quingentos calamos in calamo mensurae 596Dper circuitum. Et mensus est contra ventum aquilonarem quingentos calamos in calamo mensurae per gyrum. Et ad ventum australem mensus est quingentos calamos in calamo mensurae per circuitum, et ad ventum occidentalem mensus est quingentos calamos in calamo mensurae. Per quatuor ventos mensus est illud. Quid sit autem illud, uti jam dictum est, cujus mensuram accepit subjuncta determinatione ostendit, cum dicit: Murum ejus, inquit, undique per circuitum longitudinem quingentorum cubitorum, et latitudinem quingentorum cubitorum dividentem inter sanctuarium, et vulgi locum. Conturbat fortassis aliquem, quod idem murus quingentos calamos et quingentos cubitos dicitur habere, cum calamus superius legatur sex 597Acubitorum fuisse. Sed diversitatem calamorum intelligere nil obstat, cum manifesta prophetae sententia in hoc ipsum nos cogat. Sed si iste Scripturae locus pro diversitate calamorum ad sui testimonium minus sufficere videtur, videamus quid in sequentibus dicitur, ubi post pauca altaris mensura describitur: Istae autem, inquit, mensurae altaris in cubito verissimo, qui habebat cubitum et palmum. Quis est iste cubitus verissimus, in quo erat cubitus et palmus? Aut hoc de instrumento, id est calamo, dicitur, per quod mensuratur, aut de mensura quae instrumento sumitur. Nam perticam quidem dicimus, et illud quo mensuramus et mensuram quam accipimus. Sic palmus, sic cubitus, sic sextarius dicitur, et mensura qua mensuratur, et mensura 597Bquae mensuratur. Si itaque hoc loco cubitum non instrumentum, sed mensuratum accipimus, quomodo erit cubitus verissimus ubi erat cubitus et palmus, hoc est plus quam cubitus? Si autem per cubitum intelligimus instrumentum, hoc est calamum, in hoc dicimus ipsum verissimum, quia habebat cubitum quo metiretur, et palmum unde inter metiendum teneretur. Potuit autem (quod et ipse credo) in sex cubitorum calamo ex ea parte qua supererat palmus, unus cubitus cum eodem palmo esse segregatus, atque aliquo signo determinatus, in quo sane metiri oportebat, quod infra pleni calami mensuram remanebat. Sicut igitur pars panis dicitur panis, et pars domus dicitur domus, sic pars calami potuit dici calamus. Octoginta autem, et 597Ctres calami cum triente de majoribus complent quingentos de minoribus. Forte ergo ut inimicitias vitaret, imo absque dubio certi causa mysterii, ut plenum mysticumque numerum exprimeret, minores hoc loco calamos posuit et quod mystice docere oportuit, mystico numero expressit.

CHAPTER XVIII. On the other things which remain, unto the end of the description.

After the description of the chamber which was over against the twenty cubits of the inner court and over against the pavement of the outer court, and after the description of the portico clinging both to the pavement and to the triple portico, see with what wonderful reasoning he repeats what he said far above. And the length, he says, before the face of the temple 595Ba hundred cubits. Did he not express this same thing when he defined both the length and the breadth of the inner court? But the prophet understood that it followed, according to the description of the aforesaid, that the length before the temple could not have a hundred cubits according to the level. But by repeating the same again he admonished by inculcation that it without doubt had in some manner what, according to the consideration of the level, it could in no wise have. There follows:

And beneath these chambers there was an entrance from the East for those entering into them from the outer court, in the breadth of the enclosure of the court which was over against the eastern way, in the face of the separate building. What the chambers are that are before these chambers, and what the entrance is beneath these and above those, that is, between both sets of chambers, 595Cwe have already said above, and we avoid repeating the same; but the court which he here records was not only in the face of the separate building, but also in the face of the northern house, and that for the greater part. But because the separate building stood farther off from it, it is said chiefly of that of which it seemed less. The chambers being described which were beneath the porticoes of the northern house in the way of the north — whose likeness those which had been beneath the porticoes of the separate building held in all things — he brought in their description compendiously enough by the adjoining of the likeness, when he says: And before the building there were chambers, and a way before their face, after the likeness of the chambers which were in the way of the north. If these 595Dchambers had in all things the likeness of those which were in the way of the north before the northern house, then they supported the porticoes which projected from them, from the lower and from the middle parts of the building. Likewise in the rest we understand one likeness in both, as we have taught above, and we ought not to repeat the same. One thing which he seemed to have passed over above, namely their breadth, he seems in this place to determine, and he embraces in one utterance the measure both of these and of those, when it is said: According to their length, so also their breadth.

The description being completed of all the buildings which were toward the north, he comprehends in a brief sentence those which stood toward the south, when he says: 596AAnd every entrance of them, and the likenesses, and their doors, according to the doors of the chambers which were in the way looking toward the south wind. A door at the head of the way, which way was before the separate porch, for those entering by the eastern way. Whatever we have said concerning the chamber which was over against the twenty cubits of the inner court, whatever concerning the edifice verging to the north, or its porticoes, whatever we have taught above concerning the walk or the entrance, the whole of it we ought to understand of those things which were toward the south, according to the manner of their site, so that in these and in those there be entrance according to entrance, and likenesses according to likenesses, and doors according to doors, so that in all things throughout all there be on both sides one likeness. See nevertheless how shrewdly he expressed by name one thing 596Bconcerning which there could most be doubt. For perhaps someone would with difficulty believe that the separate building had two porches, lest by having two it should in this exceed the form of the other edifices, since in all the rest it had held the same likeness with them. He says therefore: A door at the head of the way, which way was before the separate porch. If there was a way before the separate porch on the one side, there will without doubt be both a way and a porch on the other side, so that on both sides there be one likeness. But this is plain, that the head of that way looked toward the East, which way began from the outer court beside the portico of the northern building. But manifest are the other things which follow, up to that place where it is said: And when he had completed the measures of the inner house, he led me out by the way of the gate which 596Clooked toward the eastern way, and he measured it on every side round about. By "the inner house" he means the temple, which held the middle place among the other buildings. In that he says "he measured it," it must be understood either as that inner house, or as that way, or as that gate. But how had he completed the measures of the inner house, if it still had to be measured? But that he measures in this place the wall dividing between the sanctuary and the place of the common people is most manifestly determined. And so he was measuring the outer gate of the eastern way on every side in this, that he was taking the measures of that wall on this side and on that. And he measured toward the east wind with the measuring reed five hundred reeds by the measuring reed 596Dround about. And he measured toward the north wind five hundred reeds by the measuring reed round about. And toward the south wind he measured five hundred reeds by the measuring reed round about, and toward the west wind he measured five hundred reeds by the measuring reed. By the four winds he measured it. But what that is, as has already been said, whose measure he took, he shows by a subjoined determination, when he says: Its wall, he says, on every side round about, a length of five hundred cubits and a breadth of five hundred cubits, dividing between the sanctuary and the place of the common people. It perhaps troubles someone that the same wall is said to have five hundred reeds and five hundred cubits, since the reed is read above to have been of six 597Acubits. But nothing hinders our understanding a diversity of reeds, since the manifest sentence of the prophet compels us to this very thing. But if this place of Scripture seems to suffice less for its own testimony as to the diversity of reeds, let us see what is said in what follows, where after a few things the measure of the altar is described: But these, he says, are the measures of the altar in the truest cubit, which had a cubit and a palm. What is this truest cubit, in which there was a cubit and a palm? Either this is said of the instrument, that is, of the reed, by which measuring is done, or of the measure which is taken by the instrument. For we call a "pole" both that with which we measure and the measure which we take. So "palm," so "cubit," so "sextarius" is said both of the measure by which measuring is done and of the measure 597Bwhich is measured. If therefore in this place we take the cubit not as the instrument but as the thing measured, how will it be the truest cubit where there was a cubit and a palm, that is, more than a cubit? But if by "cubit" we understand the instrument, that is, the reed, we call it truest in this, that it had a cubit wherewith it might measure, and a palm whereby it might be held while measuring. And it could be (which I myself also believe) that in the reed of six cubits, on that part where the palm was over, one cubit together with that same palm was set apart and determined by some sign, wherewith indeed that had to be measured which remained below the measure of the full reed. As therefore a part of bread is called bread, and a part of a house is called a house, so a part of a reed could be called a reed. But eighty and 597Cthree reeds with a third of the greater fill up five hundred of the lesser. Perhaps therefore, that he might avoid enmities — nay rather, without doubt for the sake of a certain mystery, that he might express a full and mystical number — he set down in this place the lesser reeds, and what had to be taught mystically he expressed by a mystical number.

CAPUT XIX. De descriptione altaris holocausti.

Restat adhuc de altari dicere, et ejus descriptionem explanare. Istae autem, inquit, mensurae altaris in cubito verissimo qui habebat cubitum et palmum. (Ezech. XLIII). De hoc cubito jam superius docuimus et eadem replicare non debemus. In sinu ejus erat cubitus, et cubitus in latitudine, et definitio 597Dusque ad labium ejus in circuitu palmi unius. Ut totius altaris mensuram breviter perstringam, erat quinque cubitorum altitudinis, et habebat usque ad extremum quarti cubiti duodecim cubitos tam longitudinis quam latitudinis. A quarto vero cubito sursum habebat ejecturam quamdam unius cubiti in omnem partem, ita ut in supremis ejus contra quintum cubitum esset altare quatuordecim cubitorum longitudinis, et quatuordecim latitudinis. Habebat ergo ejectura illa cubitum unum altitudinis, et cubitum unum latitudinis, in omnem partem extra mensuram altaris, ut de eo intelligi possit quod dicit, in sinu ejus erat cubitus, et cubitus in latitudine. Hujus autem supremi cubiti superiorem medietatem corona altaris tenebat, reliqua medietas 598Asub corona erat. Habebat autem corona ipsa unum palmum spissitudinis. Unde est illud: Et definitio usque ad labium ejus in circuitu palmi unius. Hoc loco definitio idem est quod terminatio. Erat itaque suprema extremitas altars, vel coronae, ubi altare terminabatur, vel finiebatur palmi unius scilicet spissitudinis. Erat autem adhuc circa eamdem coronae extremitatem alia quaedam parva ejectura circumquaque dependens, quam et labium vocat. Unde et dicit: Et definitio usque ad labium ejus in circuitu palmi unius. Vel rectius fortassis quoddam foramen, quod erat in medio altaris a summo usque in ima describitur in eo quod dicitur: In sinu ejus erat cubitus et cubitus in latitudine. Caetera autem quae sequuntur secundum priorem 598Bsensum exponantur. Erat etiam sub ipso altari quaedam fossa duorum cubitorum profunditatis, quae cineres qui per illud foramen defluebant, excipiebat. Unde et addit: Haec quoque erat fossa altaris, et de sinu terrae usque ad crepidinem novissimam duo cubiti, et latitudo unius cubiti. Profunditatem et latitudinem describit, et de longitudine tacet; innuit tamen in eo quod duos cubitos ad crepidinem novissimam, id est ad basim altaris, terminari ostendit. Videtur itaque mihi longitudo ejus ab anteriori parte sub basi altaris incipere, et usque post tergum altaris se extendere, ita ut sanguinem qui ad basim fundebatur, excipere posset, et post tergum altaris quasi ad extrahendos influentes cineres pateret. Sequitur:

598CEt a crepidine majori usque ad crepidinem minorem quatuor cubiti, et latitudo unius cubiti. Crepidinem possumus dicere praeruptionem, vel praecisionem alicujus planitiei, vel superficiei. Crepidinem igitur minorem vocat basim altaris quae erat cubitorum duodecim; crepidinem vero majorem vocat basim ejecturae supradictae, quae erat cubitorum quatuordecim. Erant itaque ab una usque ad aliam crepidinem quatuor cubiti, secundum ejus quod dictum est, Ariel altitudinem, et latitudo unius cubiti secundum ejectionis latitudinem. Bene autem cubitum unum seorsum, et cum determinatione posuit. Nam positio quatuor cubitorum in altum surrexit, situs vero unius cubiti se in latus deflexit, et in latum expandit. Sed a crepidine majori 598Dincipiens, cur cubitum unum prius non posuit, qui secundum hunc ordinem prius occurrit, nisi quod majorem numerum Latinorum more prius ponere voluit? Ordinatius autem videretur posuisse, si sic voluisset dicere, et a crepidine minori usque ad crepidinem majorem quatuor cubiti, et latitudo unius cubiti. Hunc ordinem Septuaginta secuti sunt, qui sic ponunt: A propitiatorio minori usque ad propitiatorium majus, eosdem locos per propitiatorium et propitiatorium designantes, quos nos in crepidinibus intelligimus. Et recte quidem satis haec duo loca poterant propitiatoria dici, quia in his duobus locis solebat sanguis ad expiationem et propitiationem poni, vel effundi: Ipse autem Ariel quatuor 599Acubitorum. Ariel dicit ipsum corpus altaris, quod quatuor cubitorum altitudinis, ut dictum est, erat, cui dicta illa superius ejectura supereminebat. Quod ideo forsitan Ariel quod est leo fortis nominavit, quia leonis more populi victimas per illud jam dictum foramen devoravit et absorbuit. Et ab Ariel sursum cornua quatuor, per quatuor videlicet angulos altaris. Et Ariel duodecim cubitorum in longitudine per duodecim cubitos latitudinis, quadrangulum aequis lateribus. Hoc dictum est de corpore altaris, ut superius jam docuimus. Et crepido quatuordecim 600Acubitorum longitudinis per quatuordecim cubitos latitudinis in quatuor angulis ejus. Et de hoc item superius diximus, quod ejusmodi mensura erat circa superiora altaris. Et corona in circuitu ejus dimidii cubiti, scilicet altitudinis. Nam de spissitudine superius dictum est: Et sinus ejus unius cubiti per circuitum. Recole duas quas superius posuimus sententias; secundum primam erit quod modo dicitur superflua repetitio, secundum secundam erit ad altitudinem altaris complendam necessaria adjectio.

CHAPTER XIX. On the description of the altar of holocaust.

It still remains to speak of the altar and to explain its description. But these, he says, are the measures of the altar in the truest cubit, which had a cubit and a palm. (Ezech. XLIII). Concerning this cubit we have already taught above, and we ought not to repeat the same. In its bosom there was a cubit, and a cubit in breadth, and the definition 597Dup to its lip round about, of one palm. That I may briefly run over the measure of the whole altar: it was of five cubits in height, and up to the end of the fourth cubit it had twelve cubits as much of length as of breadth. But from the fourth cubit upward it had a certain projection of one cubit on every side, so that in its topmost parts, over against the fifth cubit, the altar was of fourteen cubits in length and fourteen in breadth. That projection therefore had one cubit of height and one cubit of breadth on every side beyond the measure of the altar, so that of it may be understood what he says, "In its bosom there was a cubit, and a cubit in breadth." But the upper half of this topmost cubit the crown of the altar occupied; the remaining half 598Awas beneath the crown. And the crown itself had one palm of thickness. Whence is that: And the definition up to its lip round about, of one palm. In this place "definition" is the same as "termination." And so the topmost extremity of the altars, or of the crown, where the altar was terminated or finished, was namely of one palm's thickness. But there was still, about that same extremity of the crown, another certain small projection hanging round on every side, which he also calls the lip. Whence also he says: And the definition up to its lip round about, of one palm. Or perhaps more rightly a certain opening, which was in the middle of the altar from the top down to the lowest parts, is described in that which is said: In its bosom there was a cubit and a cubit in breadth. But let the other things which follow be expounded according to the former 598Bsense. There was also beneath the altar itself a certain ditch of two cubits in depth, which received the ashes that flowed down through that opening. Whence also he adds: This too was the ditch of the altar; and from the bosom of the earth up to the last brink, two cubits, and the breadth of one cubit. He describes the depth and the breadth, and is silent about the length; yet he hints at it in this, that he shows the two cubits to be terminated at the last brink, that is, at the base of the altar. And so its length seems to me to begin from the front part beneath the base of the altar, and to extend itself even behind the back of the altar, so that it might be able to receive the blood which was poured out at the base, and behind the back of the altar might lie open, as it were, for drawing out the ashes that flowed in. There follows:

598CAnd from the greater brink up to the lesser brink, four cubits, and the breadth of one cubit. By "brink" we may mean the abrupt breaking-off, or the cutting-off, of some flat or surface. He calls the lesser brink, therefore, the base of the altar, which was of twelve cubits; but the greater brink he calls the base of the aforesaid projection, which was of fourteen cubits. And so from the one brink to the other there were four cubits, according to the height of that which was called Ariel, and the breadth of one cubit according to the breadth of the projection. But well did he set down the one cubit separately and with a determination. For the placing of the four cubits rose on high, but the situation of the one cubit bent itself to the side and spread out in breadth. But beginning from the greater brink, 598Dwhy did he not first set down the one cubit, which according to this order comes first, unless because he wished, after the manner of the Latins, to set the greater number first? But he would seem to have set it down more orderly if he had wished to say thus: "and from the lesser brink up to the greater brink, four cubits, and the breadth of one cubit." This order the Seventy followed, who set it thus: From the lesser propitiatory up to the greater propitiatory, designating by propitiatory and propitiatory the same places which we understand in the brinks. And indeed rightly enough could these two places be called propitiatories, because in these two places the blood was wont to be placed or poured out for expiation and propitiation: But the Ariel itself of four 599Acubits. By "Ariel" he means the body itself of the altar, which was of four cubits in height, as has been said, above which the projection spoken of above rose. Which perhaps he named Ariel, that is, strong lion, for this reason, that after the manner of a lion it devoured and swallowed up the victims of the people through that opening already spoken of. And from the Ariel upward, four horns, namely at the four corners of the altar. And the Ariel of twelve cubits in length by twelve cubits of breadth, four-square with equal sides. This is said of the body of the altar, as we have already taught above. And the brink of fourteen 600Acubits in length by fourteen cubits in breadth at its four corners. And of this likewise we have said above, that a measure of this kind was about the upper parts of the altar. And a crown round about it of half a cubit, namely of height. For concerning the thickness it was said above: And its bosom of one cubit round about. Recall the two senses which we set down above: according to the first, what is now said will be a superfluous repetition; according to the second, it will be a necessary addition for completing the height of the altar.

CAPUT XX. Terrae promissionis descriptio seu divisio, et quae portio debeatur sacerdotibus, et quae levitis.

599B

De hoc sanctificato statim pauca adjungit quod sit sacerdotibus, et longe post in sequentibus. quasi diceret, erit in omni praedicto sanctific. aliud sanctific. in quo erit sanctuar, et templum, ita ut ipsum possit recte dici sanctificatum sanctificati, quemadmodum 599Cdici solet sanctum sanctorum.

Quod dicit a mensura ista, non est referendum ad proximum tantum, sed ad totum quod superioribus versibus est comprehensum. Quod modo mensurari jubetur, est illud quod post paululum dicitur debere esse levitarum.

600BPalam est ex posterioribus quod templum erat in sorte sacerdotum. Quod igitur dicit in ipso, referendum est ad illud mensuratum de quo praemittitur, et a mensura ista. In medio namque ejus mensurae erat templum. Sed subjuncta interpositio, et neutraliter facta relatio facit litteram obscuram.

Duobus istis subsequentibus versibus determinat quorum debeant esse duae illae praedictae mensurae, et in primo quidem de prima. Sanctificatum, inquit, de terra erit sacerdotibus, ac si diceret: Illud quod superius nominavi sanctificatum; illud, inquam, erit sacerdotibus.

600CHaec est illa mensura quam secundo loco posuit, ubi ait: Mensurabis longitudinem viginti quinque millium, et latitudinem decem millium.

Sanctificatum de terra longitudinem viginti quinque millia, et latitudinem decem millia. Sanctificatum erit in omni termino ejus per circuitum. Et erit ex omni parte sanctificatum quingentos per quingentos quadrifariam per circuitum, et quinquaginta cubitis in suburbana 599Cejus per circuitum.

Et a mensura ista mensurabis longitudinem viginti quinque millium, et latitudinem decem millium.

600BEt in ipso erit templum, Sanctumque sanctorum.

Sanctificatum de terra erit sacerdotibus ministris sanctuarii, qui accedunt ad ministerium.

600CEt erit eis locus in domos, et sanctuarium sanctitatis, viginti quinque autem millia longitudinis, et decem millia latitudinis erunt levitis, qui ministrant domui.

CHAPTER XX. A description or division of the land of promise, and what portion is owed to the priests, and what to the Levites.

599B

Concerning this sanctified place he at once adds a few things, that it is for the priests, and far afterward in what follows. as if he should say, there will be in all the aforesaid sanctified place another sanctified place, in which will be the sanctuary, and the temple, so that it may itself rightly be called the sanctified place of the sanctified place, just as 599Cit is wont to be said, the holy of holies.

That he says "from this measure" is not to be referred only to what is nearest, but to the whole which is comprehended in the verses above. What is now bidden to be measured is that which a little after is said ought to be the Levites'.

600BIt is plain from what comes after that the temple was in the lot of the priests. That therefore he says "in it" is to be referred to that measured thing concerning which it is premised, "and from this measure." For in the middle of that measure was the temple. But the interposition subjoined, and the reference made in the neuter, makes the letter obscure.

By these two following verses he determines whose those two aforesaid measures ought to be, and in the first indeed concerning the first. The sanctified place, he says, of the land shall be for the priests; as if he should say: That which above I named the sanctified place — that, I say, shall be for the priests.

600CThis is that measure which he set down in the second place, where he says: Thou shalt measure a length of twenty-five thousand, and a breadth of ten thousand.

The sanctified place of the land, a length of twenty-five thousand, and a breadth of ten thousand. It shall be sanctified in all its border round about. And on every side there shall be a sanctified place, five hundred by five hundred, four-square round about, and fifty cubits for its suburbs 599Cround about.

And from this measure thou shalt measure a length of twenty-five thousand, and a breadth of ten thousand.

600BAnd in it shall be the temple, and the Holy of Holies.

The sanctified place of the land shall be for the priests, the ministers of the sanctuary, who draw near to the ministry.

600CAnd there shall be for them a place for houses, and a sanctuary of holiness; but twenty-five thousand of length and ten thousand of breadth shall be for the Levites, who minister to the house.

Cruces for this work — where the plate is defective or the reading uncertain →

The Latin is Migne's printing of In visionem Ezechielis (PL 196, coll. 527A–600C), by Richard of Saint Victor, from the Corpus Corporum transcription. Parenthetical references in small type stand on Migne's page — editorial identifications inherited from the edition he reprinted or supplied by his shop, not necessarily the author's own; the author's own citations run in the prose itself. Every gilt column mark is an address: migne.app/pl/196/527a resolves to the first.
